Generic Compacta from Relations between Finite Graphs: Theory building and examples Thanks: Adam Bartoš was supported by the GAČR project EXPRO 20-31529X and RVO: 67985840. Thanks: Tristan Bice was supported by the GAČR project 22-07833K and RVO: 67985840. Thanks: Alessandro Vignati was partially supported by an Emergence en recherche grant from Université Paris Cité
Adam Bartoš, Tristan Bice, Alessandro Vignati

Abstract
. In recent work, the authors developed a simple method of constructing topological spaces from certain partially ordered sets – those coming from sequences of relations between finite sets. This method associates a given poset with its spectrum, which is a compact 𝖳 1 \mathsf{T}_{1} topological space. In this paper, we focus on the case where such finite sets have a graph structure and the relations belong to a given graph category. We relate topological properties of the spectrum to combinatorial properties of the graph categories involved. We then utilise this to exhibit elementary combinatorial constructions of well-known continua as Fraïssé limits of finite graphs in categories with relational morphisms.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
05C62, 54D80, 54F15

1.
Introduction

Almost since the birth of topology in the early twentieth century, people have been interested in constructing compact topological spaces from finite combinatorial structures. For example, Alexandroff
[
Ale29
]
showed how to construct Hausdorff compacta from sequences of finite abstract simplicial complexes, which inspired later constructions of Flachsmeyer
[
Fla61
]
and Kopperman et al.
[
KTW03
]
from finite posets. More recently, we have constructions from functions between finite graphs due to Irwin and Solecki
[
IS06
]
and Dębski and Tymchatyn
[
DT18
]
. The Irwin–Solecki construction is aimed at spaces like the pseudoarc which arise naturally as projective Fraïssé limits and has since been applied to other well-known continua like the Lelek fan
[
BK15
]
, the Menger curve
[
PS22
]
, fences
[
BC21
]
, Ważewski dendrites
[
CKRY
,
CK24
]
, and a previously unknown indecomposable one-dimensional hereditarily unicoherent continuum
[
CKR25
]
.
One common feature of these constructions is that the space is not obtained directly from the finite structures, but rather as a quotient of an intermediate ‘pre-space’. This ends up putting some distance between the combinatorics and topology. Also, unlike algebraic quotients, topological quotients are generally not so well-behaved and are usually avoided if at all possible.

In a somewhat different direction, constructions of spaces like the pseudoarc and pseudosolenoids have recently been obtained in
[
BK
]
as certain approximate Fraïssé limits. This has the advantage of not going through any kind of pre-space, while the general setting of metric enriched categories can also easily handle richer structures like the Gurariĭ space and other objects naturally appearing in functional analysis (see
[
Kub
]
). However, here the structures are already more analytic and less combinatorial from the outset. There is also inevitably some technical machinery that has to be developed in the approximate setting.

In the present paper we combine the best parts of both approaches, while avoiding the need to deal with quotient spaces or metric-enriched categories. Specifically, we construct compacta directly (i.e. without a pre-space) from finite graphs but this time with more general morphisms between them – relations instead of functions. These relations could be viewed as approximating functions, but as the structures involved are finite, these approximations can be handled in a purely combinatorial way.
In this approach, the relationship between the graphs and the resulting spaces is also more transparent. Specifically, the graphs get represented as a coinitial (w.r.t. refinement) family of open covers of the space – each vertex corresponds to an open set of the cover while the edge relation indicates precisely when the sets overlap. The relations between the graphs correspond exactly to the inclusion relation between the associated open covers.

The starting point of this paper is the construction of
[
BBV25
]
, which is briefly outlined for convenience in
§​​ 2
, and specifically in
§​​ 2.2
. In short, in
[
BBV25
]
we associated to a poset
\mathbb{P}
a topological space
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
, the
spectrum
of
\mathbb{P}
, by topologising the space of certain subsets of
\mathbb{P}
called
selectors
. The spectrum is, as long as the poset is suitable (an
\omega
-poset), a compact
\mathsf{T}_{1}
second-countable space. We then described how combinatorial properties of the poset
\mathbb{P}
relate to topological properties of the space
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
. Moreover, we studied closed subspaces of the spectrum and, through the development of
refiners
(relations between posets), we coded maps between topological spaces, ultimately obtaining a duality between appropriate categories.

In this article, we focus on the case where the poset arises from a sequence of graphs together with relational morphisms between them. A relation
\sqsupset
between two graphs
G
and
H
generalises a function from
G
to
H
, viewed as a subset of
H\times G
. Fix a sequence of finite graphs
G_{n}
and relations
{\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}}\subseteq G_{n}\times G_{n+1}
. For
m\leq n
, set
{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}={\sqsupset_{m+1}^{m}\circ\cdots\circ\sqsupset_{n}^{n-1}}
, where
\sqsupset_{n}^{n}
is the identity. These graphs and relations, under some extremely mild and reasonable well-behavedness conditions, give rise to a graded
\omega
-poset
\mathbb{P}=\bigsqcup_{n}G_{n}
ordered by
\bigcup_{m\leq n}\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
. Then the machinery of
[
BBV25
]
applies and we obtain a topological space
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
, the spectrum of
\mathbb{P}
, constructed concretely from the given sequence of graphs and relations.

Two questions interest us:

(1)

If the graphs and relations to belong to a given category of graphs
\mathbf{C}
, what can we say about the class of spaces obtained this way?

(2)

Say
\mathbf{C}
is rich enough to have a Fraïssé sequence (i.e.
\mathbf{C}
is directed and has amalgamation). Can we then refer to the
generic
compact space one gets, and if so, can we concretely derive such a space and its properties from the structure of
\mathbf{C}
?

Most of our results are designed to answer the above questions. Let us streamline them, while summarising the structure of the paper.

In
§​​ 2
, we recall the needed background from
[
BBV25
]
on how to construct the spectrum of a given graded
\omega
-poset.

In
§​​ 3
, we introduce notable categories and ideals of morphisms between graphs, and see how these interact with each other. With a minimal requirement represented by edge-preservation (i.e. a relation from a graph
G
to a graph
H
should send adjacent vertices to adjacent vertices), there are several additional conditions one can impose to eventually obtain interesting information regarding the associated poset (or rather its spectrum). Particular attention is paid to star-refining morphisms (which ultimately ensure Hausdorffness of the spectrum), and monotone morphisms, especially in the setting of trees.

The main part of the theory is developed in
§​​ 4
. We start by providing several answers to Question
(1)
. After introducing the key notion of the
induced poset
defined from a sequence of graphs (
Definition 4.1
), we start analysing subsequences and restrictions. This is done in
§​​ 4.2
, after comparing our approach with previous work of
[
IS06
]
and
[
DT18
]
in
§​​ 4.1
.

In
§​​ 4.3
we start heavily using the graph structure. We compare order-theoretic properties of the induced poset, graph-theoretic properties of the relations involved, and topological properties of the spectrum (see, for example, Propositions
4.17
–
4.21
). In
§​​ 4.4
, we analyse closed connected subsets of the spectrum in terms of the trace these leave on the associated poset, as well as unicoherence of the spectrum (for spectra of posets induced from sequences of trees).

In
§​​ 4.5
we switch gears and move towards answering Question
(2)
.
We define Fraïssé categories and their lax versions. The tension between on-the-nose amalgamation and lax-amalgamation, not present in the function setting, has to be assessed here. We then go into proving key properties of (lax-)Fraïssé sequences. Notably, we show in
Proposition 4.32
that under natural assumptions two lax-Fraïssé sequences give posets with homeomorphic spectra; this allows us to talk about a topological space as
the
Fraïssé limit of a certain category of graphs.
The main summarizing result, which could be dubbed “Fraïssé theorem for categories of graphs and relations” is
Theorem 4.34
.

In
§​​ 4.6
we study the clique functor, allowing us to often reduce the technical effort required to show that a given category has amalgamation. A clique in a graph
G
is a complete subgraph of
G
. From a graph
G
, it is possible to construct the graph of cliques
\mathsf{X}G
, which partially remembers the graph structure of
G
. The main result of
§​​ 4.6
,
Corollary 4.39
, asserts that if a category of graphs
\mathbf{C}
is sufficiently closed by the
\mathsf{X}
operation, then it is enough to check amalgamation in
\mathbf{C}
for morphisms which are induced by functions.

In
§​​ 5
we study specific categories and their Fraïssé sequences, giving specific answers to Question
(2)
.
The section starts with a summarizing checklist for application of the theory to particular examples.
Each subsection then contains a main theorem characterizing the Fraïssé limit and (lax-)Fraïssé sequences in the corresponding category.
We first consider the category
\mathbf{D}
discrete graphs (giving, unsurprisingly, the Cantor space), and then move to richer categories, actually utilizing relational morphisms, such the category
\mathbf{A}
of paths with monotone morphisms. We show that this category has amalgamation, and identify the interval
[0,1]
as the spectrum of the poset induced by any Fraïssé sequence in it. In
§​​ 5.3
, we consider two categories of fans,
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{L}
, obtaining the Cantor and the Lelek fan as generic spaces respectively. A notable result is that
\mathbf{L}
recovers all smooth fans (i.e. subfans of the Cantor fans) as spectra of sequences (
Corollary 5.33
). Lastly,
§​​ 6
is dedicated to concluding remarks.

2.
Preliminaries: Relations, graphs, and the spectrum

This introductory section summarises results contained in
[
BBV25
]
.

2.1.
Relation and graphs

Graphs, and relations between them (including the edge relation encoded in the definition of a graph) are our main focus.

2.1.1.
Relations

We view any
{\sqsupset}\subseteq B\times A
as a relation ‘from
A
to
B
’.
We call
\sqsupset

•

a function
if every
a\in A
is related to exactly one
b\in B
.

•

surjective
if every
b\in B
is related to at least one
a\in A
.

•

injective
if, for every
a\in A
, we have some
b\in B
which is only related to
a
.

•

bijective
if
\sqsupset
is both surjective and injective.

These notions of sur/in/bijectivity for relations generalise the usual notions for functions. The prefix ‘co’ will be used to refer to the opposite/inverse relation
\sqsupset^{-1}\ =\ \sqsubset\ \subseteq A\times B
(where
a\sqsubset b
means
b\sqsupset a
), e.g. we say
\sqsubset
is co-injective to mean that
\sqsupset
is injective. For example, one can note that every co-injective relation is automatically surjective, and the converse also holds for functions.

If
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq C\times B
and
{\sqsupset}\subseteq B\times A
are relations, the relation
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ{\sqsupset}\subseteq C\times A
is the composition of
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
and
\sqsupset
, that is, for all
a\in A
and
c\in C
,

c\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset a\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\exists b\in B\,(c\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}b\text{ and }b\sqsupset a).

If
{\sqsupset}\subseteq B\times A
is a relation and
T\subseteq B
, the
preimage
of
T
is given by

(Preimage)

\displaystyle T^{\sqsupset}=\{a\in A:\exists t\in T\ (t\sqsupset a)\}.

Likewise, the
image
of any
S\subseteq A
is the preimage of the opposite relation
\sqsubset
, i.e.

(Image)

\displaystyle S^{\sqsubset}=\{b\in B:\exists s\in S\ (b\sqsupset s)\}.

We say
S\subseteq A

refines

T\subseteq B
if it is contained in its preimage. The resulting refinement relation will also be denoted by
\sqsubset
, i.e. for any
S\subseteq A
and
T\subseteq B
,

S\sqsubset T\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad S\subseteq T^{\sqsupset}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\forall s\in S\ \exists t\in T\ (s\sqsubset t).

We will be mainly interested in the refinement relation between open covers of a topological space.

2.1.2.
Graphs

A
graph
is a set
G
(the set of
vertices
) on which we are given a symmetric reflexive relation
\sqcap
(the
edge relation
). For example, if
G
is any family of non-empty sets then
\sqcap
could be the overlap relation, i.e. where
A\mathrel{\sqcap}B
if and only if
A\cap B\neq\emptyset
. These overlap graphs are our primary motivating examples and this is the main reason we require the edge relation on graphs to be reflexive. In this paper, a graph will also always be finite and non-empty unless stated otherwise.

Following standard practice, we often refer to ‘the graph
G
’, leaving the edge relation implicit. By an
edge
, we mean a single element of
\sqcap
, i.e. a pair
(g,h)
satisfying
g\sqcap h
– if
g=h
then we call
(g,h)
a
loop
, while if
g\neq h
then we say that
g
and
h
are
adjacent
. The resulting adjacency relation will be denoted by
\sim
, i.e.

g\sim h\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad g\sqcap h\quad\text{and}\quad g\neq h.

For any
g\in G
, we call
g^{\sim}=g^{\sqcap}\setminus\{g\}
the
neighbourhood
of
g
in
G
. We call
g\in G

isolated
if it has no neighbours, and a graph is
discrete
if all its vertices are isolated.

An
induced subgraph
of a graph
G
is a subset
H\subseteq G
with the same edge relation just restricted to
H
. When applying graph theoretic terms to subsets of vertices, it is always the induced subgraph structure we are referring to unless otherwise specified. For example, we call
D\subseteq G
discrete if the induced subgraph
D
is discrete.

We will work with relations between graphs which behave well with respect to the edge relation.

Definition 2.1
.

We call a relation
{\sqsupset}\subseteq H\times G
between graphs
G
and
H

•

edge-preserving
if
h\sqsupset g\sqcap g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}
implies
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
.

•

edge-surjective
if
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
implies
h\sqsupset g\sqcap g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}
, for some
g,g^{\prime}\in G
.

•

edge-witnessing
if
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
implies
h\sqsupset g\sqsubset h^{\prime}
, for some
g\in G
.

As the edge relation is always reflexive, we immediately see that

\sqsupset\,\text{is edge-witnessing}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \sqsupset\,\text{is edge-surjective}\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ \sqsupset\,\text{is surjective}.

2.2.
The spectrum of a poset

If
X
is a topological space and
B\subseteq\mathsf{P}(X)
is a (sub)basis of open sets, the inclusion relation between elements of
B
turns it into a poset. Topological properties of
X
, and combinatorial properties of the basis, are reflected by order theoretic properties of the associated poset.
The main focus of
[
BBV25
]
is to reverse this process, constructing topological spaces from posets.

For the remaining part of this section, we fix a poset
(\mathbb{P},\leq)
. When mentioning
\mathbb{P}
, the order
\leq
will be implicit. We view
\geq\ \subseteq\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{P}
as a relation; we can therefore use the notation introduced in
§​​ 2.1
, so for example, if
B\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, the set
B^{\leq}
equals
\{p\in\mathbb{P}\mid\exists b\in B\ (b\leq p)\}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{P},\leq)
be a poset. Let
B\subseteq\mathbb{P}
. Then

•

B
is a
band
if it is finite and
\mathbb{P}=B^{\leq}\cup B^{\geq}
;

•

B
is a
cap
if it is refined by a band, i.e. if we have a band
B^{\prime}\leq B
.

The collections of bands and caps of
\mathbb{P}
will be denoted by
\mathsf{B}\mathbb{P}
and
\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}
respectively.

Fix a
\mathsf{T}_{1}
topological space
X
, and say that
\mathbb{P}
is a basis of non empty open subsets of
X
ordered by inclusion
\subseteq
. Then every cap of
\mathbb{P}
is a cover of
X
, by
[
BBV25
, Proposition 1.7]
. When the converse holds, i.e. when every cover of
X
by elements of
\mathbb{P}
is a cap,
\mathbb{P}
is said to be a
cap-basis
. Cap-bases exist for all compact second countable
\mathsf{T}_{1}
topological spaces, and in this case they can even be found by ‘massaging’ a given basis – see
[
BBV25
, Proposition 1.11]
.

The minimal elements of
\mathbb{P}
will be called
atoms
, i.e.
p\in\mathbb{P}
is an atom if and only if
p^{>}=\emptyset
. The poset
\mathbb{P}
is said to be:

•

predetermined
if for every
p\in\mathbb{P}
which is not an atom there is
q<p
such that
q^{<}=p^{\leq}
;

•

Noetherian
if it has no infinite increasing sequences, or, alternatively, if the partial order
\geq
is well-founded.

Noetherian posets come with a
rank
ordinal valued function, defined inductively, which associates to
p\in\mathbb{P}
the height of the set
p^{<}
.
More formally, the rank of
p\in\mathbb{P}
is given by
\mathsf{r}(p)=\sup\{\mathsf{r}(q)+1:q>p\}
, where maximal elements are given rank
0
.

Definition 2.3
.

A Noetherian poset
\mathbb{P}
is an
\omega
-poset
if every principal filter
p^{\leq}
is finite and there are only finitely many principal filters of a given size.
(Equivalently, a Noetherian poset is an
\omega
-poset if every element has finite rank and every set
\{p\in\mathbb{P}:\mathsf{r}(p)\leq n\}
is finite.)

An
\omega
-poset is
graded
if the rank function maps intervals to intervals, that is, for every
q<p
and
n
such that
\mathsf{r}(p)<n<\mathsf{r}(q)
there is
p^{\prime}
with
q<p^{\prime}<p
and
\mathsf{r}(p^{\prime})=n
.

If
\mathbb{P}
is an
\omega
-poset, we write
\mathbb{P}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}\mathbb{P}_{n}
, where
\mathbb{P}_{n}
is the
n
th
level of
\mathbb{P}
, given by those elements of
\mathbb{P}
which are minimal in the set of elements of rank
\leq n
, or, more formally,

\mathbb{P}_{n}=\{p\in\mathbb{P}:\mathsf{r}(p)\leq n\text{ and }\mathsf{r}(q)>n\text{ if }q<p\}.

Note that every level is finite. Furthermore, levels are co-initial among all caps, by
[
BBV25
, Proposition 1.13]
, i.e.

\forall C\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}\ \exists n\ (\mathbb{P}_{n}\leq C).

If the
\omega
-poset
\mathbb{P}
is graded, distinct levels share only atoms, by
[
BBV25
, Proposition 1.31]
. In fact, a graded poset
\mathbb{P}
is atomless if and only if all its levels are disjoint and hence
\mathbb{P}_{n}=\{p\in\mathbb{P}:\mathsf{r}(p)=n\}
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. This will hold for all the posets
\mathbb{P}
we apply the theory to in this article.

The following directly follows from
[
BBV25
, Corollary 1.27 and Theorem 1.34]
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Every second-countable compact
\mathsf{T}_{1}
space has a cap-basis which is a graded predetermined
\omega
-poset (when ordered by inclusion).

A key notion is the auxiliary relation of cap-order. For
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, we say that
A
is cap-below
B
if it is easier to make
B
a cap than to make
A
one by simply adding more elements of
\mathbb{P}
. More precisely,

A\precsim B\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\forall F\subseteq\mathbb{P}\ (A\cup F\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}\Rightarrow B\cup F\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}).

If
p\leq q
, then
p\precsim q
. When the converse holds, we say that
\mathbb{P}
is
cap-determined
. Examples of cap-determined posets are given by cap-bases of
\mathsf{T}_{1}
spaces (see
[
BBV25
, Proposition 1.38]
).

Before moving on, we introduce two weaker notions that we will need later on. These can be viewed as properties that any reasonable basis of a compact second countable Hausdorff space should have.

If there is no
p\in\mathbb{P}
such that
p\precsim\emptyset
then we say that
\mathbb{P}
is
prime
. In general, a subset
Q\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is prime if there is no
q\in Q
such that
q\precsim\mathbb{P}\setminus Q
. As we will see below, prime subsets correspond naturally to closed subspaces. Lastly, a poset
\mathbb{P}
is
branching
if, whenever
p<q
, we can find
r<q
which is not comparable with
p
.

The diagram in
Figure 1
summarises some of the implications between the different concepts we just introduced, in the setting of
\omega
-posets (see
[
BBV25
, Figure 1]
):

predetermined
prime
cap-determined
branching
predetermined
branching

Figure 1.
Implications between properties of
\omega
-posets.

2.2.1.
The spectrum

Next we recall how to construct topological spaces from posets.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
(\mathbb{P},\leq)
be a poset. A subset
S\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is a
selector
if it has non-empty intersection with every cap.

By Zorn’s Lemma, every selector contains a minimal selector. We topologise the space of minimal selectors as follows.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
(\mathbb{P},\leq)
be a poset. The
spectrum
of
\mathbb{P}
is the topological space

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}=\{S\subseteq\mathbb{P}:S\text{ is a minimal selector}\},

with the topology generated by the subbasic open sets of the form

p^{\in}=\{S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}:p\in S\}.

The representation
p\mapsto p^{\in}
is not necessarily faithful, and there can even be
p\in\mathbb{P}
such that
p^{\in}
is empty. This does not happen for prime posets and, in fact,
\mathbb{P}
is prime if and only if
p^{\in}\neq\emptyset
, for every
p\in\mathbb{P}
(see
[
BBV25
, Definition 2.27]
). More than that, one can characterise prime subsets of a poset
\mathbb{P}
as exactly those subsets which are unions of minimal selectors. The following was proved in
[
BBV25
, §2.2]
:

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
(\mathbb{P},\leq)
be a poset.

(1)

The spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a compact
\mathsf{T}_{1}
space.

(2)

If
\mathbb{P}
is an
\omega
-poset, then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is second countable, every
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a filter, and
\{p^{\in}:p\in\mathbb{P}\}
is a basis.

(3)

If
\mathbb{P}
is a prime
\omega
-poset, then
\{p^{\in}:p\in\mathbb{P}\}
is a cap-basis for
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.

(4)

\mathbb{P}
is cap-determined if and only if the map
p\mapsto p^{\in}
defines an order isomorphism onto
\{p^{\in}:p\in\mathbb{P}\}
.

Before moving on, we record two lemmas about subposets of
\omega
-posets and their spectra. These did not appear in
[
BBV25
]
, so we also provide proofs.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}
be atomless
\omega
-posets and let
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
if and only if
T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is infinite whenever
T\subseteq S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
is a minimal selector.

Proof.

Recall that minimal selectors are necessarily upwards closed and, for upwards closed subsets in atomless posets, being infinite is equivalent to being a selector. So if
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
then the only possibility for
T
is
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
itself, and
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\cap\mathbb{Q}=S
is infinite. On the other hand,
S
and hence
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
is infinite and thus a selector in
\mathbb{P}
. So
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
contains a minimal selector
T
, necessarily with
T\cap\mathbb{Q}\subseteq S
. If
T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is infinite then it is a selector in
\mathbb{Q}
and hence
T\cap\mathbb{Q}=S
, by the minimality of
S
. It then follows that
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}=T\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}
be atomless
\omega
-posets.
If
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
, for every
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
, then
S\mapsto S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
embeds
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
onto the subspace
\{T\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}:T\cap\mathbb{Q}\text{ is infinite}\}
, with the inverse map
T\mapsto T\cap\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Let
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
and let
T=S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Since
S\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
is upwards closed, we have
T\cap\mathbb{Q}=S
, which is infinite as
\mathbb{Q}
is atomless.
For every
p\in S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
there is
q\in S
with
q\leq p
, so
S\in q^{\in}
and for every
S^{\prime}\in q^{\in}\cap\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
we have
S^{\prime\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\in q^{\in}\subseteq p^{\in}
.
Hence,
S\mapsto S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
is continuous.

Now take
T\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
such that
T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is infinite and let
S=T\cap\mathbb{Q}
.
It follows that
S
is an upwards closed selector in
\mathbb{Q}
as
T
is upwards closed and intersects all levels of
\mathbb{Q}
since the intersection is infinite.
Hence, there is
S^{\prime}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
with
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
.
We have
S^{\prime\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
with
S^{\prime\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\subseteq T
, so
S^{\prime\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}=T
since
T
is minimal.
By the first paragraph it follows that
S^{\prime}=T\cap\mathbb{Q}=S
, and we have
S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}=T
, so we have the desired mutually inverse bijections.
Let
q\in T\cap\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
T\in q^{\in}
and for every
T^{\prime}\in q^{\in}
we have
T^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{Q}\in q^{\in}
, so
T\mapsto T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is continuous.
∎

For two elements
p,q
of a poset
\mathbb{P}
, we write
p\wedge q
if there is
r\in\mathbb{P}
with
r\leq p,q
.
Levels of a predetermined
\omega
-poset faithfully represent a co-initial family of minimal open covers of the spectrum. (Recall, a cover
C
of a set
X
is minimal if
C\setminus\{c\}
does not cover
X
for any
c\in C
.)

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
be a predetermined
\omega
-poset, and for every
n\in\omega
let
C_{n}=\{p^{\in}:p\in\mathbb{P}_{n}\}
.

(1)

Every
C_{n}
is a minimal open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.

(2)

For every
p,q\in C_{n}
we have
p\wedge q
if and only if
p^{\in}\cap q^{\in}\neq\emptyset
.

(3)

Every open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is refined by some
C_{n}
.

Proof.

By
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.8]
, a family
\{p^{\in}:p\in C\}
for some
C\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is a cover if and only if
C
is a cap.
For
(1)
, note predetermined
\omega
-posets are level-injective (see
[
BBV25
, §1.4]
), by
[
BBV25
, Corollary 1.26]
, so each level
\mathbb{P}_{n}
is a minimal cap, by
[
BBV25
, Corollary 1.21]
.
Therefore, every
C_{n}
is a minimal cover as subcovers of
C_{n}
correspond to subcaps of
\mathbb{P}_{n}
.

(2)
follows from
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.30]
as every level-injective
\omega
-poset is prime, by
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.28]
.
For
(3)
, every open cover is refined by a basic cover of the form
\{p^{\in}:p\in C\}
, which is refined by some
C_{n}
as the cap
C
is refined by some level
\mathbb{P}_{n}
in the poset
\mathbb{P}
, by (
2.2
).
∎

The concepts below are designed to characterise topological properties of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
(e.g. being Hausdorff) via properties of
\mathbb{P}
. If
C\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}
and
p\in\mathbb{P}
, the
star
of
p
in
C
is given by

Cp=\{q\in C:q\wedge p\}.

From this we define an auxiliary relation on
\mathbb{P}
. Specifically, if
p,q\in\mathbb{P}
, we define

p\vartriangleleft_{C}q\ \ \iff\ \ Cp\leq q.

Uniformising over all possible caps, we get the
star-below
relation defined by

p\vartriangleleft q\ \ \iff\ \ \exists C\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}\ (p\vartriangleleft_{C}q).

When
\mathbb{P}
is prime, the relation
\vartriangleleft
encodes closed containment of the corresponding open sets, i.e.
p\vartriangleleft q
if and only if
\mathrm{cl}(p^{\in})\subseteq q^{\in}
, by
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.33]
.

Important for us will be the following two facts, isolated for future reference:

•

\vartriangleleft
and
\leq
are compatible, i.e. for any cap
C
and
p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}
,

p\leq p^{\prime}\vartriangleleft_{C}q^{\prime}\leq q\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ p\vartriangleleft_{C}q.

Since if
B\leq C
and
p\vartriangleleft_{C}q
then
pB\leq pC\leq q
and hence
p\vartriangleleft_{B}q
, it follows that

•

if
B
and
C
are caps then

(2.1)

B\leq C\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ {\vartriangleleft_{C}}\subseteq{\vartriangleleft_{B}}.

For more information about stars see
[
BBV25
, §2.5]
.

The last condition we introduce is a regularity condition: An
\omega
-poset
\mathbb{P}
is said to be
regular
if every cap is star-refined by another cap, i.e.

\forall C\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}\ \exists D\in\mathsf{C}\mathbb{P}\ (D\vartriangleleft C).

Regularity allows us to separate points in the spectrum, by
[
BBV25
, Corollary 2.40]
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
be a prime
\omega
-poset. Then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is Hausdorff if and only if
\mathbb{P}
is regular.

3.
The graph category

Officially, we denote the objects and morphisms of a category
\mathbf{C}
by
\mathsf{ob}(\mathbf{C})
and
\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{C})
respectively. However, following standard abuse of notation, we also let
\mathbf{C}
itself stand for both
\mathsf{ob}(\mathbf{C})
and
\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{C})
whenever convenient. For two objects
A,B\in\mathbf{C}
, we denote the hom-set of morphisms from
A
to
B
by

\mathbf{C}^{B}_{A}=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{C}):\mathrm{dom}(\sqsupset)=A\text{ and }\mathrm{cod}(\sqsupset)=B\}.

We work exclusively in the graph category
\mathbf{G}
(and various subcategories thereof). The objects of the graph category are just non-empty finite graphs, i.e.

\mathsf{ob}(\mathbf{G})=\{(G,\sqcap):0<|G|<\infty\text{ and}=_{G}\ \subseteq\sqcap=\sqcap^{-1}\subseteq G\times G\}.

The morphisms of
\mathbf{G}
are edge-preserving relations (
Definition 2.1
) under their usual composition (note that a composition of edge-preserving relations is again edge-preserving), i.e.

\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}=\{{\sqsupset}\subseteq H\times G:\forall g,g^{\prime}\in G,h,h^{\prime}\in H\ (h\sqsupset g\sqcap g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}\Rightarrow h\sqcap h^{\prime})\}.

While the graph category is convenient for general definitions and observations, our real focus will be on certain subcategories
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{G}
where both the graphs and morphisms satisfy various extra conditions. Often the morphisms satisfying these conditions will still be quite ‘large’, in either or both of the following senses.

Definition 3.1
.

We call a collection of morphisms
M
in a category
\mathbf{C}

•

wide
if, for all
H\in\mathbf{C}
, there is
G\in\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{G}^{H}\cap M\neq\emptyset
;

•

an
ideal
if
M
is closed under composition with arbitrary morphisms of
\mathbf{C}
;
1
1

1

Ideals are always two-sided, i.e. closed under composition on the right and on the left.

•

lax-closed
if
M
is closed under taking superrelations that are morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
, i.e. for every
{\sqsupset}\in M
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{C}
such that
{\sqsupset}\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
we have
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in M
.

If
\mathbf{D}
is a subcategory of some larger category
\mathbf{C}
, we immediately see that its morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{C}
precisely when
\mathbf{D}
has the same objects as
\mathbf{C}
. In this case, following standard practice, we call
\mathbf{D}
a
wide subcategory
of
\mathbf{C}
.

In particular,
\mathbf{G}
has wide subcategories of co-injective, co-surjective and edge-surjective morphisms, which we denote by
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{S}
and
\mathbf{E}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbf{I}_{G}^{H}

\displaystyle=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\ \sqsupset\text{is co-injective}\}=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\forall h\in H\ \exists g\in G\ (g^{\sqsubset}=\{h\})\}.

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}_{G}^{H}

\displaystyle=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\ \sqsupset\text{is co-surjective}\}=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\forall g\in G\ (g^{\sqsubset}\neq\emptyset)\}.

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{G}^{H}

\displaystyle=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\ \sqsupset\text{is edge-surjective}\}=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\forall h\in H\ (h^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset\sqcap\sqsubset})\}.

Recall that
h^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset\sqcap\sqsubset}
means

\forall h^{\prime}\,(h\sqcap h^{\prime}\text{ implies }h\sqsupset g\sqcap g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}\text{ for some }g,g^{\prime}\in G).

Let
\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{I}\cap\mathbf{S}
denote the wide subcategory of co-bijective morphisms, i.e.

\mathbf{B}^{H}_{G}=\mathbf{I}^{H}_{G}\cap\mathbf{S}^{H}_{G}=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}:\ \sqsupset\text{is co-bijective}\}.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
and
C\subseteq H
, we define the
strict preimage
by

C_{\sqsupset}=\{g\in G:g^{\sqsubset}=C\}.

As usual, we omit the curly braces for singletons, writing
q_{\sqsupset}
instead of
\{q\}_{\sqsupset}
.

Let us isolate further properties of morphisms.

Definition 3.2
.

A morphism
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
is said to be

•

anti-injective
if
|h^{\sqsupset}|\geq 2
, for all
h\in H
.

•

strictly anti-injective
if
|h_{\sqsupset}|\geq 2
, for all
h\in H
.

•

star-refining
if for all
g\in G
we have
h\in H
with
g^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset}
.

These notions are crucial for us, see for example
Proposition 4.18
below for an indication of why morphisms with the above properties are significant. As a toy example, note that every function from a discrete graph is trivially star-refining, while every function onto a discrete graph is trivially edge-witnessing.

We dedicate the rest of this section to study the above properties, how they interact, and some of their refinements in particular categories.

The following simple observation is easy to see from the definitions.

Lemma 3.3
.

The families of anti-injective, edge-witnessing, and star-refining morphisms in
\mathbf{G}
(and so in every subcategory) are lax-closed. ∎

Note that being star-refining strengthens co-surjectivity and strict anti-injectivity strengthens co-injectivity. Further note that identity morphisms can not be anti-injective. Discrete graphs aside, they are not star-refining or edge-witnessing either. For this reason, such morphisms do not form a subcategory. However, they do form ideals within appropriate subcategories.

Proposition 3.4
.

(1)

The anti-injective co-injective morphisms form an ideal in
\mathbf{I}
.

(2)

The strictly anti-injective morphisms form an ideal in
\mathbf{I}
.

(3)

The star-refining morphisms form an ideal in
\mathbf{S}
.

(4)

The edge-witnessing morphisms form an ideal in
\mathbf{E}
.

Proof.

(1)
: Take any anti-injective
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
. If
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq I\times H
is co-injective and, in particular, surjective then, for every
i\in I
, we have
h\in H
with
i\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}h
. Then
|i^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\sqsupset}|\geq|h^{\sqsupset}|\geq 2
, showing that
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset
is also anti-injective.

Now take co-injective
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq G\times F
. For any
h\in H
, the anti-injectivity of
\sqsupset
yields distinct
g,g^{\prime}\in h^{\sqsupset}
. The injectivity of
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
then yields
f,f^{\prime}\in F
with
f^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}=\{g\}
and
f^{\prime\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}=\{g^{\prime}\}
. In particular,
f
and
f^{\prime}
are distinct elements of
h^{\sqsupset\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
, again showing that
\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is anti-injective.

(2)
: Take any strictly anti-injective
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
. If
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{I}^{I}_{H}
and
i\in I
then we have
h\in i_{\sqsupset}
so
|i_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\sqsupset}|\geq|h_{\sqsupset}|\geq 2
, showing
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset
is also strictly anti-injective.

On the other hand, if
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{I}^{G}_{F}
and
h\in H
then the strict preimages
g_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
, for
g\in h_{\sqsupset}
, are all disjoint and non-empty and hence
|h_{\sqsupset\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}|\geq|h_{\sqsupset}|\geq 2
, showing that
\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is again strictly anti-injective.

(3)
: Take any star-refining
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
. For any
g\in G
, we then have
h\in H
with
g^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset}
. If
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq I\times H
is co-surjective, we then have
i\in I
with
i\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}h
so
h^{\sqsupset}\subseteq i^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\sqsupset}
and hence
g^{\sqcap}\subseteq i^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\sqsupset}
, showing that
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset
is also star-refining.

Now take
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{S}_{F}^{G}
so, for every
f\in F
, we have
g\in G
with
g\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}f
. For every
f^{\prime}\sqcap f
, we then also have
g^{\prime}\in G
with
g^{\prime}\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}f^{\prime}
, which implies
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
, as
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is
\sqcap
-preserving, showing that
f^{\sqcap}\subseteq g^{\sqcap\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
. Taking
h\in H
with
g^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset}
, it follows that
f^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
, so again
{\sqsupset}\circ{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
is star-refining.

(4)
Take any edge-witnessing
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
. If
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{E}_{F}^{G}
then
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is edge-surjective and, in particular, surjective. So if
h,h^{\prime}\in H
satisfy
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
then we have
g\in G
with
h,h^{\prime}\sqsupset g
and then we can further take
f\in F
with
g\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}f
, showing that
{\sqsupset}\circ{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
is also edge-witnessing.

Now take
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{E}_{H}^{I}
. If
i,i^{\prime}\in I
satisfy
i\sqcap i^{\prime}
, this means we have
h\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}i
and
h^{\prime}\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}i^{\prime}
with
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
. As
\sqsupset
is edge-witnessing, we then have
g\in G
with
h,h^{\prime}\sqsupset g
, again showing that
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ{\sqsupset}
is edge-witnessing.
∎

We summarize the main classes of special morphisms and implications between them in
Figure 2
.

surjective
edge-surjective (
\mathbf{E}
)
co-injective (
\mathbf{I}
)
co-surjective (
\mathbf{S}
)
co-bijective (
\mathbf{B}
)
edge-witnessing
star-refining
strictly anti-injective
anti-injective
subcategories
ideals

Figure 2.
Implications between main classes of special morphisms.

Star-refining morphisms are related to co-edge-witnessing morphisms
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
, i.e. where
\sqsubset
is edge-witnessing. Explicitly this means that, for all
f,g\in G
,

f\mathrel{\sqcap}g\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\exists h\in H\ (f\sqsubset h\sqsupset g).

This is especially true for graphs of low degree. First recall that the
degree
of a vertex
g
is the size of its neighbourhood
|g^{\sim}|
, i.e. the number of vertices it is adjacent to. The degree of a graph
G
is defined to be the maximum degree of its vertices, which we denote by

\mathrm{deg}(G)=\max_{g\in G}|g^{\sim}|.

Proposition 3.5
.

Any star-refining
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
is co-edge-witnessing. If
\mathrm{deg}(F)\leq 2
, a composition of co-edge-witnessing
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{G}^{G}_{F}
is star-refining, i.e.

\text{$\sqsupset$ and $\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}$ are co-edge-witnessing}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\text{$\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}$ is star-refining}.

Proof.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
is star-refining then, for any
f,g\in G
with
f\mathrel{\sqcap}g
, we have
h\in H
with
f,g\in g^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset}
, showing that
\sqsupset
is co-edge-witnessing. On the other hand, say
\mathrm{deg}(F)\leq 2
and we have co-edge-witnessing
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{G}^{G}_{F}
. For any
f\in F
, we then have at most two other
e,e^{\prime}\in f^{\sqcap}
. As
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is co-edge-witnessing, we have
g
and
g^{\prime}
with
e,f\in g^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
and
e,f^{\prime}\in g^{\prime\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
. In particular,
e\in g^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\cap g^{\prime\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
so
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
. As
\sqsupset
is also co-edge-witnessing, we have
h\in H
with
g,g^{\prime}\in h^{\sqsupset}
and hence
f^{\sqcap}=\{e,e^{\prime},f\}\subseteq\{g,g^{\prime}\}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
. This shows that
\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is star-refining.
∎

A
triangle

T
in a graph
G
is a complete subgraph with exactly
3
vertices, i.e.
T=\{f,g,h\}\subseteq G
and
f\sim g\sim h\sim f
. We call
G

triangle-free
if it has no triangles.

Proposition 3.6
.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
and
H
is triangle-free then

\sqsupset\,\text{is star-refining and edge-surjective}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\sqsupset\,\text{is edge-witnessing}.

Proof.

Assume
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{G}^{H}
is star-refining and edge-surjective but not edge-witnessing. This means we have adjacent
h,h^{\prime}\in H
such that
h^{\sqsupset}
and
h^{\prime\sqsupset}
are disjoint. However, we still have
g\sqsubset h
and
g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}
with
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
, by edge-surjectivity. If we had
i\in H
with
g,g^{\prime}\sqsubset i
, necessarily with
h\neq i\neq h^{\prime}
, then edge-preservation would imply that
\{h,h^{\prime},i\}
is a triangle of
H
. Thus if
H
is triangle-free then
i^{\sqsupset}
can not contain both
g
and
g^{\prime}
, for any
i\in H
. In particular,
g^{\sqcap}\nsubseteq i^{\sqsupset}
, for any
i\in H
, which contradicts our assumption that
\sqsupset
is star-refining.
∎

Recall that a vertex
g\in G
is isolated if it has degree
0
. If the graphs in question have no isolated vertices, co-injective morphisms are often anti-injective.

Proposition 3.7
.

If
H
has no isolated vertices then, for any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{I}^{H}_{G}
,

\displaystyle\sqsupset\,\text{is edge-witnessing}\qquad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\sqsupset\,\text{is anti-injective}.

Similarly, if
G
has no isolated vertices then, for any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{I}^{H}_{G}
,

\displaystyle\sqsupset\,\text{is star-refining}\qquad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\sqsupset\,\text{is anti-injective}.

Proof.

Take an edge-witnessing
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{I}^{H}_{G}
. If
H
has no isolated vertices, every
h\in H
is adjacent to some
i\in H
. We then have
g\in G
witnessing
h\mathrel{\sqcap}i
, i.e.
g^{\sqsubset}=\{h,i\}
. As
\sqsupset
is co-injective, we must also have some other
f\in G
with
f^{\sqsubset}=\{h\}
and hence
\{f,g\}\subseteq h^{\sqsupset}
. As
h
was arbitrary, this shows that
\sqsupset
is anti-injective.

Now take any star-refining
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{I}^{H}_{G}
. As
\sqsupset
is co-injective, for every
h\in H
, we have
g\in G
with
g^{\sqsubset}=\{h\}
. Since
\sqsupset
is star-refining, there is
h^{\prime}\in H
with
g^{\sqcap}\subseteq h^{\prime\sqsupset}
. As
g\in g^{\sqcap}
, the only possibility is that
h^{\prime}=h
. Thus
|h^{\sqsupset}|\geq|g^{\sqcap}|\geq 2
, as long as
g
is not isolated. If
G
has no isolated vertices, this shows that
\sqsupset
is anti-injective.
∎

Under certain conditions, we can also show surjectivity implies edge-surjectivity.

A
cycle
is a connected graph in which very vertex has degree
2
. A graph
G
is
acyclic
if it contains no cycles, or, equivalently, if for all adjacent
a
and
b
we can partition
G
into subsets
A\ni a
and
B\ni b
such that
(a,b)
is the only edge between
A
and
B
.

Proposition 3.8
.

If
G
is connected and
H
is acyclic then, for any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{G}^{H}
,

\text{$\sqsupset$ is surjective}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\text{$\sqsupset$ is edge-surjective}.

Proof.

Take any adjacent
a,b\in H
. As
H
is acyclic, we can partition
H
into subsets
A\ni a
and
B\ni b
such that
(a,b)
is the only edge between them. If
\sqsupset
is surjective then
A^{\sqsupset}
and
B^{\sqsupset}
are non-empty and cover
G
. As
G
is connected, it follows that we have
f\in A^{\sqsupset}
and
g\in B^{\sqsupset}
with
f\mathrel{\sqcap}g
(if
A^{\sqsupset}
and
B^{\sqsupset}
are disjoint then they partition
G
so this follows immediately from connectedness, otherwise we have
j\in A^{\sqsupset}\cap B^{\sqsupset}
and we can just set
f=g=j
). Pick
a^{\prime}\in A
and
b^{\prime}\in B
such that
f\sqsubset a^{\prime}
and
g\sqsubset b^{\prime}
. Since
f\sqcap g
and
\sqsupset
is edge-preserving, we have
a^{\prime}\mathrel{\sqcap}b^{\prime}
. Since
(a,b)
is the only edge between
A
and
B
, it follows that
a^{\prime}=a
and
b^{\prime}=b
. This shows that
\sqsupset
is edge-surjective.
∎

A
tree
is a connected acyclic graph.
Since we shall be concerned with categories of trees in our examples, let us summarize the special properties of morphisms between trees, as a corollary of Propositions
3.6
–
3.8
.

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
G
and
H
be nondegenerate trees and let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}^{H}_{G}
.
Then
{\sqsupset}
is edge-surjective and we have

\text{$\sqsupset$ is star-refining}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{$\sqsupset$ is edge-witnessing}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{$\sqsupset$ is anti-injective}.

In
§​​ 5.2
we will consider morphisms satisfying certain extra properties. First let us call
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}

monotone
if, for all
C\subseteq H
,

C\text{ is connected}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad C^{\sqsupset}\text{ is connected}.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
is monotone, the preimage of each vertex
h^{\sqsupset}
is connected. If
\sqsupset
is edge-surjective, this is also sufficient for
\sqsupset
to be monotone.

Proposition 3.10
.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
is surjective and monotone, then
\sqsupset
is edge-surjective. Moreover, if
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
is edge-surjective, then

\forall h\in H\ (h^{\sqsupset}\text{ is connected})\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\sqsupset\,\text{is monotone}.

Proof.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
is monotone then, for any
h,h^{\prime}\in H
with
h\mathrel{\sqcap}h^{\prime}
, it follows that
h^{\sqsupset}\cup h^{\prime\sqsupset}
is connected. If
\sqsupset
is also surjective then
h^{\sqsupset}\neq\emptyset\neq h^{\prime\sqsupset}
and so connectivity implies that we have
g\sqsubset h
and
g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}
with
g\mathrel{\sqcap}g^{\prime}
, showing
\sqsupset
is edge-surjective.

Now assume
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}^{H}_{G}
is edge-surjective and
h^{\sqsupset}
is connected, for all
h\in H
. Take any connected
C\subseteq H
and say we had a discrete partition
A,B\subseteq C^{\sqsupset}
, i.e.
C=A\cup B
with no edge between
A
and
B
.
If we had
c\in A^{\sqsubset}\cap B^{\sqsubset}\cap C
then
A\cap c^{\sqsupset}
and
B\cap c^{\sqsupset}
would discretely partition
c^{\sqsupset}
, contradicting our assumption on
\sqsupset
. But if
A^{\sqsubset}\cap B^{\sqsubset}\cap C=\emptyset
then
A^{\sqsubset}\cap C
and
B^{\sqsubset}\cap C
would discretely partition
C
, as
\sqsupset
is edge-surjective, contradicting its connectedness. Thus no such partition exists, i.e.
C^{\sqsupset}
is also connected, showing that
\sqsupset
is monotone.
∎

Monotone edge-witnessing morphisms are often also co-edge-witnessing.

Proposition 3.11
.

If
G
is acyclic then, for any monotone
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{G}^{H}
,

\sqsupset\,\text{is edge-witnessing}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\sqsupset\,\text{is co-edge-witnessing}.

Proof.

Take any
e,f\in G
with
e\mathrel{\sqcap}f
. If
e=f
then the co-surjectivity of
\sqsupset
already yields
h\in e^{\sqsubset}=f^{\sqsubset}
. Otherwise, as
G
is acyclic, we can partition
G
into
E\ni e
and
F\ni f
with
E^{\sqcap}\cap F^{\sqcap}=\{e,f\}
. As
\sqsupset
is co-surjective, we have
j\sqsupset e
and
k\sqsupset f
, necessarily with
j\mathrel{\sqcap}k
, as
\sqsupset
is also edge-preserving. If
\sqsupset
is also edge-witnessing then we have
g\in j^{\sqsupset}\cap k^{\sqsupset}
so
j\in g^{\sqsubset}\cap e^{\sqsubset}
and
k\in g^{\sqsubset}\cap f^{\sqsubset}
. But
g
lies in
E
or
F
and hence
j
or
k
lies in
E^{\sqsubset}\cap F^{\sqsubset}
. However,
\sqsupset
is monotone so, for any
h\in E^{\sqsubset}\cap F^{\sqsubset}
,
h^{\sqsupset}
must be a connected subset containing elements of
E
and
F
, which can only happen if
e,f\in h^{\sqsupset}
. This shows that
\sqsupset
is co-edge-witnessing.
∎

To show that the ideals of edge-witnessing and star-refining morphisms are wide in a given category, we often use the following result, which, for every graph
G
, yields a graph
H
of a similar shape together with a suitable morphism from
H
to
G
.

Lemma 3.12

(Edge splitting)
.

For any graph
G
we consider the graph
H
where every edge of
G
is split intro three edges by two new vertices, namely
H=G\cup\{s_{g,g^{\prime}}:g\sim g^{\prime}\in G\}
with the edge relation
g\sqcap s_{g,g^{\prime}}\sqcap s_{g^{\prime},g}\sqcap g^{\prime}
for every adjacent
g,g^{\prime}\in G
.
Moreover we consider
{\sqsupset}\subseteq G\times H
defined by
g^{\sqsubset}=\{g\}
and
s_{g,g^{\prime}}^{\sqsubset}=\{g,g^{\prime}\}
.
Then
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}^{G}_{H}
and it is monotone, edge-witnessing, and star-refining.

Proof.

It is clear from the definition that
\sqsupset
is co-bijective and edge-witnessing.
As
\{g,s_{g,g^{\prime}},s_{g^{\prime},g},g^{\prime}\}^{\sqsubset}=\{g,g^{\prime}\}
for every adjacent
g,g^{\prime}\in G
, we have that
\sqsupset
is edge-preserving.
For every
h\in H
we have
h^{\sqcap}=\{g,s_{g,g^{\prime}}:g^{\prime}\sim_{G}g\}
if
h=g\in G
and
h^{\sqcap}=\{g,s_{g,g^{\prime}},s_{g^{\prime},g}\}
if
h=s_{g,g^{\prime}}
.
In both cases,
h^{\sqcap}\subseteq g^{\sqsupset}
, and so
\sqsupset
is star-refining.
Finally, for every
g\in G
we have
g^{\sqsupset}=\{g,s_{g,g^{\prime}},s_{g^{\prime},g}:g^{\prime}\sim_{G}g\}
, which is connected, and so
\sqsupset
is monotone by
Proposition 3.10
.
∎

We conclude the section with a little observation we will need later on.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
S
and
T
be trees. If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{S}^{T}
is surjective and monotone,
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is connected and
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
then
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}={\sqsupset}\cap(T^{\prime}\times S^{\prime})
is monotone.

Proof.

For every connected
C\subseteq T^{\prime}
we have
C^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}=C^{\sqsupset}\cap S^{\prime}
, which is connected as the intersection of connected subgraphs of a tree.
∎

4.
Compacta from sequences of graphs

Our aim is to construct, in the spirit of
§​​ 2.2
, spectra of
\omega
-posets coming from sequences in suitable graph categories.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a subcategory of the graph category
\mathbf{G}
.
A
sequence
in
\mathbf{C}
is a functor from the thin category
\omega
to
\mathbf{C}
. More explicitly, this is a collection of morphisms
{\sqsupset}_{n}^{m}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m}}_{G_{n}}
, for
m\leq n\in\omega
, such that each
{\sqsupset}_{n}^{n}
is the identity morphism and

l\leq m\leq n\in\omega\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad{\sqsupset_{m}^{l}\circ\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}={\sqsupset_{n}^{l}}.

When writing “let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{C}
” we mean that the
G_{n}
’s are objects of
\mathbf{C}
,
{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\in\mathbf{C}_{G_{n}}^{G_{m}}
for all
m\leq n
, and
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a sequence.

We say that such a sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
lies in
I\subseteq\mathbf{C}
if
{\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\in I
whenever
m<n
. Sequences will be named accordingly, e.g. we call
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
an “anti-injective sequence” if
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is anti-injective, whenever
m<n
. Note that for
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
to lie in an ideal
I
, it suffices that
{\sqsupset}^{m}_{m+1}\in I
, for all
m
. In particular, a co-injective/co-surjective/edge-surjective sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is anti-injective/star-refining/edge-witnessing if and only if the morphisms
\sqsupset^{m}_{m+1}
are, thanks to
Proposition 3.4
.

Now we come to the key definition of the paper. Recall that
\mathbf{S}
is the wide subcategory of
\mathbf{G}
with co-surjective morphisms (see
§​​ 3
).

Definition 4.1
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
. We define the
induced poset

(\mathbb{P},\leq)
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
as the disjoint union
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}G_{n}\times\{n\}
with
(p,n)\leq(q,m)
if and only if
p\sqsubset^{m}_{n}q
.
For convenience, we may assume that the sets
G_{n}
are pairwise disjoint so we can identify them with the sets
G_{n}\times\{n\}
. Then we have

\mathbb{P}=\bigcup_{n\in\omega}G_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\leq}\ =\bigcup_{m\leq n}\sqsubset_{n}^{m}.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
.
Then
\mathbb{P}
is an infinite graded
\omega
-poset whose
n
th
rank-set
\mathsf{r}^{-1}(\{n\})
is the graph
G_{n}
, for every
n\in\omega
.
Moreover, the following are equivalent.

(1)

The poset
\mathbb{P}
is atomless.

(2)

The
n
th
level coincides with the
n
th
rank-set, i.e.
\mathbb{P}_{n}=G_{n}
, for every
n\in\omega
.

(3)

The sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is surjective.

Proof.

As each
G_{n}
is non-empty,
\mathbb{P}
is infinite.
Since each
\sqsupset_{n}^{0}
is co-surjective, elements of
G_{0}
correspond to maximal elements.
Fix
p<q
with
p\in G_{n}
and
q\in G_{l}
. For every
m
such that
l<m<n
there is
r\in G_{m}
with
p<r<q
as
{\sqsupset_{m}^{l}}\circ{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}={\sqsupset_{n}^{l}}
.
Induction and co-surjectivity of each
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
then imply that each element of
G_{k}
has rank
k
, and the rank function maps intervals to intervals. This shows that
\mathbb{P}
is graded and that
G_{n}
is its
n^{\mathrm{th}}
rank-set.
As each
G_{n}
is finite, then each principal upset
p^{<}
is finite and
\mathbb{P}
is an
\omega
-poset.

We are left to show that the statements
(1)
–
(3)
are equivalent. Suppose
(1)
holds. In general we have
G_{n}=\mathsf{r}^{-1}(\{n\})\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{n}
, so to show
(2)
it is enough to take
p\in\mathbb{P}
with
\mathsf{r}(p)<n
and show that
p\notin\mathbb{P}_{n}
. As
\mathbb{P}
is atomless, we have
q<p
. Since
\mathbb{P}
is graded, we have
q^{\prime}
with
q\leq q^{\prime}<p
and
\mathsf{r}(q^{\prime})=\mathsf{r}(p)+1\leq n
. This shows that
p
is not minimal in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and hence
p\notin\mathbb{P}_{n}
.

Now suppose
(2)
holds. For every
p\in G_{n}=\mathbb{P}_{n}
, we have
q\leq p
such that
q\in\mathbb{P}_{n+1}=G_{n+1}
, and so
\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}
is surjective.
Since composing surjective relations give surjective relations, each
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is surjective.

Finally suppose
(3)
.
For every
p\in\mathbb{P}
, we have
n
with
p\in G_{n}
and then we have
q\in G_{n+1}
with
p\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}q
so
p>q
and hence
\mathbb{P}
is atomless.
∎

On the other hand, given an infinite graded
\omega
-poset
\mathbb{P}
we may put

G_{n}=\mathsf{r}^{-1}(\{n\})\quad\text{and}\quad{\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\ =\ {\geq}\cap{(G_{m}\times G_{n})},

for every
m\leq n\in\omega
.
Since
\mathbb{P}
is infinite, every set
G_{n}
is non-empty, and from the definition of the rank, the relations
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
are co-surjective.

Note that the definition of the induced poset and the previous proposition do not use the edge relations of the graphs
G_{n}
, and so they apply when we are concerned with co-surjective relations between sets.
Also, when sets are viewed as graphs with no edges, every relation is edge-preserving.
It is then easy to see that (
4.1
) and (
4
) are mutually inverse constructions yielding a one-to-one correspondence between infinite graded
\omega
-posets and sequences of co-surjective relations between non-empty finite sets.

4.1.
Threads and limits

In this short digression, we relate our notion of spectrum to the standard concept of inverse limits, using threads. We also sketch a comparison with similar approaches as defined in
[
IS06
]
and
[
DT18
]
.

By the
spectrum
of a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{S}
we mean the spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
of its induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, i.e. the family of all minimal selectors in
\mathbb{P}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a second-countable compact
\mathsf{T}_{1}
space (
Theorem 2.7
) which we then view as a kind of topological limit of the sequence of graphs.

By a
thread
we mean a sequence of points
(t_{n})\in\prod_{n\in\omega}G_{n}
such that
t_{0}>t_{1}>t_{2}>\cdots
in
\mathbb{P}
, i.e. we have
t_{m}\sqsupset^{m}_{n}t_{n}
for every
m\leq n
.
We shall also view a thread as a subset of
\mathbb{P}
whenever convenient.

Recall that the standard inverse limit of a sequence
(X_{n})
of compact Hausdorff spaces and continuous bonding maps
f_{n}\colon X_{n+1}\to X_{n}
is the space of all threads
\{(x_{n})\in\prod_{n}X_{n}:\forall n\ x_{n}=f_{n}(x_{n+1})\}
, which is a closed subspace of the product.
In the spectrum, threads play an important role as well.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, and let
S
be a minimal selector.
Then there is a thread
(s_{n})
such that
S=(s_{n})^{\leq}
.

Proof.

By
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.13]
,
S
is a filter. Hence we can build
(t_{k})
, a strictly decreasing sequence in
S
, such that
(t_{k})^{\leq}=S
. We can thus fill the gaps between
t_{k+1}
and
t_{k}
with linearly ordered elements so that every level is intersected. The resulting sequence
(s_{n})
will be as required.
∎

Let us compare our approach to constructing spaces from sequences of graphs to a more standard approach using quotients of inverse limits, as used by Irwin and Solecki
[
IS06
]
in the context of projective Fraïssé theory and by Dębski and Tymchatyn
[
DT18
]
in the context of constructing topologically complete spaces.
We shall also demonstrate a possible closer connection on a simple example.

In the inverse limit approach, we start with a sequence of graphs
(G_{n})
and bonding maps
f_{n}\colon G_{n}\leftarrow G_{n+1}
that are quotient homomorphisms (i.e. edge-preserving edge-surjective functions).
We consider the inverse limit graph

\textstyle G_{\infty}=\{(x_{n})\in\prod_{n\in\omega}G_{n}:\forall n\ x_{n}=f_{n}(x_{n+1})\}

with the edge relation
(x_{n})\sqcap(y_{n})\Leftrightarrow\forall n\ x_{n}\sqcap y_{n}
.
Moreover, we endow
G_{\infty}
with the subspace topology of the product of the finite discrete spaces.
Hence,
G_{\infty}
becomes a zero-dimensional metrizable compact space, typically the Cantor space, and
\sqcap
becomes a closed relation.
Note that
\sqcap
is always reflexive and symmetric. If it turns out to be also transitive,
G_{\infty}
is called a
pre-space
, and we obtain the desired compact space as the quotient
G_{\infty}/{\sqcap}
.

In our approach we allow the bonding maps to be relations.
Namely, we start with an edge-faithful (see
§​​ 4.3
) sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{B}
.
Then we consider the induced poset
\mathbb{P}
and we obtain our space as the spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Note
\mathbb{P}
is well-defined also for a sequence of functions and the definition of
G_{\infty}
may be extended to cover sequences of relations as well, though it will not be a categorical limit any more.

The elements
p\in\mathbb{P}
correspond to basic clopen subsets of
G_{\infty}
, which become closed sets in
G_{\infty}/{\sqcap}
, but they also correspond to basic open subsets of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Elements of a single graph
G_{n}
give a clopen partition of
G_{\infty}
, a “touching” closed cover of
G_{\infty}/{\sqcap}
, and an overlapping open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
. In both cases the structure of touching or overlapping is given by the edge relation of the graph.

Example 4.4
.

Let
G_{n}
be a path of
2^{n}
vertices, and let
f_{n}\colon G_{n}\leftarrow G_{n+1}
be a monotone edge-preserving surjection splitting every vertex of
G_{n}
into two vertices of
G_{n+1}
.
Then the induced poset
\mathbb{P}
is the Cantor tree, the limit graph
G_{\infty}
is the Cantor space with the edge relation being the equivalence gluing gaps in the middle-third interval representation of the Cantor space.
In other words, we obtain a canonical construction of the arc as
G_{\infty}/{\sqcap}
.
If we imagine the arc as the unit interval
[0,1]
, the elements of
\mathbb{P}
correspond to closed subintervals of the form
[k/2^{n},(k+1)/2^{n}]
.

Of course,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is just the Cantor space. The poset
\mathbb{P}
does not see the edges of the graphs
G_{n}
.
However, we may consider a closely related sequence of graphs and bonding relations.
For every
n\in\omega
let
H_{n}
be the graph of all maximal cliques in
G_{n}
, with the edge relation on
H_{n}
being the overlapping of cliques.
Hence, for
n\geq 1
,
H_{n}
is the path of
2^{n}-1
consecutive edges of
G_{n}
, while
H_{0}
is a singleton.
We put
h\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}h^{\prime}
if
f[h]\subseteq h^{\prime}
.
This induces a sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
and a poset
\mathbb{Q}
.
The induced poset
\mathbb{Q}
is (ignoring the first level coming from
H_{0}
) exactly the poset from
[
BBV25
, Example 2.16]
whose spectrum is again the arc.
The sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-preserving, co-bijective, and edge-witnessing.
We can identify the elements of
H_{n}
with open intervals
[0,2/2^{n}),\ldots,(k/2^{n},(k+2)/2^{n}),\ldots,(1-2/2^{n},1]\subseteq[0,1]
, obtained by taking the interiors of unions of pairs of consecutive closed intervals corresponding to the elements of
G_{n}
.

[0,1]
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
[\frac{1}{2},1]
[0,\frac{1}{4}]
[\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2}]
[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4}]
[\frac{3}{4},1]
[0,\frac{1}{8}]
[\frac{1}{8},\frac{1}{4}]
[\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{8}]
[\frac{3}{8},\frac{1}{2}]
[\frac{1}{2},\frac{5}{8}]
[\frac{5}{8},\frac{3}{4}]
[\frac{3}{4},\frac{7}{8}]
[\frac{7}{8},1]
\vdots
[0,1]
[0,1]
[0,\frac{1}{2})
(\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4})
(\frac{3}{4},1]
[0,\frac{1}{4})
(\frac{1}{8},\frac{3}{8})
(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2})
(\frac{3}{8},\frac{5}{8})
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{4})
(\frac{5}{8},\frac{7}{8})
(\frac{3}{4},1]
\vdots

Figure 3.
Obtaining the unit interval using the two approaches.

4.2.
Subsequences and restrictions

A
subsequence
of a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{G}
is a sequence of the form
(G_{\phi(n)},\sqsupset^{\phi(m)}_{\phi(n)})
for some strictly increasing
\phi\colon\omega\to\omega
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, and let
(\sqsupset^{\phi(m)}_{\phi(n)})
be a subsequence with induced poset
\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
\mathbb{Q}
is the subposet
\bigcup_{n}\mathbb{P}_{\phi(n)}\subseteq\mathbb{P}
, and
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
are homeomorphic via

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}\ni S\mapsto S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}\ni T\mapsto T\cap\mathbb{Q}.

Proof.

Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is surjective, we have
\mathbb{P}_{n}=G_{n}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{n}=G_{\phi(n)}=\mathbb{P}_{\phi(n)}
for every
n\in\omega
, and
{\leq}_{\mathbb{Q}}={\leq}_{\mathbb{P}}\restriction\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}
.
Since
\{\mathbb{P}_{\phi(n)}:n\in\omega\}
is a co-initial family of bands both in
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, that the above maps are homeomorphisms follows from
[
BBV25
, Corollary 3.8]
, as well as from
Proposition 4.13
.
∎

It is useful to keep in mind the following lemma when discussing properties like “
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining and anti-injective subsequence”.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in a category
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
and let
I\subseteq\mathbf{C}
be an ideal.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a subsequence in
I
.

(2)

For every
m
there is
n\geq m
such that
{\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\in I
.

(3)

Every subsequence
(\sqsupset^{\phi(m)}_{\phi(n)})
of
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a subsequence in
I
.

Hence, for another ideal
J\subseteq\mathbf{C}
, we have that
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a subsequence in
I
and a subsequence in
J
if and only if it has a subsequence in
I\cap J
.

Proof.

Suppose
(1)
and fix
m\in\omega
. Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a subsequence in
I
we can find
k
and
k^{\prime}
such that
m\leq k<k^{\prime}
and
{\sqsupset}^{k}_{k^{\prime}}\in I
. Since
I
is an ideal then
{\sqsupset}^{m}_{k^{\prime}}\in I
, showing
(2)
.
Now suppose
(2)
and let
(\sqsupset^{\phi(m)}_{\phi(n)})
be a subsequence.
We inductively define an increasing map
\psi\colon\omega\to\omega
such that the subsequence
(\sqsupset^{\phi(\psi(m))}_{\phi(\psi(n))})
is in
I
.
First we set
\psi(0)=0
.
Now suppose we have already defined
\psi(k)
.
By
(2)
, we have
n\geq\phi(\psi(k))
such that
{\sqsupset}^{\phi(\psi(k))}_{n}\in I
and then we have
\psi(k+1)>\psi(k)
such that
\phi(\psi(k+1))\geq n
.
Then
{\sqsupset}^{\phi(\psi(k))}_{\phi(\psi(k+1))}\in I
, since
I
is an ideal.
This concludes the construction and proves
(3)
.
The implication
(3)
\Rightarrow
(1)
is trivial, and to obtain a subsequence in
I\cap J
we just apply
(3)
to a subsequence in
J
.
∎

Next we focus on another form of a “subsequence”, related to subspaces of the spectrum.

Definition 4.7
.

A
restriction
of a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{G}
is a sequence
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
such that every
H_{n}\subseteq G_{n}
is an (induced) subgraph and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{m}_{n}={\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\restriction(H_{m}\times H_{n})
for every
m\leq n
.
An
upper restriction
is a restriction such that
H_{n}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq H_{m}
for every
m\leq n
.

Remark 4.8
.

Note that not every sequence of subgraphs
H_{n}\subseteq G_{n}
induces a restriction by putting
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{m}_{n}={\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\restriction(H_{m}\times H_{n})
as
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
may not be a sequence, only a
lax-sequence
, i.e. it satisfies
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{l}_{m}\circ{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{m}_{n}\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{l}_{n}
for
l\leq m\leq n
.
(Consider, for example, every
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
being the identity on a two-point discrete graph and
H_{n}
omitting one vertex if and only if
n
is odd.)
We shall call such
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
a
lax-restriction
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
.

On the other hand, every sequence of subgraphs such that
H_{n}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq H_{m}
for every
m\leq n
induces an upper restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
by putting
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{m}_{n}={\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\restriction(H_{m}\times H_{n})
.

Upper restrictions are often induced by
traces
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
and let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
For every
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
and
n\in\omega
let
[A]_{n}
denote the
trace

\{g\in G_{n}:g^{\in}\cap A\neq\emptyset\}
.
In particular, for
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
we have
[\{S\}]_{n}=G_{n}\cap S
, and so
[A]_{n}=G_{n}\cap\bigcup A
.

Clearly we have
[A]_{n}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq[A]_{m}
for every
m\leq n
.
If the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is surjective, then every trace
[A]_{n}
is non-empty since the levels of
\mathbb{P}
correspond to the graphs
G_{n}
by
Proposition 4.2
.
Moreover, every family
\{g^{\in}:g\in[A]_{n}\}
is an open cover of
A
in
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
.

(1)

For
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
a surjective upper restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{Q}
, we have that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
is the closed subspace
\{S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}:S\subseteq\mathbb{Q}\}\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
and that
[\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}]_{n}\subseteq H_{n}
for every
n
.

(2)

For a subspace
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
, we have
[A]_{n}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}=[A]_{m}
for every
m\leq n\in\omega
, i.e.
([A]_{n})
induces a surjective upper restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, and we have
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}=\overline{A}
, where
\mathbb{Q}
is the poset induced by
([A]_{n})
.

Proof.

We show
(1)
.
From the definition of an upper restriction we have that
\mathbb{Q}
is an upwards closed subset of
\mathbb{P}
.
Since the sequences
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
and
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
are surjective, the posets
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are atomless, and so
\mathbb{P}_{n}=G_{n}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{n}=H_{n}=G_{n}\cap\mathbb{Q}
.
It follows that upwards closed selectors in
\mathbb{Q}
are precisely upwards closed selectors in
\mathbb{P}
that are contained in
\mathbb{Q}
, and so the same is true for minimal selectors.
Clearly,
\{S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}:S\subseteq\mathbb{Q}\}
is closed.

To show
(2)
let
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
and
m\leq n\in\omega
.
It is easy to see that
[A]_{n}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq[A]_{m}
.
On the other hand, for
g\in[A]_{m}
there is
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
with
g\in S
.
Let
(s_{k})
be a thread such that
(s_{k})^{\leq}=S
, obtained by
Lemma 4.3
.
Note that
s_{k}\in[A]_{k}
for every
k
.
There is
k\geq n
such that
g>s_{k}
, i.e.
g\sqsupset^{m}_{k}s_{k}
.
From the definition of a sequence there is
h\in G_{n}
with
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}h\sqsupset^{n}_{k}s_{k}
.
Necessarily
h\in S
, and so
h\in[A]_{n}
.
This shows
[A]_{n}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}=[A]_{m}
.
From
(1)
it follows that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}=\{S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}:[\{S\}]_{n}\subseteq[A]_{n}\text{ for every $n$}\}
.
Hence,
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
and also
\overline{A}\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
as
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
is closed.
On the other hand, for if for
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
we have that
[\{S\}]_{n}\subseteq[A]_{n}
for every
n\in\omega
, then for every
p\in S
there is
S^{\prime}\in A\cap p^{\in}
, and so
S\in\overline{A}
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

The previous proposition gives a correspondence between closed subspaces of the spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
and surjective upper restrictions of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, analogous to
[
BBV25
, §2.4]
: every closed
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
gives the restriction induced by
([A]_{n})
, whose spectrum is again
A
.
On the other hand, if we start with a surjective upper restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
and take the corresponding closed subspace
A=\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
, we have only
[A]_{n}\subseteq H_{n}
by
Proposition 4.10

(1)
.
The equality would correspond to the property that every
h\in H_{n}
is an element of a minimal selector contained in
\mathbb{Q}
, i.e. when
\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\mathbb{P}
is a
prime subset

[
BBV25
, §2.4]
, or in this case equivalently when
\mathbb{Q}
is a prime poset.

Hence, by a
prime restriction
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
we mean a surjective upper restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
such that its induced poset
\mathbb{Q}
is prime, or equivalently such that it is induced by
([A]_{n})
for some
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Note that if an upper restriction is in
\mathbf{B}
, then it is prime as every predetermined
\omega
-poset is prime (
Figure 1
).
Altogether, we have a one-to-one correspondence between closed subspaces of the spectrum and prime restrictions, analogously to
[
BBV25
, Corollary 2.25]
.

Even if our surjective restriction is not upper, it is sometimes still possible to obtain a canonical embedding between the respective spectra.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
.
We call a subset
H_{n}\subseteq G_{n}

dense
if
{H_{n}}^{\sqsupset^{n}_{k}}=G_{k}
for some
k\geq n
.
Equivalently, if
H_{n}
is a cap contained in
G_{n}
.

A restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is dense if each
H_{n}
is dense.

Note that if
(g_{n})
is a thread and we have
m\in\omega
with
g_{n}{}^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}=\{g_{m}\}
, for all
n\geq m
, then
g_{m}
belongs to all dense subsets of
G_{m}
.
Also note that if
H_{n}\subseteq G_{n}
is dense, then
H_{n}^{\sqcap}=G_{n}
.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, and let
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
be a surjective dense restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
with induced poset
\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
S\mapsto S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
is a homeomorphism
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
with inverse
T\mapsto T\cap\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition 4.2
,
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are atomless and their levels correspond to the
G_{n}
and
H_{n}
, respectively.
We want to use Lemmas
2.8
and
2.9
.
To do so we need to show that for every
S\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
and
T\subseteq S^{\leq_{\mathbb{P}}}
a minimal selector in
\mathbb{P}
we have that
T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is infinite.
In fact, suppose
T
is any upwards closed selector in
\mathbb{P}
.
We show that
T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is infinite.

Fix
m\in\omega
.
Since
H_{m}
is dense, there is
n\geq m
such that
H_{m}{}^{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}=G_{n}
.
Since
T
is a selector, there is
t\in T\cap G_{n}
.
By the choice of
n
, there is
h\in t^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\cap H_{m}
.
Since
T
is upwards closed,
h\in(T\cap\mathbb{Q})\cap G_{m}
.
As
m
was arbitrary,
T\cap\mathbb{Q}
is infinite.
∎

4.3.
Faithful representations

Note that so far there was no connection between edge relations of the graphs
G_{n}
and the induced poset
\mathbb{P}
.
If the edge relations are to properly detect overlaps of the corresponding open sets, then they should agree with the common lower bound relation.
Recall that in a poset
\mathbb{P}
the relation
\wedge
denotes the existence of a lower bound i.e.
p\wedge q
if and only if
p,q\geq r
, for some
r\in\mathbb{P}
.
We call a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})

edge-faithful
if, for every
n\in\omega
and every
g,h\in G_{n}
, we have
g\sqcap h
if and only if
g\wedge h
in
\mathbb{P}
.
Note that if every
G_{n}
is just a set then there is a unique way to define the edge relations so that
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
becomes edge-faithful, specifically by setting
{\sqcap_{n}}=(G_{n}\times G_{n})\cap{\wedge}
.

We summarize the connection between the edge relation and the actual overlaps, and we characterize edge-faithful sequences.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
and let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
For every
m\in\omega
and
g,h\in G_{m}
we have

g^{\in}\cap h^{\in}\neq\emptyset\quad\Rightarrow\quad g\wedge h\text{ (in $\mathbb{P}$)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad g\sqcap h\text{ (in $G_{m}$).}

If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective and edge-faithful, then the implications become equivalences.

Proof.

Suppose
S\in g^{\in}\cap h^{\in}
, which means
g,h\in S
.
As the minimal selector
S
is a filter (
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.13]
), there is
p\in S
with
p\leq g,h
, and so
g\wedge h
.

Edge-faithfulness is by definition the equivalence
g\wedge h\Leftrightarrow g\sqcap h
.
But one implication is true for any sequence in
\mathbf{S}
.
Suppose
g\wedge h
.
Then there is
n\geq m
and
p_{n}\in G_{n}
such that
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}p_{n}\sqsubset^{m}_{n}h
, and hence
g\sqcap h
since
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is edge-preserving.
Moreover, if
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective, there is
p_{n+1}\in G_{n+1}
with
p_{n+1}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}=\{p_{n}\}
, and by induction there is a thread
(p_{i})
such that
(p_{i})^{\leq}\cap G_{k}=\{p_{k}\}
for every
k\geq n
.
Hence,
(p_{k})^{\leq}
is a minimal selector in
g^{\in}\cap h^{\in}
.
∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
G_{n}
be a sequence of finite graphs, and let
{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq G_{m}\times G_{n}
,
m\leq n
, be a surjective co-surjective sequence (not necessarily edge-preserving). Let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-faithful.

(2)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-preserving and for every
m\in\omega
there is
n>m
such that
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is edge-witnessing.

(3)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-preserving and edge-surjective and has an edge-witnessing subsequence.

Proof.

Suppose
(1)
.
Let
h\sqsupset^{m}_{n}g\mathrel{\sqcap}g^{\prime}\sqsubset^{m}_{n}h^{\prime}
. We have
g\mathrel{\wedge}g^{\prime}
, so there is
p\in\mathbb{P}
such that
h\geq g\geq p\leq g^{\prime}\leq h^{\prime}
, and hence
h\wedge h^{\prime}
and
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
. We have shown that
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is edge-preserving.
Now let
m\in\omega
.
For every edge
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
in
G_{m}
there is
p_{g,g^{\prime}}\leq g,g^{\prime}
.
Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is surjective and so
\mathbb{P}
is atomless, we can suppose
p_{g,g^{\prime}}\in G_{n_{g,g^{\prime}}}
for some
n_{g,g^{\prime}}>m
even when
g=g^{\prime}
.
Again by the surjectivity, for
n\geq\max\{n_{g,g^{\prime}}:g\sqcap g^{\prime}\in G_{n}\}>m
we have that
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is edge-witnessing, giving
(2)
.

Next suppose
(2)
.
Clearly, we can inductively define and edge-witnessing subsequence.
Also for every
m\in\omega
and
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
