in
G_{m}
there is
n>m
with edge-witnessing
{\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}={\sqsupset}^{m}_{m+1}\circ{\sqsupset}^{m+1}_{n}
, and so there are
h
,
h^{\prime}
and
p
with
g\sqsupset^{m}_{m+1}h\sqsupset^{m+1}_{n}p\sqsubset^{m+1}_{n}h^{\prime}\sqsubset^{m}_{m+1}g^{\prime}
.
As
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-preserving, we have that
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
and
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-surjective, which proves
(3)
.

Finally suppose
(3)
.
If
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
in
G_{m}
, then there are
n^{\prime}>n\geq m
such that
\sqsupset^{n}_{n^{\prime}}
is edge-witnessing.
Since
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is edge-surjective, there are
h,h^{\prime}\in G_{m}
with
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}h\mathrel{\sqcap}h^{\prime}\sqsubset^{m}_{n}g^{\prime}
.
Since
\sqsupset^{n}_{n^{\prime}}
is edge-witnessing, there is
p\in G_{n^{\prime}}
with
g\geq h\geq p\leq h^{\prime}\leq g^{\prime}
, and so
g\wedge g^{\prime}
.
The other half of edge-faithfulness follows from
Lemma 4.14
.
∎

Corollary 4.16
.

An edge-surjective sequence in
\mathbf{B}
is edge-faithful if and only if it has an edge-witnessing subsequence.
∎

We want to make the point that co-bijective edge-faithful sequences are the right notion to reflect the topological properties of the spectrum.
This is demonstrated by the following proposition about metrizability as well as in
Proposition 4.23
about connectedness.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be an edge-faithful sequence in
\mathbf{B}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

The sequence
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a star-refining subsequence.

(2)

The poset
\mathbb{P}
is regular.

(3)

The spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is Hausdorff.

(4)

The spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is metrizable.

Proof.

First, notice that for second-countable compact spaces being Hausdorff and being metrizable coincide. Further, this is equivalent to
\mathbb{P}
being regular (as
\mathbb{P}
is a prime
\omega
-poset), by
Theorem 2.11
. We are left to show that
(1)
and
(2)
are equivalent. Notice that
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is star-refining if and only if
G_{n}\vartriangleleft_{G_{n}}G_{m}
, where
\vartriangleleft
is the star-below relation (see the end of
§​​ 2.2.1
).

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
is regular and let
m\in\omega
. By regularity, we know that
G_{m}
is
\vartriangleleft
-refined by another cap, and since levels are coinitial for refinement (
Proposition 2.10
) and by the compatibility properties of the relations
\vartriangleleft
and
\leq
(see (
•
‣
2.2.1
)), there is
n
such that
G_{n}\vartriangleleft G_{m}
. Since levels are finite and by (
2.1
), we can assume that there is
k>n
such that
G_{n}\vartriangleleft_{G_{k}}G_{m}
. Using again (
•
‣
2.2.1
), we have that
G_{k}\vartriangleleft_{G_{k}}G_{m}
, hence
\sqsupset_{k}^{m}
is star-refining.

Vice versa, suppose
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a subsequence of star-refining morphisms. As each cap is refined by a level, and
G_{n}\vartriangleleft_{G_{n}}G_{m}
for all indexes belonging to the appropriate subsequence, (
•
‣
2.2.1
) implies that every cap is
\vartriangleleft
-refined by a level, and therefore
\mathbb{P}
is regular.
∎

Before we continue, let us summarize the relationship between the properties of (sub)sequences of a sequence
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
, the induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, and its spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Recall that a topological space is said to be
perfect
if it has no isolated points.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
.
Then we have the following equivalences.

(1)

({\sqsupset}_{n}^{m})
is surjective
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathbb{P}
is atomless.

(2)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is co-injective
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathbb{P}
is predetermined and atomless.

For the following suppose that
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is in
\mathbf{B}
. Then

(3)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an anti-injective subsequence
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is perfect, and

(4)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is anti-injective
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathbb{P}
is branching
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathbb{P}
is cap-determined.

Let us additionally suppose that
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-faithful. Then

(5)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a star-refining subsequence
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathbb{P}
is regular
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is Hausdorff
\,\Leftrightarrow\
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is metrizable.

Proof.

(1)
and
(5)
were already proved in Propositions
4.2
and
4.17
, respectively.
We now show
(2)
–
(4)
.

(2)
: If
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is co-injective, it is in particular surjective, so
\mathbb{P}
is atomless. Further, fix
p\in G_{n}
. As
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
is co-injective, there is
q\in G_{n+1}
such that
q^{\leq}\cap G_{n}=\{p\}
. This witnesses that
\mathbb{P}
is predetermined. Vice versa, notice that if each
\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}
is co-injective, then each
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is such. Fix
p\in G_{n}
. Since
\mathbb{P}
is atomless,
p
is not minimal. Since
\mathbb{P}
is predetermined, there is
q\in\mathbb{P}
such that
q^{<}=p^{\leq}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}
is graded,
q
has rank
n+1
, and so
q\in G_{n+1}
and
q^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}=\{p\}
. This shows that
\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}
is co-injective.

(3)
:
For
\Rightarrow
, let
p^{\in}
for
p\in G_{l}
be a basic open subset of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an anti-injective subsequence, there are
n>m\geq l
such that
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is anti-injective, and so there are
q\neq r\in G_{n}
with
q,r<p
.
Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-injective, as in the proof of
Lemma 4.14
, there are threads
(q_{i})
and
(r_{i})
with
q_{n}=q
and
r_{n}=r
such that
(q_{i})\cap G_{k}=\{q_{k}\}
and
(r_{i})\cap G_{k}=\{r_{k}\}
for
k\geq n
, and so
(q_{i})^{\leq}\neq(r_{i})^{\leq}
are minimal selectors in
p^{\in}
.

For
\Leftarrow
, first note that to obtain an anti-injective subsequence it is enough that for all
p\in\mathbb{P}
there are
q,r<p
with
q\nleq r
and
r\nleq q
:
If
q,r<p
are incomparable with
p\in G_{m}
,
q\in G_{i}
, and
r\in G_{j}
for
i,j>m
, then by co-injectivity for every
n\leq i,j
there are
q^{\prime},r^{\prime}\in G_{n}
such that
q^{\prime}{}^{\sqsubset^{i}_{n}}=\{q\}
and
r^{\prime}{}^{\sqsubset^{j}_{n}}=\{r\}
, and so we can find
n>m
that works for every
p\in G_{m}
.
Now if
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is perfect, then every
p\in\mathbb{P}
has incomparable predecessors.
Otherwise, the set of
\leq
-predecessors of
p
is linearly ordered. Then
p^{\leq}\cup p^{\geq}
is the only minimal selector containing
p
, and therefore
p^{\in}
is a singleton.

(4)
: Since
(2)
holds,
\mathbb{P}
is predetermined. As
\mathbb{P}
is an
\omega
-poset, being branching and being cap-determined coincide (see Figure
1
).
For
\Rightarrow
, fix
p<q
, and let
n>m
such that
p\in G_{n}
and
q\in G_{m}
. Since
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is anti-injective, we can find
r\in G_{n}\setminus\{p\}
with
r<q
. As
p
and
r
are not comparable,
\mathbb{P}
is branching.
For
\Leftarrow
, let
q\in G_{m}
, and
p\in G_{m+1}
with
p<q
(such
p
exists by
(2)
). Since
\mathbb{P}
is branching, we can find
r<q
which is incomparable with
p
. Let
n
be such that
r\in G_{n}
. Since
{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}={\sqsupset_{m+1}^{m}}\circ{\sqsupset_{n}^{m+1}}
, there is
r^{\prime}\in G_{m+1}
such that
r\leq r^{\prime}\leq q
. As
r
and
p
are incomparable,
p\neq r^{\prime}
. Since
m
and
q
are arbitrary, each
\sqsupset_{m+1}^{m}
is anti-injective, and so each
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is.
∎

Recall that every level of the induced poset induces an open cover of the spectrum.
In the following proposition we summarize properties of these covers. For two covers
C
and
D
of a set
X
, we say that
C

consolidates

D
when
D
refines
C
and, moreover, every
c\in C
is a union of elements of
D
, i.e.

\forall c\in C\qquad(c=\bigcup\{d\in D:d\subseteq c\}).

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
, let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset, and let
C_{n}=\{g^{\in}:g\in G_{n}\}
for every
n\in\omega
.
Then we have the following.

(1)

Every
C_{n}
is an open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.

(2)

Every open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is refined by some
C_{n}
.

(3)

Every
C_{n}
consolidates
C_{n+1}
.

For the following suppose that
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is in
\mathbf{B}
.

(4)

Every
C_{n}
is a minimal cover and
g\mapsto g^{\in}
is a bijection
G_{n}\to C_{n}
.

(5)

We have
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}h
if and only if
g^{\in}\supseteq h^{\in}
for every
m<n
,
g\in G_{m}
,
h\in G_{n}
.

(6)

If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-faithful, then
g\mapsto g^{\in}
is an isomorphism of graphs when
C_{n}
is endowed with the actual overlap relation.

(7)

If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is anti-injective, then
p\mapsto p^{\in}
is an isomorphism of the posets
(\mathbb{P},{\leq})\to(\{p^{\in}:p\in\mathbb{P}\},\subseteq)
.

Proof.

Most items are just a translation of
Theorem 2.7
and
Proposition 2.10
to the language of sequences of graphs.
By
Proposition 4.2
, the levels
\mathbb{P}_{n}
correspond to the graphs
G_{n}
.
By
Proposition 4.18
, if
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective (and anti-injective), then
\mathbb{P}
is predetermined (and cap-determined).

To show
(3)
let
p\in G_{n}
and
S\in p^{\in}
.
By
Proposition 4.10
, we have
[\{S\}]_{n}=[\{S\}]_{n+1}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}
, and so there is
q\in G_{n+1}
with
q\leq p
and
S\in q^{\in}
.
Hence,
p^{\in}=\bigcup\{q^{\in}:p\geq q\in G_{n+1}\}
.

To show
(5)
, let
h\in G_{n}
.
By co-injectivity there is a thread
(h_{k})_{k\geq n}
such that
h_{n}=h
and
h_{k+1}{}^{\sqsubset^{k}_{k+1}}=\{h_{k}\}
for every
k\geq n
. Then
S=(h_{k})_{k\geq n}\cup h^{\leq}
is a minimal selector, and for every
m\leq n
and
g\in G_{m}
such that
g^{\in}\supseteq h^{\in}
we have
g\in S
, and so
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}h
.
The other implication is trivial.
∎

Let us conclude the subsection by showing that suitable compact spaces admit faithful representations.

Proposition 4.20
.

Every non-empty second-countable compact
\mathsf{T}_{1}
space
X
is the spectrum of an edge-witnessing sequence in
\mathbf{B}
.

Moreover, if
X
is perfect or Hausdorff, we can obtain a sequence that is additionally anti-injective or star-surjective, respectively.

Proof.

By
Proposition 2.4
,
X
is the spectrum of a non-empty predetermined cap-determined graded
\omega
-poset
\mathbb{P}
.
Let
\mathbb{Q}\supseteq\mathbb{P}
be the modification of
\mathbb{P}
where below every atom
p\in\mathbb{P}
we add a decreasing sequence of new elements:
p=q_{p,0}>q_{p,1}>q_{p,2}>\cdots
.
Then
\mathbb{Q}
is still a predetermined graded
\omega
-poset, which is additionally infinite and atomless, and so it corresponds to a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
of finite sets and surjective co-surjective relations by (
4
).
If we put
g\sqcap h\Leftrightarrow g\wedge h
for every
g,h\in G_{n}
and
n\in\omega
,
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
becomes an edge-faithful surjective sequence in
\mathbf{S}
.

The spectrum of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is homeomorphic to
X
since its induced poset is
\mathbb{Q}
and the spectra of
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{P}
are canonically homeomorphic: a minimal selector in
\mathbb{Q}
is either contained in
\mathbb{P}
, or it is of the form
p^{\leq}\cup\{q_{p,i}:i\in\omega\}
for an atom
p\in\mathbb{P}
.

Finally, we use Propositions
4.15
and
4.18
.
Since
\mathbb{Q}
is predetermined,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is in
\mathbf{B}
.
Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-faithful, it has an edge-witnessing subsequence.
If
X
is perfect,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an anti-injective subsequence, and if
X
is Hausdorff,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining subsequence.
By
Proposition 4.5
, passing to a subsequence does not change the spectrum.
∎

In the case of metric compacta we describe a particular construction of a faithful representation that will be useful when we want to have control over the shapes of the graphs
G_{n}
.

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
X
be a non-empty compact metric space and let
(G_{n})
be a sequence of minimal open covers of
X
such that

(1)

G_{n}
consolidates
G_{n+1}
for every
n\in\omega
,

(2)

G_{n}\cap G_{n+1}=\emptyset
for every
n\in\omega
,

(3)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\max\{\operatorname{diam}(g):g\in G_{n}\}=0
, or equivalently every open cover of
X
is refined by some
G_{n}
.

Endow every
G_{n}
with the edge relation
g\sqcap g^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow g\cap g^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, and let, for
m\leq n
,
{\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\subseteq G_{m}\times G_{n}
be the restriction of the inclusion relation on
\mathsf{P}(X)
. Let
\mathbb{P}
denote the poset
\bigcup_{n\in\omega}G_{n}
with inclusion as the order relation.

Then
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is an edge-faithful sequence in
\mathbf{B}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{P}
is a cap-basis of
X
, and hence
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
Moreover, the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is anti-injective and has an edge-witnessing and star-refining subsequence.

Proof.

By
(3)
,
\mathbb{P}
is a basis of
X
.
By
[
BBV25
, Lemma 1.32]
,
\mathbb{P}
is a predetermined graded
\omega
-poset with
\mathbb{P}_{n}=G_{n}
for every
n
, and it is a cap-basis of
X
.
By
[
BBV25
, Proposition 2.9]
,
X
is homeomorphic to
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
By
(2)
, we have even
\mathbb{P}_{n}=\mathsf{r}^{-1}(\{n\})=G_{n}
, and so (also using that
\mathbb{P}
is graded)
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a well-defined sequence of co-surjective relations, and
\mathbb{P}
is its induced poset.
Since the levels of the graded
\omega
-poset
\mathbb{P}
are disjoint and consolidating,
\mathbb{P}
is atomless and branching.
Also note that the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is by definition edge-faithful.
Hence, by
Proposition 4.18
, the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective, anti-injective, and has a star-refining subsequence, and by
Proposition 4.15
, it is edge-preserving, edge-surjective, and has an edge-witnessing subsequence.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

If
X
is perfect then, even if
(2)
fails, we can always revert to a subsequence if necessary to make
(2)
hold. Indeed, in this case
(3)
implies that, for every
m
, we have
n\geq m
such that
\operatorname{diam}(g)<\operatorname{diam}(h)
for every
g\in G_{n}
and
h\in G_{m}
and hence
G_{m}\cap G_{n}=\emptyset
.

4.4.
Connectedness

Next we focus on characterizing when the spectrum or its subsets are connected.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be an edge-faithful sequence in
\mathbf{B}
, and let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
Then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected if and only if every graph
G_{n}
is connected.

Proof.

By
Proposition 2.10
, every open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is refined by
\{p^{\in}:p\in G_{n}\}
for some
n
, and for every
p,q\in G_{n}
we have
p\wedge q
if and only if
p^{\in}\cap q^{\in}\neq\emptyset
.
In particular,
p^{\in}\neq\emptyset
for every
p\in G_{n}
.
It follows that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected if and only if no
G_{n}
admits a partition
G_{n}=A\cup B
such that
(\bigcup_{a\in A}a^{\in})\cap(\bigcup_{b\in B}b^{\in})=\emptyset
, i.e. if and only if every
G_{n}
with
\wedge\restriction G_{n}\times G_{n}
as the edge relation is a connected graph.
The conclusion follows from edge-faithfulness.
∎

It turns out that for acyclic graphs, edge-faithfulness does not have to be assumed a priori.

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{B}
and suppose each
G_{n}
is acyclic. Let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
Then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected if and only if every graph
G_{n}
is connected and
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an edge-witnessing subsequence.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected.
Then the graphs
G_{n}
are connected by
Lemma 4.14
and the proof
Proposition 4.23
, or alternatively by
Proposition 4.25
.
If for every
n
and every adjacent
g,h\in G_{n}
we have
g\wedge h
, then we can construct an edge-witnessing subsequence as in the proof of
Proposition 4.15
.
Otherwise, there are adjacent
g,h\in G_{n}
such that
g^{\geq}\cap h^{\geq}=\emptyset
.
As
G_{n}
is a tree, we may consider the connected components
G\ni g
and
H\ni h
of
G_{n}
with the edge
(g,h)
removed.
Then
G^{\geq}\cap H^{\geq}=\emptyset
, and the the graph
(G_{n},\wedge)
is disconnected, and so
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is disconnected as in the proof of
Proposition 4.23
.

Suppose that the graphs
G_{n}
are connected and that
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an edge-witnessing subsequence.
The sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-surjective by
Proposition 3.8
, edge-faithful by
Proposition 4.15
, and so
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected by
Proposition 4.23
.
∎

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, and let
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
We consider the following conditions:

(1)

A
is connected,

(2)

[A]_{n}
is connected for every
n\in\omega
,

(3)

[A]_{n}^{\sqcap}
is connected for every
n\in\omega
.

We have
(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)

\Rightarrow

(3)
.
If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective and edge-faithful,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is Hausdorff, and
A\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is closed, then
(1)

\Leftrightarrow

(2)

\Leftrightarrow

(3)
.

Proof.

(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
:
Suppose
[A]_{n}
is disconnected, i.e.
[A]_{n}=H_{1}\cup H_{2}
for some non-empty subgraphs
H_{1},H_{2}\subseteq G_{n}
with no edge between them.
Let
U_{i}=\bigcup\{h^{\in}:h\in H_{i}\}
be the induced open subsets of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.
We have
A\subseteq U_{1}\cup U_{2}
.
As
H_{i}\neq\emptyset
, we have
A\cap U_{i}\neq\emptyset
.
As there is no edge between
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, we have
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\emptyset
by
Lemma 4.14
.
This shows that
A
is disconnected.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(3)
is clear as
C^{\sqcap}
is connected for every connected subgraph
C\subseteq G_{n}
.

Next suppose that
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective and edge-faithful, so we have the equivalences in
Lemma 4.14
, that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is Hausdorff and so can be endowed with a compatible metric, and that
A
is closed.
To show
(3)

\Rightarrow

(1)
we suppose that
A
is disconnected, i.e. there are non-empty disjoint closed subsets
B,C\subseteq\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
such that
A=B\cup C
.
As
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a Hausdorff compactum, there are open sets
U_{0}\supseteq B
and
U_{3}\supseteq C
with disjoint closures.
Let us put
U_{1}=\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}\setminus(B\cup\overline{U_{3}})
and
U_{2}=\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}\setminus(\overline{U_{0}}\cup C)
.
Then,
\{U_{i}:i<4\}
is an open cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
that is a chain, i.e.
U_{i}\cap U_{j}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
|i-j|\leq 1
.
Let
n\in\omega
be such that the cover
\{g^{\in}:g\in G_{n}\}
refines
\{U_{i}:i<4\}
.
Then
[B]_{n}^{\sqcap}\cap[C]_{n}^{\sqcap}=\emptyset
.
Otherwise, there are
[B]_{n}\ni b\sqcap g\sqcap c\in[C]_{n}
in
G_{n}
, and so
b^{\in}\subseteq U_{0}
and
c^{\in}\subseteq U_{3}
, but also
b^{\in}\cap g^{\in}\neq\emptyset\neq c^{\in}\cap g^{\in}
by
Lemma 4.14
.
Then
g^{\in}
cannot refine any member of the chain
\{U_{i}:i<4\}
.
Together with the facts that
[A]_{n}^{\sqcap}=[B]_{n}^{\sqcap}\cup[C]_{n}^{\sqcap}
and that
[B]_{n}\neq\emptyset\neq[C]_{n}
, we have that
[A]_{n}^{\sqcap}
is disconnected.
∎

Recall that by a
continuum
we mean a non-empty compact connected metrizable space.
A continuum is
locally connected
or
Peano
if it has an open basis consisting of connected sets, or equivalently if it is a continuous image of the unit interval, by the Hahn–Mazurkiwicz theorem (see
[
Nad92
, VIII]
).
A continuum
X
is
tree-like
if every open cover of
X
can be refined by a finite open cover whose overlap graph is a tree.
A continuum
X
is
hereditarily unicoherent
if for every two subcontinua
A,B\subseteq X
we have that
A\cap B
is connected.
Every tree-like continuum is hereditarily unicoherent (see
[
Nad92
, Theorem 12.2 and page 232]
) and clearly a circle cannot be embedded into a hereditarily unicoherent continuum.
For Peano continua, these conditions are equivalent and define the notion of a
dendrite
(see
[
Nad92
, X]
).

Proposition 4.26
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be an edge-faithful sequence in
\mathbf{B}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
and suppose that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is Hausdorff.
If the relations
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
are monotone, then
p^{\in}
is connected for every
p\in\mathbb{P}
, and so
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is locally connected.

Proof.

Let
m\in\omega
be such that
p\in G_{m}
, and let
A=p^{\in}
.
For every
n\geq m
let
C_{n}=p^{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}
, which is a connected subset of
G_{n}
since
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is monotone.
We will show that
C_{n}^{\sqcap}=[A]_{n}
for every
n\geq m
.
It will follow that
p^{\in}=A
is connected by
Proposition 4.25
, and so
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is locally connected as sets
p^{\in}
form a basis.

Let
g\in G_{n}
.
We have
g\in[A]_{n}
if and only if
g^{\in}\cap p^{\in}\neq\emptyset
.
By
Proposition 4.19
we have
p^{\in}=\bigcup\{c^{\in}:c\in C_{n}\}
, and so
g\in[A]_{n}
if and only if for some
c\in C_{n}
we have
g^{\in}\cap c^{\in}\neq\emptyset
.
The latter is equivalent to
g\mathrel{\sqcap}c
by
Lemma 4.14
.
Hence,
[A]_{n}=C_{n}^{\sqcap}
.
∎

By combining the previous observations we obtain the following corollary as an application.

Corollary 4.27
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{B}
such that every
G_{n}
is a tree, and let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected and Hausdorff.

(2)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an edge-witnessing and a star-refining subsequence.

If the conditions hold, then
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-faithful and
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a tree-like continuum.
If moreover
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is monotone, then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a dendrite.

Proof.

By
Proposition 3.8
, the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-surjective.
By
Proposition 4.24
, if
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected, then
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an edge-witnessing subsequence.
Hence,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-faithful on both sides of the equivalence, and the equivalence follows from Propositions
4.23
and
4.17
.

Assuming the equivalent conditions,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is clearly a continuum, and it is tree-like as every open cover is refined by
\{p^{\in}:p\in G_{n}\}
for some
n
.
If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is monotone, then, by
Proposition 4.26
,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is also locally connected and so a dendrite.
∎

4.5.
Fraïssé sequences

We are particularly interested in sequences which are ‘as complicated as possible’, in a sense capturing a whole given subcategory of graphs.
In particular, such sequences absorb all morphisms in the following sense.

Definition 4.28
.

A sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
in a subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{G}
is
(
lax-
)
absorbing
if, whenever
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m}}_{G}
, there are
n\geq m
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{G_{n}}
with
{\dashv\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}={\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}
(
{\dashv\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\subseteq{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}
).
More symbolically, these notions are defined as follows.

(Absorbing)

\displaystyle{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m}}_{G}\qquad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\text{ exists }{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{G_{n}}\ ({\dashv\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}={\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}),

(lax-Absorbing)

\displaystyle\dashv\ \in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m}}_{G}\qquad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\text{ exists }{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{G_{n}}\ ({\dashv\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\subseteq{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}).

A
(
lax-
)
Fraïssé
sequence is a (lax-)absorbing sequence that is also
cofinal
, meaning that for every object
G
in
\mathbf{C}
there is a morphism (in
\mathbf{C}
) onto
G
from a member of the sequence, i.e.

G\in\mathbf{C}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\text{exists }{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{G_{n}}.

Fraïssé sequences play a key role in construction of universal homogeneous objects, see
[
Kub14
]
.
Usually, the cofinality does not have to be verified as it is automatic if the category
\mathbf{C}
is
directed
in that all objects
E,F\in\mathbf{C}
are the codomains of morphisms with a common domain.

The proof of the following is immediate and left as an exercise.

Proposition 4.29
.

In a directed subcategory
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
, every lax-absorbing sequence
{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m}}_{G_{n}}
is cofinal and so lax-Fraïssé.∎

Of course, not all directed subcategories
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{G}
have (lax-)Fraïssé sequences. The key property for
\mathbf{C}
to have here is
amalgamation
, which means that, for any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}_{H}^{G}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{C}_{I}^{G}
, we have
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}_{J}^{H}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}_{J}^{I}
such that
{\sqsupset\circ\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
, i.e.

{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}_{H}^{G},{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{C}_{I}^{G}\ \Rightarrow\ \text{exist }{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}_{J}^{H},{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}_{J}^{I}\ ({\sqsupset\circ\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}).

Note that if
\mathbf{C}
has amalgamation as well as a
weakly terminal object

T
(i.e. such that
\mathbf{C}^{T}_{G}\neq\emptyset
, for all
G\in\mathbf{C}
) then it is automatically directed.

In what follows we will use the definition of wide subcategories and ideals, as given in
Definition 3.1
.

Proposition 4.30
.

A subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{G}
has a Fraïssé sequence whenever
\mathbf{C}
has amalgamation and is directed.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Kub14
, Theorem 3.7]
as soon as we note that
\mathbf{G}
and hence
\mathbf{C}
is an essentially countable category – the full subcategory of graphs whose vertices lie in some fixed countable set like
\omega
forms a countable essentially wide (i.e. wide once we include compositions with isomorphisms) subcategory of
\mathbf{G}
. In particular,
\mathbf{C}
has a countable dominating family.
∎

It is easy to see that a subsequence of a (lax-)Fraïssé sequence is still (lax-)Fraïssé.
Moreover, we can construct subsequences in wide ideals.

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{G}
be a subcategory and let
I\subseteq\mathbf{C}
be an ideal.

(1)

If
I
is wide, then every Fraïssé sequence has a subsequence in
I
.

(2)

If
I
is wide and lax-closed, then every lax-Fraïssé sequence has a subsequence in
I
.

(3)

If a single cofinal sequence has a subsequence in
I
, then
I
is wide.

Proof.

Take any (lax-)Fraïssé sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
in
\mathbf{C}
.
Let
k\in\omega
be arbitrary.
As
I
is wide, we have
{\dashv}\in I\cap\mathbf{C}^{G_{k}}_{H}
, for some
H\in\mathbf{C}
.
As
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is (lax-)Fraïssé, we have
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{H}_{G_{l}}
, for some
l>k
, such that
{\sqsupset^{k}_{l}}={\dashv}\circ{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
(or
{\sqsupset^{k}_{l}}\supseteq{\dashv}\circ{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
).
As
I
is a (lax-closed) ideal,
{\sqsupset^{k}_{l}}\in I
.
Finally we obtain a subsequence in
I
by
Lemma 4.6
.

On the other hand, if a cofinal sequence has a subsequence
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
in
I
, the subsequence is cofinal as well.
Then for every
K\in\mathbf{C}
there is
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}^{K}_{H_{m}}
for some
m
, and
{\dashv}\circ{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{m+1}}\in I
.
∎

We have seen in
§​​ 4.3
that edge-faithful sequences in
\mathbf{B}
nicely represent their spectra.
For this reason and also in order to avoid trivialities, we shall consider categories of co-bijective morphisms.
While a subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{B}
may have many different (lax-)Fraïssé sequences, the key point for our work is that they all have homeomorphic spectra.

Proposition 4.32
.

Assume
\mathbf{C}
is a subcategory of
\mathbf{B}
, and let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
and
(H_{n},{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m}})
be sequences in
\mathbf{C}
, with induced posets
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, respectively.
Suppose one of the following.

(1)

The sequences
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
and
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
are lax-Fraïssé, and they have edge-witnessing star-refining subsequences.

(2)

The sequences
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
and
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
are Fraïssé.

Then the spectra
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
and
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
are homeomorphic.

Proof.

Let us prove the lax-Fraïssé case.
First, we can assume that the two lax-Fraïssé sequences of interest are edge-witnessing and star-refining, as the spectrum given a sequence and the one given by a subsequence are homeomorphic (
Proposition 4.5
).

As
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is cofinal, we can take any
m_{0}\in\omega
and find
{\dashv_{0}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m_{0}}}_{H_{n_{0}}}
.
As
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is lax-absorbing, we have
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{0}}\in\mathbf{C}^{H_{n_{0}}}_{G_{m_{1}}}
with
{\dashv_{0}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{0}}\subseteq{\sqsupset^{m_{0}}_{m_{1}}}
.
As
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
is lax-absorbing, we have
{\dashv_{1}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m_{1}}}_{H_{n_{1}}}
with
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{0}\circ\dashv_{1}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{n_{0}}_{n_{1}}}
.
Continuing in this way, we obtain
{\dashv_{k}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G_{m_{k}}}_{H_{n_{k}}}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}}\in\mathbf{C}^{H_{n_{k}}}_{G_{m_{k+1}}}
such that
{\dashv_{k}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}}\subseteq{\sqsupset^{m_{k}}_{m_{k+1}}}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}\circ\dashv_{k+1}}\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{n_{k}}_{n_{k+1}}}
, for all
k\in\omega
.
As the morphisms
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m}
are edge-witnessing and therefore edge-surjective (see
Figure 2
),
\wedge
and
\sqcap
agree on levels of
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
respectively, by
Proposition 4.15
.
Hence the morphisms
\dashv_{k}
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}
are
\wedge
-preserving.
Since a subrelation of a
\wedge
-preserving relation is again
\wedge
-preserving, each
\dashv_{k}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}
is
\wedge
-preserving. Likewise, each
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}\circ\dashv_{k+1}
is
\wedge
-preserving.
As the morphisms
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m}
are star-refining, the induced posets are regular (see
Proposition 4.18
).
The result now follows immediately from
[
BBV25
, Proposition 3.11]
.

To prove the Fraïssé case, we construct the morphisms
\dashv_{k}
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}
analogously, now satisfying
{\dashv_{k}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}}={\sqsupset^{m_{k}}_{m_{k+1}}}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}\circ\dashv_{k+1}}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{n_{k}}_{n_{k+1}}}
, for all
k\in\omega
.
The result follows from
Proposition 4.5
applied multiple times:
(\dashv_{k}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k})
is both a subsequence of
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
and of the intertwining sequence
(\dashv_{0},\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{0},\dashv_{1},\ldots)
, and analogously for
(\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{k}\circ\dashv_{k+1})
.
∎

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{B}
be a subcategory with amalgamation such that edge-witnessing morphisms are wide.
Then all morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
are edge-surjective.

Proof.

Let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{H}
and let
g\sqcap g^{\prime}\in G
.
There is an edge-witnessing morphism
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{I}
and a vertex
i\in I
with
g,g^{\prime}\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}i
.
By the amalgamation property, there are
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{C}^{H}_{J}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{I}_{J}
with
{\sqsupset}\circ{\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
.
Hence, there are vertices
j\in J
and
h,h^{\prime}\in H
such that
i\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}j
and
g\sqsupset h\dashv j
and
g^{\prime}\sqsupset h^{\prime}\dashv j
.
We have
h\sqcap h^{\prime}
since
\dashv
is edge-preserving, and we are done.
∎

The following theorem summarizes a typical situation. (Recall, the category
\mathbf{E}
is that of edge-surjective morphisms.)

Theorem 4.34
.

Let
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{B}
be a directed subcategory with amalgamation such that edge-witnessing morphisms and star-refining morphisms are wide. Then:

(1)

There is a Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{C}
.

(2)

We have
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
, and edge-witnessing and star-refining morphisms form wide ideals.

(3)

Every lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{C}
is edge-faithful and has an edge-witnessing and a star-refining subsequence.

(4)

All lax-Fraïssé sequences in
\mathbf{C}
have homeomorphic spectra.

(5)

The spectrum is metrizable.

Proof.

(2)
follows from
Lemma 4.33
and
Proposition 3.4
.
Every lax-Fraïssé sequence has an edge-witnessing and star-refining subsequence by
Lemma 3.3
and
Proposition 4.31
.
It follows from
Proposition 4.15
that it is also edge-faithful.
That proves
(3)
.
Then
(4)
follows from
Proposition 4.32
, and
(5)
follows from
Proposition 4.17
.
∎

The above theorem calls for the following abuse of terminology:

Definition 4.35
.

Let
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{B}
be a directed subcategory with amalgamation such that edge-witnessing morphisms and star-refining morphisms are wide (and so form wide ideals as also
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{E})
.
The unique spectrum of a lax-Fraïssé sequence is called the
Fraïssé limit
of
\mathbf{C}
.

4.6.
The clique functor

To prove that a subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{G}
has amalgamation it will often suffice to consider morphisms that are functions. Indeed, this will be the case whenever
\mathbf{C}
is closed under the clique functor which we now describe. First let us denote the cliques (i.e. non-empty complete subgraphs) of a graph
(G,\sqcap)
by

\mathsf{X}G=\{C\in\mathsf{P}(G)\setminus\{\emptyset\}:C\times C\subseteq\sqcap\}.

In particular, cliques of triangle-free graphs are really just edges, including loops.

The
clique graph
has vertices
\mathsf{X}G
and edges given by containment, i.e. cliques are related in
\mathsf{X}G
if and only if one is contained in the other.

Let
\mathbf{F}
denote the wide subcategory
\mathbf{G}
consisting of all morphisms which are functions. Recall that
\mathbf{S}
is the wide subcategory of
\mathbf{G}
with co-surjective morphisms.

Proposition 4.36
.

We have a functor
\mathsf{X}:\mathbf{S}\rightarrow\mathbf{F}
taking a graph
G
to its clique graph
\mathsf{X}G
and any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{G}^{H}
to the function
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{F}_{\mathsf{X}G}^{\mathsf{X}H}
given by

\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(C)=C^{\sqsubset}.

Moreover, if
H
is triangle-free and
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}_{G}^{H}\cap\mathbf{E}_{G}^{H}
then
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}_{\mathsf{X}G}^{\mathsf{X}H}
too.

Proof.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{G}^{H}
and
C
is a clique of
G
then
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(C)=C^{\sqsubset}
is a clique of
H
, as
\sqsupset
is co-surjective and preserves the edge relation. Moreover, if
D
is another clique of
G
then
C\subseteq D
certainly implies
C^{\sqsubset}\subseteq D^{\sqsubset}
, hence if
C
and
D
are adjacent so are
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(C)
and
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(D)
, i.e.
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
is in
\mathbf{F}_{\mathsf{X}G}^{\mathsf{X}H}
. For any other morphism
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{S}^{F}_{H}
, we immediately see that

\mathsf{X}^{{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset}}(C)=C^{{\sqsubset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}}=C^{\sqsubset{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}}=\mathsf{X}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}(\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(C))=\mathsf{X}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(C).

Thus
\mathsf{X}^{{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset}}=\mathsf{X}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
, showing that
\mathsf{X}
is a functor.

For the last statement, take any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}_{G}^{H}\cap\mathbf{E}_{G}^{H}
and any clique
D\in\mathsf{X}H
. If
D=\{h\}
then the co-injectivity of
\sqsupset
yields
g\in G
with
g^{\sqsubset}=\{h\}
and hence
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(\{g\})=\{h\}=D
. On the other hand, if
D=\{h,h^{\prime}\}
, for distinct
h,h^{\prime}\in H
, then we can take
g,g^{\prime}\in G
with
h\sqsupset g\sqcap g^{\prime}\sqsubset h^{\prime}
, as
\sqsupset
is edge-surjective, and hence
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(\{g,g^{\prime}\})\supseteq\{h,h^{\prime}\}=D
. If
D
is triangle-free then this implies
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(\{g,g^{\prime}\})=D
, showing that
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
is surjective and hence
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}^{\mathsf{X}H}_{\mathsf{X}G}
.
∎

Proposition 4.37
.

The restriction
\in_{G}
of the relation
\in
to
G\times\mathsf{X}G
, for each
G\in\mathbf{G}
, yields a natural transformation
2
2

2

Recall that a natural transformation between functors
F,G\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C^{\prime}}
is a family of morphisms
(\eta_{C}\colon F(C)\to G(C))_{C\in\mathbf{C}}
such that
G(f)\circ\eta_{C}=\eta_{D}\circ F(f)
for every
\mathbf{C}
-morphism
f\colon C\to D
.

from the clique functor
\mathsf{X}
to the identity functor
\mathsf{I}
.

Proof.

Say we have adjacent
C,D\in\mathsf{X}G
and let
c\in C
and
d\in D
. If
C\subseteq D
then
c\in D
, while if
D\subseteq C
then
d\in C
. In either case,
c\sqcap d
, as
C
and
D
are cliques, showing that
\in_{G}
preserves the edge relation of
\mathsf{X}G
and is thus a morphism in
\mathbf{S}^{G}_{\mathsf{X}G}
. Now just note that
{\sqsupset\circ\in_{H}}={\in_{G}\circ\ \mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}}
, for any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{G}^{H}
, as

d\mathrel{(\sqsupset\circ\in_{H})}C\ \Leftrightarrow\ \exists c\ (d\sqsupset c\in C)\ \Leftrightarrow\ d\in C^{\sqsubset}=\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(C)\ \Leftrightarrow\ d\mathrel{({\in_{G}}\circ{\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}})}C.

Thus
(\in_{G})_{G\in\mathbf{G}}
is a natural transformation from
\mathsf{X}
to
\mathsf{I}
.
∎

Definition 4.38
.

We call a subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of
\mathbf{S}

clique-closed
if
{\in_{G}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathsf{X}G}
(in particular
\mathsf{X}G\in\mathbf{C}
), for all
G\in\mathbf{C}
, and
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}_{\mathsf{X}G}^{\mathsf{X}H}
, whenever
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}_{G}^{H}
.

In other words,
\mathbf{C}
is clique-closed if
\mathsf{X}
restricts to a functor on
\mathbf{C}
and
(\ni_{G})
restricts to a natural transformation between the restricted endofunctors
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{I}
.

Given categories
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C^{\prime}}
, we let
\mathbf{C}\cap\mathbf{C^{\prime}}
be the category of all objects and morphisms that are both in
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C^{\prime}}
.

Corollary 4.39
.

If
\mathbf{C}
is clique-closed and
\mathbf{C}\cap\mathbf{F}
has amalgamation, so does
\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

Take
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{H}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{C}_{I}^{G}
. If
\mathbf{C}
is clique-closed and
\mathbf{C}\cap\mathbf{F}
has amalgamation then, in particular, we can amalgamate
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
and
\mathsf{X}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
, i.e. we have functions
\phi\in\mathbf{C}_{J}^{\mathsf{X}H}
and
\pi\in\mathbf{C}_{J}^{\mathsf{X}I}
with
{\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\circ\phi}={\mathsf{X}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ\pi}
so

{\sqsupset}\circ{\in_{H}}\circ\phi\ \ =\ \ {\in_{G}}\circ\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\circ\phi\ \ =\ \ {\in_{G}}\circ\mathsf{X}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ\pi\ \ =\ \ \mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\in_{I}\circ\ \pi.

Thus
{\in_{H}}\circ\phi
and
{\in_{I}}\circ\ \pi
amalgamate
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
, i.e.
\mathbf{C}
has amalgamation.
∎

Before moving on, let us note some more basic properties of
\in_{G}
, for any
G\in\mathbf{G}
.

Proposition 4.40
.

\in_{G}
is always co-bijective, edge-witnessing and monotone.

Proof.

Co-surjectivity of
\in_{G}
follows from the fact that cliques are, by definition, non-empty, while co-injectivity follows from the fact that every vertex
g
is the unique element of the singleton clique
\{g\}
. For any
g,h\in G
with
g\sqcap h
, we see that
g,h\in_{G}\{g,h\}\in\mathsf{X}G
, showing that
\in_{G}
is also edge-witnessing. In particular,
\in_{G}
is edge-surjective so to verify monotonicity it suffices to consider single vertices
g\in G
(
Proposition 3.10
). But any clique
C\in\mathsf{X}G
containing
g
is related to the singleton clique
\{g\}
, so preimages of single vertices are indeed connected and hence
\in_{G}
is monotone.
∎

Corollary 4.41
.

If a graph
G
is connected then so is its clique graph
\mathsf{X}G
.

Proof.

As
\in_{G}
is co-surjective,
G^{\in_{G}}=\mathsf{X}G
. If
G
is connected, this implies
\mathsf{X}G
is also connected, as
\in_{G}
is also monotone.
∎

By showing that our category is clique-closed, besides reducing amalgamation to functions, we get that edge-witnessing morphisms are wide for free.

Corollary 4.42
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a clique-closed category.
Then edge-witnessing morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{C}
.

Proof.

For every
G\in\mathbf{C}
,
{\in_{G}}\in\mathbf{C}^{G}_{\mathsf{X}G}
is edge-witnessing, by
Proposition 4.40
.
∎

Let us conclude with an example of how to obtain a clique-closed category. Recall, a subcategory
\mathbf{C}
of a category
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
is full if
\mathbf{C}
contains some objects of
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, but all morphisms between them.

Corollary 4.43
.

Let
\mathbf{T}\subseteq\mathbf{B}
be the full subcategory of all trees.

\mathbf{T}
as well as any full
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{T}
such that
\mathsf{X}G\in\mathbf{C}
for every
G\in\mathbf{C}
is clique-closed.

Proof.

For every tree
T
, the cliques of
T
are exactly the singletons and edges, i.e.
\mathsf{X}T=\{\{g\}:g\in T\}\cup\{\{g,h\}:g\sim h\in T\}
.
It is easy to see that
\mathsf{X}T
is the tree constructed from
T
by replacing every edge
g\sim h
by
\{g\}\sim\{g,h\}\sim\{h\}
, similarly to
Lemma 3.12
.
Hence,
\mathbf{T}
itself satisfy the assumptions on
\mathbf{C}
.

By
Proposition 3.8
we have
\mathbf{C}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
, and so by the last part of
Proposition 4.36
,
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{B}^{\mathsf{X}T}_{\mathsf{X}S}=\mathbf{C}^{\mathsf{X}T}_{\mathsf{X}S}
for every
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{C}^{T}_{S}
.
By
Proposition 4.40
,
{\in_{T}}\in\mathbf{C}^{T}_{\mathsf{X}T}
for every
T\in\mathbf{C}
.
Altogether,
\mathbf{C}
is clique-closed.
∎

5.
Examples

The general theory we have developed so far leads to the following procedure for constructing compacta from the desired overlap graphs of their finite open covers:

(1)

We define a category
\mathbf{C}
of graphs of desired shape and suitable relational morphisms.
As argued in the previous section, it is natural to consider subcategories of
\mathbf{B}\cap\mathbf{E}
, though sometimes this is not assumed a priori and follows from other assumptions (e.g. by
Lemma 4.33
).

(2)

We show that
\mathbf{C}
is suitable for our Fraïssé theory, i.e. is directed, has amalgamation and wide families of edge-witnessing and star-refining morphisms.
Then
Theorem 4.34
applies.

Every category
\mathbf{C}
we consider will have a terminal object, making directedness an immediate consequence of amalgamation.
We will often show that
\mathbf{C}
is clique-closed, and so it is enough to show amalgamation for functions (
Corollary 4.39
).
That edge-witnessing and star-surjective morphisms are wide often follows from the edge-splitting construction (
Lemma 3.12
) or from being clique-closed (
Corollary 4.42
).

(3)

If possible, we characterize lax-Fraïssé and Fraïssé sequences in
\mathbf{C}
.

By
Theorem 4.34
, every lax-Fraïssé sequence has an edge-witnessing and a star-refining subsequence.
Sometimes this is enough, but sometimes we find other (lax-closed) wide ideals
I
in
\mathbf{C}
, so every (lax-)Fraïssé sequence has also a subsequence in
I
by
Proposition 4.31
.
Also note that it is enough to prove lax-absorption by
Proposition 4.29
– cofinality follows from directedness.

(4)

We characterize the Fraïssé limit
X
(
Definition 4.35
), and possibly show that every sequence in
\mathbf{C}
with spectrum
X
is lax-Fraïssé.

To do so we translate between properties of a sequence in
\mathbf{C}
and topological properties of its spectrum (e.g. we use
Corollary 4.27
).
Either known properties of Fraïssé sequence will translate to a topological characterization of a particular space, or we will a priori have a particular space
X
in mind and show that every corresponding sequence is lax-Fraïssé.
In the latter case it is then enough to find any sequence in
\mathbf{C}
with spectrum
X
, e.g. with help of
Proposition 4.21
.

Below, we apply this reasoning to various quite simple categories of graphs and we obtain their Fraïssé limits, namely we obtain the Cantor space, the arc, the Cantor fan, and the Lelek fan.

5.1.
The Cantor space

Here we start with the simplest possible example, the full subcategory of
\mathbf{B}
whose objects are
discrete
graphs
D
, where the edge relation is just equality on
D
. We denote this subcategory by
\mathbf{D}
.

Note that any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{G}^{D}
is automatically a function on
G
when
D\in\mathbf{D}
– then
c,d\sqsupset g
implies
c\sqcap d
, by edge-preservation, and hence
c=d
, by discreteness. A function is co-injective precisely when it is surjective, so morphisms of
\mathbf{D}
are precisely surjective functions.

Recall that a
Stone space
is a
\mathsf{T}_{0}
compactum with a clopen basis (which is then
\mathsf{T}_{2}
).

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
(G_{n},{\sqsupset}_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{D}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
. Then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a Stone space.

Proof.

As noted above,
(G_{n})\subseteq\mathbf{D}
implies that each
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is a function and hence that
p^{\leq}\cap G_{m}
is a singleton, for each
p\in G_{n}
. It follows that each filter and, in particular, each minimal selector in
\mathbb{P}
contains at most one vertex from
G_{m}
, and so is a thread. The corresponding basic open sets
(p^{\in})_{p\in G_{m}}
are thus disjoint and hence form a clopen cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
. As
m
was arbitrary, it follows that
(p^{\in})_{p\in\mathbb{P}}
is a clopen basis of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
and hence
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a Stone space.
∎

Note that the singleton graph is a terminal object in
\mathbf{D}
, and that
\mathbf{D}
has pullbacks given by the usual fibre-product, so
\mathbf{D}
has amalgamation.

\mathbf{D}
is also trivially clique-closed as the clique functor is isomorphic to the identity functor.
Finally, note that every function from a discrete graph is trivially star-refining, while every function onto a discrete graph is trivially edge-witnessing.
Hence, we have a Fraïssé limit by
Theorem 4.34
.
The key extra ingredient for a sequence to be Fraïssé is anti-injectivity.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
(G_{n},{\sqsupset}_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{D}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{D}
;

(2)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{D}
;

(3)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an anti-injective subsequence;

(4)

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is homeomorphic to the Cantor space.

Proof.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2)
: Since all morphisms in the category of interest are total surjective functions if
{\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq{\dashv}
for
{\sqsupset},{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}},{\dashv}\in\mathbf{D}
, then we have equality. This shows that every lax-Fraïssé sequence is Fraïssé.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3)
: For every
G\in\mathbf{D}
we may consider the projection
\pi
of
H=G\times\{0,1\}
, considered as a discrete graph, onto
G
.
This is immediately seen to be anti-injective.
Hence, the anti-injective ideal is wide in
\mathbf{D}
, and every Fraïssé sequence has an anti-injective subsequence by
Proposition 4.31
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1)
: Take any
\pi\in\mathbf{D}^{G_{j}}_{E}
. If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an anti-injective subsequence then, in particular, we can find
k>j
such that
\sqsupset^{j}_{k}
-preimages always have more vertices than than
\pi
-preimages, i.e.
|d^{\sqsupset^{j}_{k}}|\geq|d^{\pi}|
, for all
d\in G_{j}
. For each
d\in G_{j}
, we thus have a map
\phi_{d}
from
d^{\sqsupset^{j}_{k}}
onto
d^{\pi}
. Putting these together, we obtain a map
\phi=\bigcup_{d\in D}\phi_{d}
from
G_{k}
onto
E
with
{\sqsupset^{j}_{k}}={\pi\circ\phi}
. This shows that
(\sqsupset^{j}_{k})
is indeed a Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{D}
.

(3)
\Leftrightarrow
(4)
: We already know that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is a Stone space, by
Proposition 5.1
. As
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is also second countable/metrizable, it will be homeomorphic to the Cantor space precisely when it is perfect. This is equivalent to
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
having an anti-injective subsequence, by
Proposition 4.18
(3)
.
∎

5.2.
The Arc

Let
\mathbf{P}
be the full subcategory of
\mathbf{B}
whose objects are paths, i.e.

\mathbf{P}=\{P\in\mathbf{G}:P\text{ is a path}\}.

The first thing to observe is that this subcategory is clique-closed. Recall that
\mathsf{E}G
is the set of ends (i.e. vertices of degree at most
1
) of a graph
G
.

Proposition 5.3
.

\mathbf{P}
is clique-closed.

Proof.

Any path
P
is of the form
\{p_{0}\sim p_{1}\sim\cdots\sim p_{n}\}
, and therefore

\mathsf{X}P=\{\{p_{0}\}\sim\{p_{0},p_{1}\}\sim\{p_{1}\}\sim\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\sim\cdots\sim\{p_{n-1},p_{n}\}\sim\{p_{n}\}\}.

This show that
\mathbf{P}
is a full subcategory of the category of trees.
Corollary 4.43
gives the thesis.
∎

Some notation is needed. Every pair of vertices
q
and
r
in a path
P
defines a
subpath

[q,r]
defined to be the smallest connected subset of
P
containing both
q
and
r
. Equivalently,
[q,r]
is the unique connected subset of
P
with
\mathsf{E}[q,r]=\{q,r\}
. Similarly, we let
(q,r)
denote the smallest subset connecting
\{q,r\}
, i.e.
(q,r)=[q,r]\setminus\{q,r\}
. For a path
P
and
Q\subseteq P
, we say that
b\in P
separates
Q\subseteq P
precisely when
b\in[q,r]\setminus Q
, for some
q,r\in Q
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{P}^{Q}_{P}
be monotone. Then
q_{\sqsupset}
is connected for every
q\in Q
.

Proof.

By monotonicity, we know that the non-strict preimage
q^{\sqsupset}
is connected. If
q_{\sqsupset}
were separated by some
p
, necessarily in
q^{\sqsupset}\setminus q_{\sqsupset}
, then we would have
q^{\prime}\neq q
with
p^{\sqsubset}=\{q,q^{\prime}\}
. As
\sqsupset
is co-injective, we would then also have
p^{\prime}\in P
with
p^{\prime\sqsubset}=\{q^{\prime}\}
, which must therefore be separated from
p
by some vertices of
q_{\sqsupset}
. But then
q^{\prime\sqsupset}
would not be connected, contradicting the monotonicity of
\sqsupset
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Note that both the fact that
\sqsupset
is co-injective and that we are dealing with paths (and not just trees) are necessary for the above lemma, as the following two examples show.

Let
Q=\{a,b\}
,
P=\{x\sim y\sim z\}
. Consider
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}_{P}^{Q}
be the surjective monotone morphism obtained by
x,z\sqsubset a
and
y\sqsubset a,b
. Then
a_{\sqsupset}=\{x,z\}
is not connected. When one wants to fix co-bijectivity, one adds a vertex
w
to
P
by connecting it only to
y
(obtaining therefore the claw graph, or the letter ‘T’). Extending
\sqsupset
to
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
by setting
w\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}b
, one obtains a co-bijective monotone morphism between trees such that
a_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
is the disconnected set
\{x,z\}
.

Here we focus on the subcategory
\mathbf{A}
of monotone morphisms, for the whole category
\mathbf{P}
, see
§​​ 6.2
. Our goal here is to prove that
\mathbf{A}
has amalgamation and hence Fraïssé sequences, which can be characterised in an analogous manner to
Theorem 5.2
.

Next proposition formalizes
Figure 4
, which describes a typical morphism in
\mathbf{A}
.

\bullet
q_{0}
\bullet
q_{1}
\bullet
q_{2}
\cdots

\bullet
q_{n}
\cdots
\bullet
a_{0}
\bullet
b_{0}
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
a_{1}=b_{1}
\bullet
a_{2}
\bullet
b_{2}
\bullet
a_{n}
\bullet
\bullet
b_{n}
\cdots

Figure 4.
A typical morphism in
\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
Q=\{q_{0}\sim q_{1}\sim\cdots\sim q_{n}\}
be a path, and let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}_{P}^{Q}
. Then there exists a unique sequence
a_{0},b_{0},\ldots,a_{n},b_{n}\in P
such that

(1)

[a_{i},b_{i}]=(q_{i})_{\sqsupset}\neq\emptyset
,

(2)

\mathsf{E}P=\{a_{0},b_{n}\}
, and

(3)

if
i<j<k
then
a_{j}\in[a_{i},a_{k}]
, or equivalently that for every
i
with
0<i<n
we have
[a_{i},b_{i}]\subseteq(b_{i-1},a_{i+1})
.

As a consequence for every
i<n
we have that
(b_{i},a_{i+1})=\{q_{i},q_{i+1}\}_{\sqsupset}
(which might be empty).

Proof.

We will define the points
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
and then show that they satisfy the thesis. By
Lemma 5.4
, each
(q_{i})_{\sqsupset}
is nonempty and connected, hence it is a subpath
[a_{i},b_{i}]
.

Let us show that one of
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
must be an end. Since
Q\setminus\{q_{0}\}
is connected, so is
P\setminus(q_{0})_{\sqsupset}=(Q\setminus\{q_{0}\})^{\sqsupset}
by monotonicity. Hence
P
is the disjoint union of the two subpaths
[a_{0},b_{0}]
and
P\setminus(q_{0})_{\sqsupset}
, so
[a_{0},b_{0}]
must contain an end of
P
. We can assume without loss of generality that
a_{0}
is an end, and the same proof shows that we can assume that
b_{n}
is an end too and that
\{a_{0},b_{n}\}=\mathsf{E}P
.

Generalising the above reasoning, one can, for every
i
, write
P
as the disjoint union of the possibly empty subpaths
P=[q_{0},q_{i})^{\sqsupset}\cup(q_{i})_{\sqsupset}\cup(q_{i},q_{n}]^{\sqsupset}
. (Note that by edge-preservation
[q_{0},q_{i})^{\sqsupset}
and
(q_{i},q_{n}]^{\sqsupset}
are disjoint, each contain an end, and therefore
(q_{i})_{\sqsupset}=[a_{i},b_{i}]
is truly in between). If
0<i<n
then
a_{i-1}\in[q_{0},q_{i})^{\sqsupset}
and
b_{i+1}\in(q_{i},q_{n}]^{\sqsupset}
, which implies that
[a_{i},b_{i}]\subseteq(b_{i-1},a_{i+1})
.

For the last statement, we swap
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
if
b_{i}\in(a_{0},a_{i})
. (In this way, the points
a_{0},b_{0},\ldots,a_{n},b_{n}
are truly ordered in the path, matching
Figure 4
.) Fix now
c\in(b_{i},a_{i+1})
. If
c\sqsubset q_{j}
for some
j<i
, then
b_{i}
separates the connected
q_{j}^{\sqsupset}\ni a_{j},c
, which is a contradiction. Likewise, we cannot have that
i+1<j
. Hence
c^{\sqsubset}\subseteq\{q_{i},q_{i+1}\}
, but since
c\notin[a_{i},b_{i}]\cup[a_{i+1},b_{i+1}]
, we must have that
c^{\sqsubset}=\{q_{i},q_{i+1}\}
.
∎

It follows from
Proposition 5.6
that
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}_{P}^{Q}
satisfies that for every
e\in\mathsf{E}Q
then
\mathsf{E}P\cap e_{\sqsupset}\neq\emptyset
. In particular
\sqsupset
is

(End-Preserving)

\displaystyle e\in\mathsf{E}P\qquad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad e^{\sqsubset}\subseteq\mathsf{E}Q,\text{ and }

(End-Surjective)

\displaystyle f\in\mathsf{E}Q\qquad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad f^{\sqsupset}\cap\mathsf{E}P\neq\emptyset.

Observation 5.7
.

From the structure described in
Proposition 5.6
, it follows that for
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{P}
and
q\sim r\in Q
there exists uniquely determined
q_{r}
and
r_{q}
such that
q_{r}^{\sqsubset}=\{q\}
,
r_{q}^{\sqsubset}=\{r\}
and for every
p\in(q_{r},r_{q})
we have
p^{\sqsubset}=\{q,r\}
. (In the case
q=q_{i}
and
r=q_{i+1}
in a given enumeration, then
q_{r}=b_{i}
and
r_{q}=a_{i+1}
).

Proposition 5.8
.

\mathbf{A}
is clique-closed.

Proof.

We need to check
Definition 4.38
. By Propositions
5.3
and
4.40
, it only remains to show that
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}
whenever
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{P}
. Since
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
is a surjective morphism between two connected acyclic graphs, by
Proposition 3.8
, it is edge-surjective, therefore to check monotonicity, by
Proposition 3.10
, it is enough to check that
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
-preimages of vertices in
\mathsf{X}Q
are connected.

Take
D\in\mathsf{X}Q
. If
D=\{q\}
then,
q_{\sqsupset}
is connected (
Lemma 5.4
) and hence so is
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset-1}\{D\}=\mathsf{X}q_{\sqsupset}
. If
D=\{q,r\}
, for distinct
q,r\in Q
, then
Observation 5.7
yields
q_{r}\in q_{\sqsupset}
and
r_{q}\in r_{\sqsupset}
such that
\{q,r\}_{\sqsupset}=(q_{r},r_{q})
. The definition of
\mathsf{X}
readily gives that
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset-1}\{q,r\}=\mathsf{X}((q^{r},r^{q}))
, which is connected. Thus
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{\mathsf{X}Q}_{\mathsf{X}P}
.
∎

To prove
\mathbf{A}
has amalgamation, it now suffices to consider functions in
\mathbf{A}
, by
Corollary 4.39
. A more detailed analysis of the morphisms in terms of their ‘types’ will also allow us to prove amalgamation directly, even for relations.

Fix paths
P
and
Q
. For any
{\sqsupset}\subseteq Q\times P
, we define
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}:\mathsf{X}Q\rightarrow\omega
by

\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(C)=|C_{\sqsupset}|.

Certain properties of
\sqsupset
can already be extracted from
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}
, e.g.

•

\sqsupset
is co-injective precisely when
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(\{q\})\geq 1
, for all
q\in Q
.

•

\sqsupset
is strictly anti-injective precisely when
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(\{q\})\geq 2
, for all
q\in Q
.

•

\sqsupset
is edge-witnessing precisely when
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(\{q,r\})\geq 1
for all adjacent
q,r\in Q
.

•

\sqsupset
is a partial function precisely when
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(C)\neq 0
implies
C=\{q\}
, for some
q\in Q
.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{P}
then, moreover,

•

\sqsupset
is star-refining precisely when
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(\{q,r\})\geq 2
for all adjacent
q,r\in Q
.

It could still make sense to consider a function
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}
associated to a morphism
{\sqsupset}\subseteq Q\times P
even outside the category of paths. In this case, some of the above properties are true in general, while others need some additional assumptions (for example, the characterisation of edge-witnessing holds only when
P
is triangle-free).

As our focus is on co-injective morphisms, we define types as follows.

Definition 5.9
.

We call
t:\mathsf{X}Q\rightarrow\omega
a
type
if
t(\{q\})\geq 1
, for all
q\in Q
.

Proposition 5.10
.

For each path
Q
and each type
t:\mathsf{X}Q\rightarrow\omega
, there are a path
P
and
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{P}
with
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}=t
.

Proof.

Take disjoint
P_{q}\in\mathbf{P}
with
|P_{q}|=t(\{q\})
, for all
q\in Q
. Further choose
q_{r}\in\mathsf{E}P_{q}
, for all adjacent
q,r\in Q
, such that
P_{q}=[q_{r},q_{s}]
when
q\sim r\neq s\sim q
. Then take
P_{q,r}\in\mathbf{P}
with
\mathsf{E}P=\{q_{r},r_{q}\}
, for all adjacent
q,r\in Q
, further ensuring that
P^{\prime}_{q,r}=P_{q,r}\setminus\{q_{r},r_{q}\}
are disjoint, both from each other and the
(P_{q})_{q\in Q}
, and that
|P^{\prime}_{q,r}|=t(\{q,r\})
, for all adjacent
q,r\in Q
. Thus
P=\bigcup_{C\in\mathsf{X}Q}P_{C}
is a path on which we can define
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{P}
by
p^{\sqsubset}=\{q\}
, for all
p\in P_{q}
, and
p^{\sqsubset}=\{q,r\}
, whenever
p\in P^{\prime}_{q,r}
. Then
|q_{\sqsupset}|=|P_{q}|=t(\{q\})
, for all
q\in Q
, and
|\{q,r\}_{\sqsupset}|=|P^{\prime}_{q,r}|=t(\{q,r\})
, for all adjacent
q,r\in Q
, showing that
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}=t
.
∎

The natural order on types, given by pointwise domination, corresponds to the factorisation order on morphisms.

Proposition 5.11
.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{R}_{P}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{A}^{R}_{Q}
then

\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}\leq\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\text{ there is }\,{\dashv}\in\mathbf{A}^{P}_{Q}\ ({\sqsupset}\circ{\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}).

Moreover, if
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(\{r,s\})=0
implies
\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}(\{r,s\})=0
for every adjacent
r,s\in R
, then we can choose
\dashv
to be a function.

Proof.

The
\Leftarrow
part is immediate from the fact that
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset\circ\dashv}\geq\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}
, for any
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{I}^{P}_{Q}
. Conversely, assume
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}\leq\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
. In particular, for each
r\in R
, this means
|r_{\sqsupset}|\leq|r_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}|
so we have a function
{\dashv_{r}}\in\mathbf{A}^{r_{\sqsupset}}_{r_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}}
with
r_{s}\dashv_{r}r^{\prime}_{s}
, for all
s\in r^{\sim}
, where
r_{s}
and
r^{\prime}_{s}
are the vertices defined from
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
respectively as in
Observation 5.7
. For all adjacent
r,s\in R
, this also means
|\{r,s\}_{\sqsupset}|\leq|\{r,s\}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}|
so we also have
{\dashv_{r,s}}\in\mathbf{A}^{[r_{s},s_{r}]}_{[r^{\prime}_{s},s^{\prime}_{r}]}
again with
r^{\prime\vdash_{r,s}}_{s}=\{r_{s}\}
and
s^{\prime\vdash_{r,s}}_{r}=\{s_{r}\}
, but this time with
q^{\vdash_{r,s}\sqsubset}=\{r,s\}
, for all
q\in(r^{\prime}_{s},s^{\prime}_{r})
. Indeed, if
(r_{s},s_{r})=\emptyset
then
r_{s}\sim s_{r}
and so we may set
q^{\vdash_{r,s}}=\{r_{s},s_{r}\}
, for all
q\in(r^{\prime}_{s},s^{\prime}_{r})
. On the other hand, if
(r_{s},s_{r})\neq\emptyset
then we can take
\dashv_{r,s}
to be a function from
(r^{\prime}_{s},s^{\prime}_{r})
onto
(r_{s},s_{r})
. Now
{\dashv}=\bigcup_{C\in\mathsf{X}R}\dashv_{C}
satisfies
{\sqsupset\circ\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
.

For the moreover part, when
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}(\{r,s\})=0
implies
\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}(\{r,s\})=0
for every adjacent
r,s\in R
, the second part of the proof does not apply, and therefore the union of the functions
\dashv_{r}
, for
r\in R
, does the job.
∎

The existence of sub-factorisations
{\sqsupset\circ\dashv}\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
could also be characterised in terms of
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}
and
\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
. For our purposes, the following sufficient condition will suffice.

Proposition 5.12
.

If
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{R}_{P}
,
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{A}^{R}_{Q}
,
|P|\leq|Q|
and
|P|\leq|\{r,s\}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}|
, for all adjacent
r,s\in R
, then we have
\phi\in(\mathbf{A}\cap\mathbf{F})^{P}_{Q}
with
{\sqsupset}\circ\phi\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
.

Proof.

If
|R|=1
then, as
|P|\leq|Q|
, we have monotone
\phi
mapping
Q
onto
P
. Otherwise, for each
r\in R
, pick
r^{\prime}\in r_{\sqsupset}
, the only proviso being that
r^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}P
whenever
r\in\mathsf{E}R
. Set
\phi(q)=r^{\prime}
whenever
q\in r_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
. As
|(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})|\leq|P|\leq|\{r,s\}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}|
, we can extend
\phi
to a monotone function mapping the path
[r_{s},s_{r}]
(where
r_{s}
and
s_{r}
are chosen by
Observation 5.7
for
r
,
s
, and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
) onto
[r^{\prime},s^{\prime}]
, for all adjacent
r,s\in R
. The resulting function
\phi\in(\mathbf{A}\cap\mathbf{F})^{P}_{Q}
then satisfies
{\sqsupset}\circ\phi\subseteq{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
.
∎

Amalgamation now follows from the fact that types are directed.

Proposition 5.13
.

\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{A}\cap\mathbf{F}
have amalgamation.

Proof.

Given any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{P}_{Q}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{A}^{P}_{R}
, we have a type
t\geq\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset},\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
(e.g. take
t=\max\{\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset},\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\}
). By
Proposition 5.10
, we have
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{A}^{P}_{S}
with
\mathsf{t}_{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}}=t
. By
Proposition 5.11
, we have
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{S}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{A}^{Q}_{R}
with
{\sqsupset\circ\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
. Again by
Proposition 5.11
, if
{\sqsupset}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
were functions,
{\dashv}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
can be chosen to be functions too.
∎

The singleton graph is a terminal object in
\mathbf{A}
, and so amalgamation implies directedness. By
Lemma 3.12
, edge-witnessing and star-refining morphisms are wide. Altogether,
Theorem 4.34
applies and we have a Fraïssé limit. Now we shall completely characterise (lax-)Fraïssé sequences in
\mathbf{A}
.

A sequence
(G_{n},{\sqsupset}_{n}^{m})
in
\mathbf{A}
is said to be
nontrivial
if
|G_{n}|\neq 1
, for some
n\in\omega
. By co-injectivity, if
|G|>1
and
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{P}_{H}^{G}
is edge-witnessing (or star-refining) then
|H|>|G|
.
3
3

3

This is true more generally if
G
is not discrete and we have a co-bijective edge-witnessing morphism, or if
H
is not discrete and we have a co-bijective star-refining morphism.
Hence any nontrivial sequence of edge-witnessing (or star-refining) morphisms gives that
\lim_{n}|G_{n}|=\infty
.

Lemma 5.14
.

In
\mathbf{A}
, every star-refining morphism is edge-witnessing, and every composition of two edge-witnessing morphisms is star-refining.

Proof.

The first part follows from
Corollary 3.9
and the second part from Propositions
3.5
and
3.11
.
∎

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{A}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{A}
;

(2)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is nontrivial, and it has an edge-witnessing subsequence;

(3)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is nontrivial, and it has a star-refining subsequence;

(4)

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is homeomorphic to the arc.

A lax-Fraïssé sequence is Fraïssé precisely when it also has a strictly anti-injective subsequence.

Proof.

Clearly every lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{A}
is nontrivial and by
Theorem 4.34
has an edge-witnessing and a star-refining subsequence.
Similarly, it is easy to see that strictly anti-injective morphisms are wide and so form a wide ideal in
\mathbf{A}
by
Proposition 3.4
.
Hence, every Fraïssé sequence has a strictly anti-injective subsequence by
Proposition 4.31
.
Together with a use of
Lemma 5.14
we have
(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3)
.

To show
(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
take
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{G_{m}}_{P}
. If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an edge-witnessing subsequence then we have
n>m
such that
|P|\leq|G_{n}|
(since the sequence is nontrivial) and
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
can be written as a composition of
|P|
edge-witnessing morphisms so
|P|\leq|\{g,h\}_{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}|
, for all adjacent
g,h\in G_{m}
. These are precisely the hypotheses of
Proposition 5.12
, which then applies, giving
\phi\in(\mathbf{A}\cap\mathbf{F})^{P}_{Q}
with
{\sqsupset}\circ\phi\subseteq{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}
. Hence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is lax-Fraïssé.

Similarly, if
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has both nontrivial edge-witnessing and strictly anti-injective subsequences, then for any
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{A}^{G_{m}}_{P}
there is
n>m
such that
\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}\geq\mathsf{t}_{\sqsupset}
.
By
Proposition 5.11
, we then have
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{A}^{P}_{Q}
with
{\sqsupset}\circ{\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
, showing
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is Fraïssé.

The implication
(4)
\Rightarrow
(2)
(and
(3)
) follows directly from
Corollary 4.27
.
On the other hand, as all lax-Fraïssé sequences have homeomorphic spectra by
Theorem 4.34
, it suffices to find a single lax-Fraïssé sequence whose spectrum is the arc.
By
Proposition 4.21
it is enough to find a suitable sequence of minimal open covers of
[0,1]
.
Such sequence was described in
[
BBV25
, Example 2.16]
: consider

C_{n}=\{\mathrm{int}([(k-1)/2^{n+1},(k+1)/2^{n+1}]):1\leq k\leq 2^{n+1}-1\}

(see also
Figure 3
). Then each
C_{n}
with the overlap relation is a path, and the inclusion relation restricted to
C_{n}\times C_{n+1}
is monotone and edge-witnessing.
Hence the induced sequence in
\mathbf{A}
is lax-Fraïssé by
(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
.
∎

Example 5.16
.

Let us give a concrete example of a lax-Fraïsse sequence (therefore leading to the arc) which is not Fraïssé. Let
G_{0}
be a two-point path, and for every
n\in\omega
, let
G_{n+1}
be
\mathsf{X}{G_{n}}
and let
\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}
be the natural map
\ni_{G_{n}}
.
By
Proposition 5.8
,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{A}
.
It is easy to see that
\mathsf{t}_{\ni_{G_{n}}}
is a constant function taking the value
1
, and so
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is edge-witnessing and anti-injective, but not star-refining.
Also,
g_{\sqsupset^{m}_{n}}
is a singleton for every
g\in G_{m}
and
n\geq m
, so
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
does not have a strictly anti-injective subsequence.
Hence,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is lax-Fraïssé in
\mathbf{A}
but not Fraïssé.

5.2.1.
More sequences leading to the arc

As the following example shows, we do not have a Fraïssé sequence in the category of paths and monotone surjective relations in
\mathbf{S}
, as the amalgamation property fails.
However, we show sequences in this category admin a
co-bijective modification
, and often have the arc as the spectrum of the induced poset.
This will be used later when analyzing certain categories of fans.

Example 5.17
.

Let
F=\{f,f^{\prime}\}
,
G=\{g\}
, and
H=\{h,h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}\}
be paths.
Let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}^{F}_{G}
be the only surjective morphism, and let
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{S}^{F}_{H}
be the function satisfying
f\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}h,h^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}h^{\prime\prime}
.
Note that
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
are monotone and surjective.
There is no path
P
with surjective morphisms
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{S}^{G}_{P}
,
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\in\mathbf{S}^{H}_{P}
such that
{\sqsupset}\circ{\dashv}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\circ{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}
.
Otherwise, there is
p\in P
such that
p\vDash h
, and hence
p^{\vDash}\subseteq\{h,h^{\prime}\}
and
p^{\vDash\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}=\{f\}
.
On the other hand,
p^{\vdash\sqsubset}=\{f,f^{\prime}\}
for every
p\in P
.

By the following lemma, monotone surjective morphisms between paths are automatically co-bijective at non-ends.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
F
and
G
be paths and let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}^{G}_{F}
be monotone and surjective.
Then for every
g\in G\setminus\mathsf{E}G
there is
f\in F\setminus\mathsf{E}F
such that
f^{\sqsubset}=\{g\}
.

Proof.

Let
g_{-}
and
g_{+}
be the two neighbours of
g
and pick
e_{-},e_{+}\in\mathsf{E}G
such that
g_{-}\in[e_{-},g]
and
g_{+}\in[g,e_{+}]
.
Let
X^{-}=[e_{-},g_{-}]^{\sqsupset}
and
X^{+}=[g_{+},e_{+}]^{\sqsupset}
.
Since
\sqsupset
is monotone,
X_{-}
and
X_{+}
are connected, and hence they are (non-empty) subpaths of
G
.
Note that no vertex of
[e_{-},g_{-}]
is adjacent to a vertex of
[g_{+},e_{+}]
, and so no vertex of
X_{-}
is adjacent to a vertex of
X_{+}
.
Hence, there is a vertex
f\in G
strictly between the paths
X_{-}
and
X_{+}
.
Clearly,
f\notin\mathsf{E}F
, and the only possibility is that
f^{\sqsubset}=\{g\}
.
∎

For
e
an end of a path
P
, let
e^{\mathrm{op}}
denote the opposite end.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a monotone surjective sequence of paths in
\mathbf{S}
. Suppose that there is a thread of ends
(e_{n})
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
such that
e_{n+1}{}^{\sqsubset_{n+1}^{n}}=\{e_{n}\}
for every
n
.
Then there is the largest co-bijective restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
, satisfying

(1)

H_{n}=[e_{n},f_{n}]
for some
f_{n}
with
f_{n}\sqcap e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
,

(2)

for every
m
there is
n\geq m
such that
f_{m}\sqsupset_{n}^{m}e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
,

(3)

(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{A}
, and

(4)

(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
and
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
have canonically homeomorphic spectra.

Proof.

We construct the sets
H_{n}
, and then prove that
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
, where
