{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m}}={\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\restriction H_{n}\times H_{m}
, satisfies the required properties.

This construction is done by removing ‘redundant’ points from
G_{n}
. First, declare
g\in G_{n}
to be ‘red’ if
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
is not co-bijective at
g
, i.e. there is no
h\in G_{n+1}
such that
h^{\sqsubset_{n+1}^{n}}=\{g\}
. These points have to be removed to ensure co-bijectivity. Removing red points may not be enough. In general, if
h\in G_{n+1}
needs to removed and
g\in G_{n}
is such that co-bijectivity at
g
is witnessed only by
h
(that is, the strict preimage
g_{\sqsupset}
equals
\{h\}
), then
g
must be removed too. We apply this reasoning inductively: we say that
g\in G_{n}
is a ‘blue’ point if the only point in
G_{n+1}
witnessing co-bijectivity of
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
at
g
is either blue or red. In particular, every blue point
g\in G_{m}
is blue because of a unique red point belonging to some later
G_{n}
.

By
Lemma 5.18
and our hypotheses, if
g
is red then
g=e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Furthermore by the same reasons, if
g\in G_{n}
is blue because of
h\in G_{n+1}
and
h=e_{n+1}^{\mathrm{op}}
, then
g=e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
, as co-bijectivity of non-ends is witnessed at non-ends and no original end
e_{n}
is red or blue.

Let
H_{n}=[e_{n},f_{n}]
where
f_{n}
is the last point which neither red nor blue. By the above,
f_{n}\sqcap e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
. We are now going to show that
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
is a co-bijective sequence which satisfies
(2)
–
(4)
(
(1)
follows from the definition of
H_{n}
).

To show that
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n+1}^{n}
is co-bijective, pick
h\in H_{n}
. If
h\neq e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
, then co-bijectivity of
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
at
h
is witnessed by some
g\in G_{n+1}\setminus\{e_{n+1}^{\mathrm{op}}\}\subseteq H_{n+1}
(
Lemma 5.18
). If
h=e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
then, since
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
is not red, there is
g\in G_{n+1}
such that
g^{\sqsubset_{n+1}^{n}}=\{h\}
. Since
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
is not blue, such
g
can be chosen to be neither blue nor red, i.e. in
H_{n+1}
. Notice that
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n+1}^{n}
is a co-bijective morphism between paths that is monotone by
Lemma 3.13
, i.e. it belongs to
\mathbf{A}
, and therefore it is end-preserving. This entails that
f_{n+1}{}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n+1}^{n}}=\{f_{n}\}
, and in particular
(f_{n})
that is a thread.

Let us now show
(2)
. Fix
m
. If
f_{m}=e_{m}^{\mathrm{op}}
, there is nothing to prove. Else, let
n\geq m
be minimal such that
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
is red. Hence
e_{n+1}^{\mathrm{op}}\sqsubset_{n+1}^{n}f_{n}\sqsubset_{n}^{m}f_{m}
.

For
(3)
, we now show that
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
is indeed a sequence. Otherwise (by induction), there are
m,n
with
\ell=n-m\geq 2
,
g\in H_{m}
and
g^{\prime}\in H_{n}
such that
g\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m}g^{\prime}
yet there is no
k
with
m<k<n
and
h\in H_{k}
such that
g\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{k}h\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{k}_{n}g^{\prime}
. Since
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a sequence and
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}g^{\prime}
, there are
g_{0},\ldots,g_{\ell}
such that

g=g_{0}\sqsupset_{m+1}^{m}g_{1}\sqsupset_{m+2}^{m+1}\cdots\sqsupset_{n-1}^{n-2}g_{\ell-1}\sqsupset_{n}^{n-1}g_{\ell}=g^{\prime}.

By our choice of
m
and
n
, we have that
g_{i}\notin H_{m+i}
for any positive
i<\ell
, and hence
g_{i}=e_{m+i}^{\mathrm{op}}
for each such
i
. We aim to show
g=f_{m}
and
f_{n-1}\sqsupset_{n}^{n-1}g^{\prime}
. If this is the case, the sequence
g=f_{m},f_{m+1},\ldots,f_{n-1},g^{\prime}
witnesses the property of
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
being is a sequence is not broken by
g
and
g^{\prime}
.

Suppose
g\neq f_{m}
. Note that
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}_{m+1}^{m}\in\mathbf{A}
and
f_{m+1}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{m+1}}=\{f_{m}\}
, and therefore there is
h\in[e_{m+1},f_{m+1})
with
h^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{m+1}}=\{g\}
. Hence, the set
g^{\sqsupset_{m+1}^{m}}
contains both
h
and
e_{m+1}^{\mathrm{op}}
, but does not contain
f_{m+1}\in(h,e_{m+1}^{\mathrm{op}})
, and so is disconnected. This contradicts the monotonicity of
\sqsupset_{m+1}^{m}
.

Next, since
e_{n-1}^{\mathrm{op}}\sqsupset_{n}^{n-1}g^{\prime}
, and
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
was either blue or red, the image of
g^{\prime}
cannot be just the point
e_{n-1}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and so
g^{\prime}{}^{\sqsubset^{n-1}_{n}}=\{e_{n-1}^{\mathrm{op}},h\}
for some
h\sim e_{n-1}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Clearly,
h=f_{n-1}
. This concludes the proof that
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}^{m})
forms a sequence in
\mathbf{A}
.

We are left to show
(4)
. This will follow from
Proposition 4.13
once we show that each
H_{n}
is dense. If
H_{n}=G_{n}
there is nothing to prove. Else, if
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
is red, then
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
is not co-bijective at
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
, meaning that
H_{n}{}^{\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}}=G_{n+1}
. If
e_{n}^{\mathrm{op}}
is blue as witnessed by the red point
e_{k}^{\mathrm{op}}\in G_{k}
for some
k>n
, then
G_{k+1}=H_{k}{}^{\sqsupset_{k+1}^{k}}\subseteq H_{n}{}^{\sqsupset_{k+1}^{n}}
as
H_{k}\subseteq H_{n}{}^{\sqsupset_{k}^{n}}
.
∎

We call the restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
obtained in the previous proposition the
co-bijective modification
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
.

Remark 5.20
.

Note that co-bijective modification can be almost uniquely defined for every monotone surjective sequence of paths
([a_{n},b_{n}],\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{S}
.
If
|[a_{0},b_{0}]|\geq 3
, there is a thread
(g_{n})
with
g_{n+1}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}=\{g_{n}\}
such that
([a_{n},g_{n}])
and
([g_{n},b_{n}])
induce upper restrictions of
([a_{n},b_{n}],\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, for which
Proposition 5.19
can be applied separately, and the resulting modifications can be combined.

If an initial segment or the whole sequence consists of short paths, we use the following observation: for a path
G
with
\leq 2
points and
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}^{G}_{H}
for some
H
, the relation
\sqsupset
is co-bijective or there is
g\in G
with
g^{\sqsupset}\supseteq H
.

Corollary 5.21
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a monotone surjective sequence of paths in
\mathbf{S}
and let
\mathbb{P}
be the induced poset.
If
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining subsequence, then
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is an arc or a point.

Proof.

Let
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
be a co-bijective modification of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, which exists and is in
\mathbf{A}
by
Proposition 5.19
and by the previous remark, and let
\mathbb{Q}
be the induced poset, whose spectrum is canonically homeomorphic to
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.

Let
m\leq n
and suppose
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is star-refining.
We show that
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n}
is also star-refining, and so
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining subsequence as well.
For every
h\in H_{n}
there is
g\in G_{m}
such that
h^{\sqcap}\sqsubset^{m}_{n}g
.
If
g\in H_{m}
, we are done.
Otherwise,
g
is an end of
G_{m}
and
h^{\sqcap}\cap H_{n}\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n}g_{+}
where
g_{+}
is the unique vertex adjacent to
g
.
This is because
x^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq\{g,g_{+}\}
and so
x^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n}}=\{g_{+}\}
for every
x\in h^{\sqcap}\cap H_{n}
.

If
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is trivial, its spectrum is a point. Otherwise, by
Theorem 5.15
,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}
is the arc.
∎

5.3.
Fans

In this section, we work with both graph theoretic and topological fans. Even though the terminology tends to converge, there are some slight differences.

5.3.1.
Topological and graph theoretic fans

Most of our terminology is taken from
[
Cha67
]
.
Recall that a
dendroid
is a continuum
X
that is
arcwise connected
, i.e. for every
x\neq y\in X
there is an arc
A\subseteq X
with endpoints
x,y
, and hereditarily unicoherent (the intersection of every two subcontinua is connected).
It follows that for every two points
x\neq y
in a dendroid
X
there is a unique arc in
X
with endpoints
x,y
, sometimes denoted by
[x,y]
.
Note that a dendrite is exactly a locally connected dendroid (as every Peano continuum is arcwise connected).

A point
x\in X
of a dendroid is called an
endpoint
if for every arc
A\subseteq X
such that
x\in A
we have that
x
is an endpoint of
A
.
Let
\mathsf{E}X
denote the set of endpoints of
X
.
A point
x\in X
of a dendroid is called a
ramification point
if there is a simple triod
T\subseteq X
such that
x
is the ramification point of
T
, i.e. there are three arcs
A_{i}\subseteq X
,
i<3
, such that
A_{i}\cap A_{j}=\{x\}
for every
i\neq j<3
.

A
(topological) fan
is a dendroid
X
that has exactly one ramification point
0
, called the
root
.
The arcs
[0,e]
,
e\in\mathsf{E}X
, are called
spokes
, and they form the unique family of arcs
\mathcal{A}
such that
\bigcup\mathcal{A}=X
and
A\cap A^{\prime}=\{0\}
for every
A,A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
– the
spoke decomposition
of a (topological) fan.
A dendroid with a spoke decomposition is already a fan.

There are two fans we will mainly focus our attention on:

•

The
Cantor fan
is the space
X=(C\times[0,1])/{\sim}
where
(x,t)\sim(y,s)
if and only if the pairs are equal or
s=t=0
, where
C
is the Cantor space. This can be viewed as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
obtained by drawing
C
as a subset of
[0,1]\times\{1\}
and adding the segment between
(1/2,0)
and each point of
C
.

•

A
Lelek fan
is a nondegenerate subcontinuum of the Cantor fan whose endpoints are dense. All Lelek fans are homeomorphic, and it makes therefore sense to speak of
the
Lelek fan (
[
BO90
]
or
[
Cha89
]
).

Let’s now focus on graphs.
A (nontrivial)
fan

F
is a tree with exactly one node of degree
\geq 3
, the
root
of the fan, denoted by
0_{F}
.
A finite fan can be obtained by gluing together a certain amount of finite paths by one of their ends.
The corresponding subpaths of
F
, i.e. the subpaths whose ends are the root and an end of
F
, are called
spokes
.
We denote the set of all spokes of
F
by
*F
.

Note that the singleton graph is not a fan, but the
claw
(i.e. the graph
\{0,a,b,c\}
where
0
is connected to
a
,
b
, and
c
) is the weakly terminal object in our category.

5.3.2.
The fan categories

We focus on the category of nontrivial fans where morphisms are co-bijective edge-preserving morphisms, i.e. elements of the category
\mathbf{B}
. By
Proposition 3.8
, such morphisms are edge-surjective, i.e. are in
\mathbf{E}
.

For fans
F
and
G
, we call
{\sqsupset}\subseteq G\times F

spoke-monotone
if it is root-preserving (i.e.
0_{F}^{\sqsubset}=\{0_{G}\}
), and the restriction to any spoke
S\subseteq F
is a monotone relation between
S
and another spoke
T\subseteq G
i.e.

S\in\ast F\quad\Rightarrow\quad\exists T\in*G\ (S^{\sqsubset}\subseteq T\text{ and }({\sqsupset}\restriction T\times S)\text{ is monotone}).

The spoke
T
does not have to be unique (
S
can be collapsed to the root).

Let
\mathbf{L}

be the category
4
4

4

The name
\mathbf{L}
comes from that, as we will see below, the Fraïssé limit of
\mathbf{L}
is the Lelek fan.
whose objects are nontrivial fans and whose morphisms are spoke-monotone morphisms in
\mathbf{B}
. Further, we let
\mathbf{X}
be the subcategory of
\mathbf{L}
with the same objects but where the morphisms are
end-preserving
, meaning the image of an end consists only of ends. In formulas,
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{G}_{F}^{G}
is end-preserving if for every
e\in\mathsf{E}F
we have that
e^{\sqsubset}\subseteq\mathsf{E}G
.
In formulas, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{ob}(\mathbf{L})=\mathsf{ob}(\mathbf{X})=\{\text{nontrivial fans}\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{L})=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{B}):{\sqsupset}\text{ is spoke-monotone}\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{X})=\{{\sqsupset}\in\mathsf{mor}(\mathbf{L}):{\sqsupset}\text{ is end-preserving}\}.

In particular, if
F
and
G
are fans and
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{X}_{F}^{G}
, then for every pair of spokes
S\in\ast F
and
T\in\ast G
such that
S^{\sqsubset}\subseteq T
, we have that
{\sqsupset}\restriction T\times S
is a monotone surjective morphism between the paths
S
and
T
which sends ends to ends. Since co-injectivity for monotone surjective maps between paths can only fail at ends (
Lemma 5.18
) and ends get sent to ends,
{\sqsupset}\restriction T\times S
is co-injective and therefore belongs to
\mathbf{A}
. This also shows that
\mathbf{L}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
, i.e., every morphism is edge-surjective.

The difference between
\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{X}
is that for a morphism
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{F}^{G}
we do not require that the image of each spoke of
F
is a spoke, yet it may be a proper subset of one. Nevertheless, co-bijectivity of
\sqsupset
ensures the following.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
G
and
H
be fans and let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{G}^{H}
.
For every
T\in*H
there is
S\in*G
and
e\in\mathsf{E}G\cap S
such that
e^{\sqsubset}=\mathsf{E}H\cap T
.
Hence,
{\sqsupset}\restriction T\times S
belongs to
\mathbf{A}
, and
\sqsupset
is end-surjective.

Proof.

Let
\{f\}=\mathsf{E}H\cap T
.
Since
\sqsupset
is co-bijective, there is
e\in G
with
e^{\sqsubset}=\{f\}
.
Since
f\neq 0_{H}
, we have
e\neq 0_{H}
, and there is a unique spoke
S\in*G
with
e\in S
.
Without loss of generality,
e
is the vertex of
S
farthest from
0_{G}
such that
e^{\sqsubset}=\{f\}
.
The restriction
{\sqsupset}\restriction T\times[0_{G},e]
is surjective since
[0_{G},e]^{\sqsubset}
is connected and contains
f
, is monotone by
Lemma 3.13
, and is co-bijective by
Lemma 5.18
, and so is in
\mathbf{A}
.
We want to show that
e
is an end of
S
.
Otherwise, there is a vertex
e_{+}\in S\setminus[0_{G},e]
adjacent to
e
.
But
e_{+}^{\sqsubset}\ni f_{-}\sim f
would contradict the connectedness of
f_{-}^{\sqsupset}
, and so
e_{+}^{\sqsubset}=\{f\}
, which contradicts the maximality of
[0_{G},e]
.
∎

As every
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{X}^{G}_{F}
is end-preserving,
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset)={\sqsupset}\restriction\mathsf{E}F^{\sqsubset}\times\mathsf{E}F
is in
\mathbf{S}^{\mathsf{E}G}_{\mathsf{E}F}
.
As
\sqsupset
is end-surjective by the previous lemma,
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset)
is surjective.
Since
\mathsf{E}G
is a discrete graph,
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset)
is a (surjective) function.
Altogether, we obtain the
end functor

\mathsf{E}\colon\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{D}
.
Hence, to every sequence in
\mathbf{X}
there is an associated sequence in
\mathbf{D}
, i.e. an inverse sequences of surjections between finite sets, which corresponds to a finitely branching
\omega
-tree.
The situation in
\mathbf{L}
is more delicate, but still can be captured.

For
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
, we write
0_{n}
(instead of
0_{n})
for the root of
G_{n}
.

Definition 5.23
.

The
tree of spokes
of a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{L}
is the following structure consisting of
\omega
-many levels: the
n
th
level is the finite set of spokes
*G_{n}
.
Moreover, we declare a spoke
S\in*G_{n+1}
to be an immediate successor of a spoke
T\in*G_{n}
if
\{0_{n}\}\subsetneq S^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}\subseteq T
.
As it may happen that
S^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}=\{0_{n}\}
, there may be nodes at level
n+1
without a predecessor.

By a
branch of spokes
we mean a sequence
\bar{S}=(S_{n})
such that there exists
n_{0}
such that for every
n\geq n_{0}
we have
S_{n}\in*G_{n}
and
S_{n+1}
is an immediate successor of
S_{n}
, while
S_{m}=\{0_{m}\}=S_{n_{0}}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n_{0}}}
for every
m\leq n_{0}
.
We identify
\bar{S}
with
(S_{n},{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\restriction S_{m}\times S_{n})
, which is a sequence in
\mathbf{S}
and an upper restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
.

Note that
n_{0}
in the definition above is unique for
\bar{S}
, and that if for two branches of spokes
\bar{S}
and
\bar{S}^{\prime}
we have
S_{n}=S^{\prime}_{n}
, then also
S_{m}=S^{\prime}_{m}
for every
m\leq n
.
Also note that if the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is in
\mathbf{X}
, then the tree of spokes is just the
\omega
-tree corresponding to
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, and branches of spokes are all sequences of spokes
(S_{n})
such that
S_{n}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}=S_{m}
for every
m\leq n
.

Given a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
, we would like to analyze the subspace of its spectrum associated with a branch of spokes.
Since a branch of spokes may not be surjective, we need to pass to its
surjective core
.

Observation 5.24
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be any sequence in
\mathbf{S}
.
By putting
H_{n}=\bigcap_{k\geq n}G_{k}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{k}}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}^{m}_{n}={\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}\restriction H_{m}\times H_{n}
for every
m\leq n
we obtain the largest surjective upper restriction
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, which we call the
surjective core
.

There is
k_{0}\geq n
such that
H_{n}=G_{k}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{k}}
for every
k\geq k_{0}
.

Proof.

The last part follows from the fact that
(G_{k}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{k}})_{k\geq n}
is a decreasing sequence of finite sets, and so it stabilises.
For every surjective restriction
(H^{\prime}_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
we have
H^{\prime}_{n}=H^{\prime}_{k}{}^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}^{n}_{k}}\subseteq G_{k}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{k}}
for every
n\leq k
, and so
H^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq H_{n}
.
The rest follows from the equalities

\textstyle H_{n}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}=(\bigcap_{k\geq n}G_{k}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{k}})^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}=\bigcap_{k\geq n}G_{k}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{k}}=H_{m}.\qed

We say that a branch of spokes is
nondegenerate
if the spectrum of its surjective core is not a singleton, equivalently if the co-bijective modification of the surjective core is nontrivial.
Note that a nondegenerate branch of spokes
\bar{S}
is uniquely determined by its surjective core
\bar{T}=(T_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
and also by the co-bijective modification
\bar{T}^{\prime}
of
\bar{T}
: as
\bar{T}^{\prime}
is nontrivial, we have
|T^{\prime}_{n}|\geq 2
for every
n\geq n_{0}
, and so
T^{\prime}_{n}
and
T_{n}
are contained in a unique spoke
S_{n}\in*G_{n}
, and
\bar{S}
is uniquely determined by its tail.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
.
For every
k\in\omega
and every spoke
S\in*G_{k}
there is a nondegenerate branch of spokes
(S_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
with
S_{k}=S
such that
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})_{n\geq m\geq k}
is in
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

We put
S_{k}=S
. We recursively choose
S_{n}
for
n>k
by an application of
Lemma 5.22
. For
n<k
, we recursively define
S_{n}
to be the unique spoke
T\in*G_{n}
such that
\{0_{n}\}\subsetneq S_{n+1}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}\subseteq T
if the image is nondegenerate, and
S_{n}=\{0_{n}\}
otherwise.
∎

If
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
, we denote by
\bar{0}\in\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
the thread of ends
(0_{n})
, viewed also as a minimal selector.
Moreover, for a branch of spokes
\bar{S}
, let
\mathbb{Q}_{\bar{S}}
denote the poset induced by the surjective core of
\bar{S}
,
and let
(e_{\bar{S},n})
denote the thread of ends of the co-bijective modification of the surjective core of
\bar{S}
different from
\bar{0}
.

Putting it all together, we describe the spoke decomposition of the resulting spectrum, which turns out to be a (topological) fan, coming from nondegenerate branches of spokes of the starting sequence.

Proposition 5.26
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
with spectrum
X
, and suppose that
X
is connected and Hausdorff. Then:

(1)

X
is a fan with root
\bar{0}
.

(2)

The map
\bar{S}\mapsto\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}_{\bar{S}}\subseteq X
is a one-to-one correspondence between nondegenerate branches of spokes in
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
and spokes in
X
,
with
(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}\in\mathsf{E}X\cap\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}_{\bar{S}}
being the corresponding endpoint.

If in addition the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
belongs to
\mathbf{X}
, then

(3)

\mathsf{E}X
is the closed subspace
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{R}\subseteq X
where
\mathbb{R}
is the poset induced by the sequence
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
in
\mathbf{D}
.

Proof.

By
Corollary 4.27
,
X
is a hereditarily unicoherent continuum, and
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is edge-faithful and has a star-refining subsequence.
We will show
(1)
and
(2)
at once.
To show that
X
is a fan, we need to show that it is arcwise connected and that
\bar{0}
is its only ramification point.

For every nondegenerate branch of spokes
\bar{S}
let
A_{\bar{S}}
denote the spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}_{\bar{S}}
.
We show that
A_{\bar{S}}\subseteq X
is an arc with endpoints
\bar{0}
and
(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}
.
Let
\bar{T}
be the surjective core of
\bar{S}
and let
\bar{T^{\prime}}
be the co-bijective modification of
\bar{T}
obtained by
Proposition 5.19
, with induced poset
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}_{\bar{S}}
.
We have that
S\mapsto S^{\leq}
is an embedding
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}_{\bar{S}}\to X
onto
A_{\bar{S}}
by
Proposition 4.10
and
Proposition 4.13
.
Since
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining subsequence, so does
\bar{T}

as for every star-refining
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{S}^{H}_{G}
and
T\subseteq G
, the restriction
{\sqsupset}\restriction T^{\sqsubset}\times T
is star-refining as well.
Hence,
A_{\bar{S}}
is a singleton or an arc by
Corollary 5.21
.
Since
\bar{S}
is nondegenerate,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}_{\bar{S}}
is an arc with endpoints
\bar{0}
and
(e_{\bar{S},n})
, and so the claim follows.

For nondegenerate branches of spokes
\bar{S}\neq\bar{S}^{\prime}
there is
n\in\omega
such that
S_{n}\neq S^{\prime}_{n}
.
Then for every
x\in A_{\bar{S}}\cap A_{\bar{S}^{\prime}}
we have
[\{x\}]_{n}\subseteq[A_{\bar{S}}]_{n}\cap[A_{\bar{S}^{\prime}}]_{n}\subseteq S_{n}\cap S^{\prime}_{n}=\{0_{n}\}
.
Hence,
x=\bar{0}
and
A_{\bar{S}}\cap A_{\bar{S}^{\prime}}=\{\bar{0}\}
.

For every
x\in X
there is a nondegenerate branch of spokes
\bar{S}
such that
x\in A_{\bar{S}}
.
If
x=\bar{0}
, this is clear.
If for some
n
(and so for almost every
n
) the trace
[\{x\}]_{n}
intersects two spokes of
G_{n}
, then the trace contains
0_{n}
as it is connected by
Proposition 4.25
. Hence,
\bar{0}\subseteq x
, and so
x=\bar{0}
.
Otherwise,
[\{x\}]_{n}\subseteq T_{n}\subseteq S_{n}
for a branch of spokes
\bar{S}
and its surjective core
\bar{T}
. If the co-bijective modification of
\bar{T}
is nontrivial, then
\bar{S}
is nondegenerate with
x\in A_{\bar{S}}
.
Otherwise, again
x=\bar{0}
.
Altogether,
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is arcwise connected, and so is a dendroid.

Since, by
Lemma 5.25
, every spoke of
G_{0}
can be extended to a nondegenerate branch of spokes and since
G_{0}
is a nontrivial fan with at least three spokes,
\bar{0}
is indeed a ramification point.
Altogether,
\{A_{\bar{S}}:\bar{S}\text{ is a nondegenerate branch of spokes}\}

is a spoke decomposition of
X
, and so
X
is a fan with ramification point
\bar{0}
, and
(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}
are the endpoints of
X
.
This proves
(1)
and
(2)
.

To prove
(3)
suppose that the sequence
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is end-preserving.
Then all sequences
\bar{S}=(S_{n})
of spokes with
S_{n}{}^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\subseteq S_{m}
for all
m\leq n
induce nondegenerate branches of spokes, which are already sequences in
\mathbf{A}
.
Their threads of ends
(e_{\bar{S},n})=(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}
, which form the endpoints of
X
, are exactly all threads of
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
, which form the spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{R}
.
The spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{R}
is a closed subspace of
X
by
Proposition 4.10
since
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{D}
and an upper restriction of
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
.
∎

Before moving on to study
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{L}
individually we show that these two categories are Fraïssé.

Lemma 5.27
.

Star-refining morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{X}
, and therefore in
\mathbf{L}
.

Proof.

Pick a fan
F
. For every spoke
S\in\ast F
, we can find a path graph
S^{\prime}
and a star-refining morphism in
\mathbf{A}_{S^{\prime}}^{S}
. Since gluing together star-refining morphisms at the root results in a star-refining morphism between the corresponding fans, we have that star-refining morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{X}
. As
\mathbf{L}
has the same objects as
\mathbf{X}
, the thesis follows.
∎

Proposition 5.28
.

Both
\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{X}
are clique-closed.

Proof.

Let
G
be a fan. As there are no triangles in fans, cliques are either singletons or pairs of adjacent vertices. The graph
\mathsf{X}G
has a root given by
C=\{0_{G}\}
. Further, since every point in
G\setminus\{0_{G}\}
is related to at most two elements, every element of
\mathsf{X}G\setminus\{C\}
is only related to two elements. Hence
\mathsf{X}G
is a fan. Note that
\mathsf{X}G
has exactly as many spokes as
G
does (yet they are longer).

Let
{\in_{G}}\subseteq G\times\mathsf{X}G
be the morphism associating to each clique
C\in\mathsf{X}G
its elements.
\in_{G}
maps the root to the root. Furthermore, if
S\in\ast G
, then
{\in_{G}}\restriction S\times\mathsf{X}S
is end-preserving, monotone, and co-bijective, and therefore belongs to
\mathbf{A}
. Hence
{\in_{G}}\in\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{L}
.

We are left to show that if
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{G}^{H}
, for fans
G
and
H
, then
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}
. We further prove that if
\sqsupset
is end-preserving so is
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
; this will give that both
\mathbf{L}
and
\mathbf{X}
are clique-closed. Since
\mathbf{L}\subseteq\mathbf{E}
and fans are triangle-free,
Proposition 4.36
implies that
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
belongs to
\mathbf{B}
. Since
\sqsupset
is spoke-monotone, we can find
i^{\prime}
such that
(G^{i})^{\sqsubset}\subseteq H^{i^{\prime}}
, hence
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(\mathsf{X}G^{i})\subseteq\mathsf{X}H^{i^{\prime}}
, and the induced morphism is monotone and sends the root to the root. If further each inclusion as above is not strict, each inclusion of the form
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}(\mathsf{X}G^{i})\subseteq\mathsf{X}H^{i^{\prime}}
cannot be strict, therefore if
\sqsupset
is end-preserving, so is
\mathsf{X}^{\sqsupset}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.29
.

The categories
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{L}
are directed and have amalgamation.

Proof.

The idea is to amalgamate spoke-by-spoke, and then to glue all amalgamations.
Since we have a weakly terminal object (the claw), it is enough to show amalgamation and we will get directedness. Fix then nontrivial fans
F
,
G
, and
H
, and let
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{G}^{F}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{L}_{H}^{F}
be two morphisms. (For amalgamation in
\mathbf{X}
, we will just show that if
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
belong to
\mathbf{X}
, so do the amalgamating morphisms). Since
\mathbf{L}
(and
\mathbf{X}
) are clique-closed (
Proposition 5.28
), we can assume that both
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
are functions.

Define

\Gamma=\{(S,S^{\prime})\in\ast G\times\ast H:S^{\sqsubset}\subseteq(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\text{ or }(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq S^{\sqsubset}\}.

Fix
(S,S^{\prime})\in\Gamma
such that
S^{\sqsubset}\subseteq(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
and let
R=S^{\sqsubset}
. By spoke-monotonicity,
R
is a subpath of
F
which has the root
0_{F}
as one of its ends. In particular, if
R\neq\{0_{F}\}
, it is completely contained in a spoke of
F
.
Let

{\sqsupset_{S,S^{\prime}}}:={\sqsupset}\restriction R\times S\text{ and }{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}}:={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\restriction R\times(R^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\cap S^{\prime}).

Both
\sqsupset_{S,S^{\prime}}
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}
are monotone surjective morphisms which are furthermore induced by functions, i.e. they belong to
\mathbf{A}
. Therefore, by
Proposition 5.13
, there are a path
K=K(S,S^{\prime})
and morphisms
{\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}}\in\mathbf{A}_{K}^{S}\cap\mathbf{F}
and
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}}\in\mathbf{A}_{K}^{R^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\cap S^{\prime}}\cap\mathbf{F}

such that

{\sqsupset_{S,S^{\prime}}\circ\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}}={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}}.

If
(S,S^{\prime})\in\Gamma
and
(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\subseteq S^{\sqsubset}
, we define symmetrically
T=(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
and amalgamate

{\sqsupset_{S,S^{\prime}}}:={\sqsupset}\restriction T\times(T^{\sqsupset}\cap S)\text{ and }{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}}:={\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\restriction(T\times S^{\prime})

to obtain a path
K=K(S,S^{\prime})
and amalgamating functions
{\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}}\in\mathbf{A}_{K}^{T^{\sqsupset}\cap S}\cap\mathbf{F}\text{ and }{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}}\in\mathbf{A}_{K}^{S^{\prime}}\cap\mathbf{F}
.

Let
e_{S,S^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{E}K(S,S^{\prime})
be such that

e_{S,S^{\prime}}^{\vdash_{S,S^{\prime}}\circ\sqsubset_{S,S^{\prime}}}=e_{S,S^{\prime}}^{\vDash_{S,S^{\prime}}\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}}=\{0_{F}\}.

(If
S^{\sqsubset}=\{0_{F}\}=(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
, any end of
K(S,S^{\prime})
would do, otherwise,
e_{S,S^{\prime}}
is uniquely determined, as all morphisms are monotone.)

Let
K
be the fan whose spokes are the
K(S,S^{\prime})
, where the ends
e_{S,S^{\prime}}
are glued together. Let

{\dashv}=\bigcup_{(S,S^{\prime})\in\Gamma}\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}\text{ and }{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}=\bigcup_{(S,S^{\prime})\in\Gamma}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}.

It is routine to check that
\dashv
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}
give the required amalgamation, and that they are edge-preserving morphisms between fans. Further, as
\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}
are monotone,
\dashv
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}
are spoke-monotone.

We just have to show these morphisms belong to
\mathbf{L}
and, if
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
are in
\mathbf{X}
, then
\dashv
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}
are in
\mathbf{X}
too.

•

Since both
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
are co-bijective, for every
S
there is
S^{\prime}
such that
(S,S^{\prime})\in\Gamma
and therefore
\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}
surjects onto
S
. This shows that
\dashv
is surjective, and since
\dashv
is a function, it belongs to
\mathbf{B}
. Symmetry ensures that
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}
is surjective. This gives that
\mathbf{L}
has amalgamation.

•

If both
\sqsupset
and
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
are in
\mathbf{X}
, then by end-preservation, we have that
R=S^{\sqsubset}=(S^{\prime})^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}
is a spoke whenever
(S,S^{\prime})\in\Gamma
. In this case,
R^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\cap S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}
. This implies that each
\dashv_{S,S^{\prime}}
is surjecting onto
S
and each
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{S,S^{\prime}}
is surjecting onto
S^{\prime}
. Hence if
f
is an end of
K(S,S^{\prime})
then
f^{\vdash_{S,S^{\prime}}}
is an end in
S
(likewise, images of ends of
K(S,S^{\prime})
are ends in
S^{\prime}
), meaning that
\dashv
and
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}
are end-preserving, i.e. they belong to
\mathbf{X}
. This gives that
\mathbf{X}
has amalgamation.

This concludes the proof.
∎

By Propositions
5.29
and
4.30
, both
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{L}
have a Fraïssé sequence.

5.3.3.
Smooth fans as spectra

In this subsection we prove that every
smooth fan
, i.e. a subfan of the Cantor fan, is the spectrum of a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
.
For that we carefully define a particular sequence of covers, and use
Proposition 4.21
.

For every
n,k\in\omega
let
A_{n,k}
be the open interval
(k2^{-n}-2^{-n},k2^{-n}+2^{-n})
in the real line, i.e. the open ball with center
x_{n,k}=k2^{-n}
and radius
2^{-n}
.
Note that the sequence of covers
(\{A_{n,k}:0\leq k\leq 2^{n}\})_{n}
, when intersected with the unit interval
[0,1]
, yields an
\omega
-poset similar to
[
BBV25
, Example 2.16]
and
Figure 3
.

Observation 5.30
.

Our sequence of covers has the following structure.

(1)

We have
A_{n,k}\setminus\bigcup_{k^{\prime}\neq k}A_{n,k^{\prime}}=\{x_{n,k}\}
, i.e. the center is the unique point not covered by the other sets at the same level.
Moreover, if
x_{n,k}\in A_{m,j}
for some
m\leq n
, then
A_{n,k}\subseteq A_{m,j}
.

(2)

We have
A_{n,k}\supseteq A_{n+1,j}
if and only if
j\in\{2k-1,2k,2k+1\}
and in fact
A_{n,k}=A_{n+1,2k-1}\cup A_{n+1,2k}\cup A_{n+1,2k+1}
.

(3)

Given
A_{n+1,k}\subseteq A_{n,j}
, either
k
is even (
k=2k^{\prime}
) and
j=k^{\prime}
and the two sets share the center
x_{n+1,2k}=x_{n,k}
, or
k
is odd (
k=2k^{\prime}+1
) and
j\in\{k,k+1\}
and
A_{n+1,k}=A_{n,k^{\prime}}\cap A_{n,k^{\prime}+1}
.

We represent the Cantor space as
2^{\omega}
with the canonical basis
\{B_{s}:s\in 2^{<\omega}\}
, and we represent the Cantor fan as the quotient
X=([0,1]\times 2^{\omega})/(\{0\}\times 2^{\omega})
.
Let
\pi_{[0,1]}\colon X\to[0,1]
denote the canonical projection.
Let
\alpha,\beta\colon\omega\to\omega
be two strictly increasing functions, parametrizing our construction.
Finally, for every
n
we put

G_{n}=\{(A_{\alpha(n),0}\cap[0,1])\times B_{\emptyset}\}\cup\{(A_{\alpha(n),k}\cap[0,1])\times B_{s}:0<k\leq 2^{\alpha(n)},s\in 2^{\beta(n)}\},

i.e. we take the elementwise product of the
\alpha(n)
th
cover of the unit interval with the
\beta(n)
th
cover of the Cantor space, and we obtain a basic cover of the product
[0,1]\times 2^{\omega}
. Then we replace the sets
(A_{\alpha(n),0}\cap[0,1])\times B_{s}
,
s\in 2^{\beta(n)}
, with their union
(A_{\alpha(n),0}\cap[0,1])\times B_{\emptyset}
, which corresponds to gluing the endpoints and obtaining the Cantor fan.

We turn the sequence
(G_{n})
of sets into a lax-sequence of graphs and relational morphisms by putting
g\sqcap g^{\prime}
if and only if
g\cap g^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
in every
G_{n}
, and by putting
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}h
if and only if
g^{\in}\supseteq h^{\in}
for every
m\leq n
,
g\in G_{m}
,
h\in G_{n}
.

Lemma 5.31
.

(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is eventually a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
whose spectrum is the Cantor fan.

Proof.

It is easy to see that for
n\geq 2
,
G_{n}
is a fan with root
(A_{\alpha(n),0}\times B_{\emptyset})\cap X
and with
2^{\beta(n)}
spokes.
We use
Proposition 4.21
to obtain that
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{B}
with spectrum
X
, but we need to verify its hypotheses.

Every
G_{n}
is a minimal cover of
X
since
(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\setminus\bigcup_{(k^{\prime},s^{\prime})\neq(k,s)}(A_{\alpha(n),k^{\prime}}\times B_{s^{\prime}})=\{x_{\alpha(n),k}\}\times B_{s}
(see
Observation 5.30
), and that
x_{\alpha(n),k}\in[0,1]
if and only if
0\leq k\leq 2^{\alpha(n)}
.

G_{n}
consolidates
G_{n+1}
essentially because the cover
\{A_{m,k}\cap[0,1]\}_{0\leq k\leq 2^{m}}
consolidates
\{A_{m+1,k}\cap[0,1]\}_{0\leq k\leq 2^{m+1}}
, and
\{B_{s}\}_{s\in 2^{m}}
consolidates
\{B_{s}\}_{s\in 2^{m+1}}
.

It is easy to see that
G_{n}\cap G_{n+1}=\emptyset
.
Next, clearly,
\operatorname{diam}(A_{\alpha(n),k})
and
\operatorname{diam}(B_{s})
for
s\in 2^{\beta(n)}
depend only on
n
and go to
0
as
n\to\infty
, so every open cover of
[0,1]\times 2^{\omega}
is refined by a cover consisting of the products of the members of the
\alpha(n)
th
and
\beta(n)
th
levels of the respective sequences of covers.
It follows that every open cover of the quotient
X
is refined by some
G_{n}
.
Altogether we have verified all hypotheses of
Proposition 4.21
.

Finally, the every
\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}
is spoke-monotone and end-preserving, and so
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
.
Every spoke of
G_{n}
is of the form

S_{n,s}=\{(A_{\alpha(n),0}\times B_{\emptyset})\cap X\}\cup\{(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap X:0<k\leq 2^{\alpha(n)}\}

for some
s\in 2^{\beta(n)}
.
Hence,
S_{n+1,s}{}^{\sqsubset^{n}_{n+1}}=S_{n,s\restriction\beta(n)}
, and
\sqsupset^{n}_{n+1}\restriction(S_{n+1,s}\times S_{n,s\restriction\beta(n)})
is monotone – it is essentially the composition of the refinement relations
\dashv^{m}_{m+1}
on
\{A_{m,k}\cap[0,1]\}_{k\leq 2^{m}}\times\{A_{m+1,k}\cap[0,1]\}_{k\leq 2^{m+1}}
, which are monotone as every
\dashv^{m}_{m+1}
is the result of a variant of the edge-splitting construction (
Lemma 3.12
).
∎

Next, for every subfan
Y\subseteq X
of the Cantor fan and
n\in\omega
, we put
\beta(n)=n
for every
n
and we pick suitable
\alpha
(below).
Then we put

H_{n}=\{g\cap Y:g\in G_{n},\ x_{g}\in\pi_{[0,1]}(g\cap Y)\}

where
x_{g}
denotes the center
x_{n,k}
of the unique set
A_{n,k}
such that
g=(A_{n,k}\times B_{s})\cap X
, and define the corresponding lax-sequence of graphs
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
.
Without loss of generality, we may identify every element
h\in H_{n}
with the corresponding element
g\in G_{n}
such that
h=g\cap X
, so that
H_{n}\subseteq G_{n}
.

We inductively define
\alpha
depending on
Y
as follows.
We put
\alpha(0)=0
and given
\alpha(n)
we pick
\alpha(n+1)>\alpha(n)
large enough so that whenever
g\cap Y\in H_{n}
for some
g=(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap X\in G_{n}
, then for both
i=0,1
, if there is a spoke
[0,y]\times\{f\}
of
Y
for some
y\leq 1
and
f\in 2^{\omega}
with
f\supseteq s^{\smallfrown}i
, then there is such a spoke with
y\geq x_{g}-2^{\alpha(n)}+2^{\alpha(n+1)}
, so for the leftmost set
g^{\prime}=(A_{\alpha(n+1),k^{\prime}}\times B_{s^{\smallfrown}i})\cap X\in G_{n+1}
contained in
g
we have
g^{\prime}\cap Y\in H_{n+1}
.

Lemma 5.32
.

There is
n_{0}
such that above
n_{0}
we have the following.

(1)

H_{n}
is a fan with
0_{n}\cap Y=0_{n}
.

(2)

For every
g\in G_{n}
there is
h\in H_{n}
such that
g\cap Y\subseteq h
, and so
H_{n}
is a minimal cover of
Y
.

(3)

For every
n\geq m
,
g\in G_{n}
,
g^{\prime}\in G_{m}
such that
g\cap Y\in H_{n}
and
g^{\prime}\cap Y\in H_{m}
we have
g\subseteq g^{\prime}
if and only if
g\cap Y\subseteq g^{\prime}\cap Y
.
If
n=m
, we also have
g\cap g^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
g\cap g^{\prime}\cap Y\neq\emptyset
.
Altogether,
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is a lax-restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
.

(4)

The sequence of covers
(H_{n})
is consolidating.

(5)

(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
with spectrum
Y
, and it is the unique co-bijective modification of the prime restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
induced by the traces
[Y]_{n}
.

Proof.

Claim
(1)
.
Clearly,
0_{n}\cap Y\in H_{n}
.
If
(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap Y\in H_{n}
, then also
(A_{\alpha(n),k^{\prime}}\times B_{s})\cap Y\in H_{n}
for every
0<k^{\prime}<k
since
\pi_{[0,1]}(([0,1]\times B_{s})\cap Y)
is connected.
Hence,
H_{n}
is a fan as long as
\{s\in 2^{n}:(\{x_{\alpha(n),1}\}\times B_{s})\cap Y\neq\emptyset\}
has at least three elements, which happens for every
n\geq n_{0}
for suitable
n_{0}
.

For
(2)
let
g\in G_{n}
.
Without loss of generality,
g=(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap X
for some
k>0
(as
(A_{\alpha(n),0}\times B_{\emptyset})\cap Y\in H_{n}
) and
s\in 2^{n}
.
If
g\cap Y\neq\emptyset
but
x_{g}\notin\pi_{[0,1]}(g\cap Y)
, then
(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap Y\subseteq(A_{\alpha(n),k-1}\times B_{s})
and
x_{\alpha(n),k-1}\in\pi_{[0,1]}((A_{\alpha(n),k-1}\times B_{s})\cap Y)
as
Y
is connected.
Hence,
g\cap Y
is covered by
g^{\prime}\cap Y\in H_{n}
where
g^{\prime}
is the unique vertex of
G_{n}
adjacent to
g
.
It follows that
H_{n}
is a cover of
Y
.
The minimality follows from the definition of the covers
H_{n}
as
\{x_{\alpha(n),k}\}\times B_{s}
is the part of
A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s}
not covered by the other sets
A_{\alpha(n),k^{\prime}}\times B_{s^{\prime}}
.

In
(3)
, we have
g=(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap X
and
g^{\prime}=(A_{\alpha(m),k^{\prime}}\times B_{s^{\prime}})\cap X
for suitable
k,k^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}
.
Suppose
g\cap Y\subseteq g^{\prime}\cap Y
.
Then clearly,
s\supseteq s^{\prime}
.
We also have
x_{\alpha(n),k}\in\pi_{[0,1]}(g\cap Y)\subseteq A_{m,k^{\prime}}
, and so
A_{\alpha(n),k}\subseteq A_{\alpha(m),k^{\prime}}
by
Observation 5.30
.
Hence,
g\subseteq g^{\prime}
.

Next, suppose
n=m
,
k<k^{\prime}
, and
g\cap g^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
Then
s\in\{s^{\prime},\emptyset\}
,
k^{\prime}=k+1
, and
Y
contains a spoke
[0,y]\times\{f\}
for some
f\in 2^{\omega}
such that
f\supseteq s^{\prime}
and
y\geq x_{\alpha(n),k^{\prime}}>x_{\alpha(n),k}
.
Hence,
g\cap g^{\prime}\cap Y\supseteq(A_{\alpha(n),k}\cap A_{\alpha(n),k+1}\cap[0,y])\times\{f\}=(x_{\alpha(n),k},x_{\alpha(n),k+1})\times\{f\}\neq\emptyset
.
Therefore, we may identify
H_{n}
with an induced subgraph of
G_{n}
.

For
(4)
, let
(y,f)\in[0,1]\times 2^{\omega}
be a point of
(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap Y\in H_{n}
.
Let
i\in\{0,1\}
be such that
f\supseteq s^{\smallfrown}i
, and let
k^{\prime}\in\omega
be the greatest such that
x_{\alpha(n+1),k^{\prime}}\leq y
.
We put
h^{\prime}=(A_{\alpha(n+1),k^{\prime}}\times B_{s^{\smallfrown}i})\cap Y\in H_{n+1}
.
Then we have
(y,f)\in h^{\prime}
.
Let
k^{\prime\prime}\in\omega
be the smallest such that
A_{\alpha(n+1),k^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq A_{\alpha(n),k}
, and put
h^{\prime\prime}=(A_{\alpha(n+1),k^{\prime\prime}}\times B_{s^{\smallfrown}i})\cap Y
.
From the defining property of
\alpha
we have
\alpha
we have
h^{\prime\prime}\in H_{n+1}
.
If
k^{\prime}\geq k^{\prime\prime}
, then we have
(y,f)\in h^{\prime}\subseteq g
.
Otherwise, we have
k^{\prime\prime}=k^{\prime}+1
and
(y,f)\in h^{\prime\prime}\subseteq g
.

Claim
(5)
.
Let us first show that
H_{n}\cap H_{n+1}=\emptyset
.
Every
h=(A_{\alpha(n),k}\times B_{s})\cap Y\in H_{n}
contains a point
(x_{h},f)
for some
f\in 2^{\omega}
with
f\supseteq s^{\smallfrown}i
.
Then
h^{\prime}=(A_{\alpha(n+1),2^{\alpha(n+1)-\alpha(n)}k}\times B_{s^{\smallfrown}i})\cap Y
is the only member of
H_{n+1}
containing the point
(x_{h},f)=(x_{h^{\prime}},f)
.
But
(x_{\alpha(n+1),2^{\alpha(n+1)-\alpha(n)}k-1},f)\in h\setminus h^{\prime}
.

Next,
\max\{\operatorname{diam}(h):h\in H_{n}\}\to 0
as
n\to 0
since
H_{n}
refines
G_{n}
.
This together with
(2)
,
(4)
, and the previous paragraph are the hypotheses of
Proposition 4.21
, and so
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is a sequence, it is in
\mathbf{B}
, and
Y
is its spectrum.
By
(3)
,
(H_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is a restriction of
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
.
By
(1)
, every
H_{n}
is a fan.
It is easy to see that every
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n}
maps spokes into spokes, and so it is spoke-monotone by
Lemma 3.13
.
Hence,
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is in
\mathbf{L}
.

Let
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
be the prime restriction of
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
induced by the traces
[Y]_{n}
.
For every
n
, the trace
[Y]_{n}
is the cover
\{g\cap Y:g\in G_{n},g\cap Y\neq\emptyset\}
.
By
(2)
,
[Y]_{n+1}
refines
H_{n+1}
and so
H_{n}
. It follows that
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is a dense restriction of
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
.
Since for every
g\cap Y\in H_{n}
,
g
is the unique element of
G_{n}
containing the point
(x_{g},f)
of
Y
for suitable
f\in 2^{\omega}
,
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
is the smallest dense restriction of
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$=$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
and so its unique co-bijective modification.
∎

Corollary 5.33
.

The Cantor fan is the spectrum of a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
.
Every smooth fan is the spectrum of a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
.
∎

5.3.4.
The Cantor fan

As in
§​​ 5.1
and
§​​ 5.2
, we intend to characterise Fraïssé and lax-Fraïssé sequences in
\mathbf{X}
.

First of all, by
Theorem 4.34
, a lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{X}
has an edge-witnessing subsequence. Differently from the case of the interval, this does not suffice:

Example 5.34
.

For
n\geq 2
, let
G_{n}
be a fans with
2^{n}
spokes, such that the first
2^{n}-1
spokes have length
2^{n}
, and the last spoke has length
2
. Viewing each set
\{0,\ldots,2^{n}-1\}
as a path (where
i\sqcap j
if and only if
|i-j|\leq 1
), let
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}}\subseteq\{0,\ldots,2^{n}-1\}\times\{0,\ldots,2^{n+1}-1\}
be a monotone edge-witnessing morphism in
\mathbf{A}
such that
0^{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}}=\{0\}
. For each spoke of
G_{n}
of length
2^{n}
, we associate two spokes of
G_{n+1}
of length
2^{n+1}
via
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n}
, while to the spoke of
G_{n}
of length
2
, we associate one spoke of the
G_{n+1}
of length
2^{n+1}
via a monotone edge-witnessing morphism in
\mathbf{A}
, and the spoke of
G_{n+1}
of length
2
via the identity. Gluing appropriately such morphisms gives an edge-witnessing
{\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}}\in\mathbf{X}
. The sequence
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
cannot be lax-Fraïssé, as it is not even cofinal:
if
F
is the fan with
3
spokes each of length
3
, there is no
n
such that
\mathbf{X}^{F}_{G_{n}}\neq\emptyset
.

Recall that, in
\mathbf{X}
, branches of spokes coincide with their surjective cores, as morphisms are end-preserving.
Example 5.34
shows that, for a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
, having an edge-witnessing subsequence and the fact that every branch of spokes has an edge-witnessing subsequence are not the same concept. This motivates the following:

Definition 5.35
.

A morphism
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{X}_{F}^{G}
is
spokewise edge-witnessing
if for every
S\in\ast F
the restriction
{\sqsupset}\restriction S^{\sqsubset}\times S
is edge-witnessing. We define
spokewise star-refining
analogously.

Proposition 5.36
.

In
\mathbf{X}
we have the following.

(1)

Every spokewise star-refining/edge-witnessing morphism is star-refining/edge-witnessing;

(2)

Every star-refining morphism is already spokewise star-refining;

(3)

Every spokewise star-refining morphism is spokewise edge-witnessing;

(4)

The composition of two spokewise edge-witnessing morphisms is (spokewise) star-refining.

(5)

Spokewise edge-witnessing/star-refining morphism form lax-closed ideals.

Proof.

The first implications are obvious. For the second one, note that the star of any non root point is contained in a single spoke, and so is its image, so the only point which may cause problems is the root. On the other hand, the image of the root is only the root.

To show that spokewise star-refining morphisms are spokewise edge-witnessing, note that this is the case when restricting to pairs of spokes, as one is dealing with maps in
\mathbf{A}
(
Proposition 3.6
). The same argument, together with
Proposition 3.5
, gives that composing spokewise edge-witnessing morphisms gives (spokewise) star-refining ones. Once again, the last statements follows from that we are truly handling glued morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
, and the fact that the original properties form lax-closed ideals (
Lemma 3.3
and
Proposition 3.4
).
∎

Proposition 5.37
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a (spokewise) star-refining subsequence;

(2)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a spokewise edge-witnessing subsequence;

(3)

every branch of spokes is lax-Fraïssé in
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

The equivalence between
(1)
and
(2)
follows directly from
Proposition 5.36
.
Since spokes are nondegenerate paths, all branches of spokes are nontrivial, and hence a branch of spokes is lax-Fraïssé if and only if it has a star-refining subsequence, by
Theorem 5.15
.
Clearly, if
(G_{n_{k}})
is a spokewise star-refining subsequence, then
(S_{n_{k}})
is a star-refining subsequence of
\bar{S}
for every branch of spokes
\bar{S}
.
To prove the equivalence of
(1)
and
(3)
, we are left to show that if all branches of spokes have star-refining subsequences, then there exists a spokewise star-refining subsequence.
Suppose former.
We will show that for every
m\in\omega
there is
n\geq m
such that
\sqsupset^{m}_{n}
is spokewise star-refining.
Otherwise there is
m
such that the family

\textstyle T=\{S\in\bigcup_{n\geq m}\ast G_{n}:{\sqsupset}\restriction S^{\sqsubset^{m}_{n}}\times S\text{ is not star-refining}\}

intersects every
\ast G_{n}
,
n\geq m
.
Note that as star-refining morphisms form an ideal, if a spoke
S\in\ast G_{n}
is a member of
T
, then so is every spoke
S^{\sqsubset^{k}_{n}}
for
m\leq k\leq n
.
Since the tree of spokes is finitely branching, it follows that there is a branch of spokes
\bar{S}
such that
S_{n}\in T
for every
n\geq m
, and so
\bar{S}
has no star-refining subsequence.
∎

Recall, for a morphism
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{X}
, its image under the end functor
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset)
was defined before
Definition 5.23
. If
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
,
(\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset_{n}^{m}))
is a sequence in
\mathbf{D}
.

Lemma 5.38
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
. The following are equivalent.

•

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-splitting subsequence;

•

(\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset_{n}^{m}))
is Fraïssé in
\mathbf{D}
.

If further the spectrum is connected and Hausdorff, then the above are equivalent to

•

\mathsf{E}X
is homeomorphic to the Cantor space.

Proof.

Clearly,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an end-splitting subsequence if and only if
(\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset^{m}_{n}))
has an anti-injective subsequence, which, by
Theorem 5.2
, is equivalent to
(\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset^{m}_{n}))
being Fraïssé in
\mathbf{D}
and to its spectrum being the Cantor space.
Under the extra hypotheses, by
Proposition 5.26
(3)
,
\mathsf{E}X
is the spectrum of
(\mathsf{E}({\sqsupset}^{m}_{n}))
.
∎

Theorem 5.39
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{X}
;

(2)

every branch of spokes is lax-Fraïssé in
\mathbf{A}
and
(\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset_{n}^{m}))
is Fraïssé in
\mathbf{D}
;

(3)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a star-refining end-splitting subsequence;

(4)

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is homeomorphic to the Cantor fan.

Moreover,
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is Fraïssé precisely when every branch of spokes
\bar{S}
is Fraïssé in
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3)
follows from
Proposition 5.37
and
Lemma 5.38
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(3)
: Since star-refining end-splitting morphisms form a wide ideal in
\mathbf{X}
(by
Proposition 3.4
and
Lemma 5.27
for star-refining, and
Lemma 5.40
for end-splitting),
Proposition 4.31
gives that any lax-Fraïssé sequence has a star-refining end-splitting subsequence.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1)
: Fix
m
, a fan
F
, and
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{X}_{F}^{G_{m}}
. Let
M=\max\{|S|:S\in*F\}
. By applying condition
(3)
of
Proposition 5.37
, to every spoke of
G_{m}
, and using that
(\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset_{n}^{m}))
is a Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{D}
, we can find
n
large enough such that

•

there is
{\dashv}\in\mathbf{D}_{\mathsf{E}G_{n}}^{\mathsf{E}F}
such that
\mathsf{E}(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})=\mathsf{E}({\sqsupset})\circ{\dashv}
, and

•

if
S\in*G_{m}
and
T\in*G_{n}
are such that
T^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}=S
, the morphism
{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\restriction S\times T
can be written as a composition of
M
edge-witnessing morphisms in
\mathbf{A}
.

As in the proof of
Theorem 5.15
, we get that for every adjacent
g,h\in G_{m}
,
M\leq|\{g,h\}_{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}|
, as each two adjacent vertices belong to the same spoke.

Fix
S\in*G_{m}
and
T\in*G_{n}
such that
T^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}=S
. Let
e\in\mathsf{E}T\setminus\{0_{n}\}
, and let
S^{\prime}\in*F
be such that
e^{\vdash}\subseteq S^{\prime}
. We now apply
Proposition 5.12
to
P=S^{\prime}
,
Q=T
,
R=S
, and we get that there is
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{ST}}\in\mathbf{A}_{T}^{S^{\prime}}
such that

{({\sqsupset}\restriction S\times S^{\prime})}\circ{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{ST}}\subseteq{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\restriction S\times T.

Note that
0_{n}^{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{ST}}=\{0_{F}\}
, and hence we can glue together all morphisms of the form
\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}_{ST}
, for relevant
S
and
T
, and get a morphism
{\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\subseteq F\times G_{n}
such that
{\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\mathchoice{\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\textstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\vDash$}}{\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vDash$}}}}\subseteq{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}
.
This shows that the sequence
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is lax-Fraïssé, and proves
(1)
.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(3)
: If
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is homeomorphic to Cantor fan, it is Hausdorff and connected, and its set of endpoints is homeomorphic to the Cantor space.
Therefore,
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining subsequence by
Corollary 4.27
and an end-splitting subsequence by
Lemma 5.38
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(4)
: By
Proposition 4.32
, all spectra of posets induced by lax-Fraïssé sequences are homeomorphic. Since the sequence given in
Lemma 5.31
is lax-Fraïssé (it satisfies, for example, the requirements of
(3)
), we have the thesis.

Checking the last statement is an easy generalisation of the fact that for sequences in
\mathbf{A}
, being Fraïssé is precisely equivalent to having a strictly anti-injective sequence (see
Proposition 5.13
).
∎

5.3.5.
The Lelek fan

We now focus on the category
\mathbf{L}
and to the study the spectrum of the poset induced by a Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{L}
.

We call a morphism
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{G}^{H}

•

end-dense
if every
h\in H
is above some
e\in\mathsf{E}G
, i.e.
e\sqsubset h
.

•

end-splitting
if every
e\in\mathsf{E}H
is above two distinct
g
and
g^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{E}G
.

End-dense morphisms are as far as possible from morphisms in
\mathbf{X}
.

Lemma 5.40
.

Both end-dense and end-splitting morphisms form a wide ideal in
\mathbf{L}
. Also, end-splitting morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{X}
(and so form a wide ideal in it).

Furthermore, if
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
, then

(1)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-dense subsequence if and only if for every
m
and
g\in G_{m}
there are
n\geq m
and
h\in\mathsf{E}G_{n}
such that
h\sqsubset_{n}^{m}g
;

(2)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-splitting subsequence if and only if for every
m
and
g\in\mathsf{E}G_{m}
there are
n\geq m
and two distinct
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}G_{n}
such that
h,h^{\prime}\sqsubset_{n}^{m}g
;

(3)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-dense end-splitting subsequence if and only if for every
m
and
g\in G_{m}
there are
n\geq m
and distinct
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}G_{n}
such that
h,h^{\prime}\sqsubset_{n}^{m}g
.

Proof.

We leave to the reader the easy verification that end-dense/end-splitting morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{L}
. Since ‘doubling’ the spokes in an obvious way is an end-splitting morphism in
\mathbf{X}
, end-splitting morphisms are wide in
\mathbf{X}
.

To show these classes of morphisms form ideals, fix fans
F
,
G
, and
H
, and morphisms
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{G}^{F}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}\in\mathbf{L}_{H}^{G}
.

If
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is end-dense, fix
f\in F
and let
g\in G
with
g\sqsubset f
. Fix now
h\in\mathsf{E}F
such that
h\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}g
, so that
h\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}g\sqsubset f
. As
f
was arbitrary,
\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is end-dense. If
\sqsupset
is end-dense, fix again
f\in F
, and pick
g\in\mathsf{E}G
with
g\sqsubset f
. Since
g
is an end in
G
, we can find
h\in\mathsf{E}H
such that
h\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}g
, so that
h\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsubset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsubset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}g\sqsubset f
, which shows that
\sqsupset\circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is end-dense.

Similarly, if
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}
is end-splitting, fix
f\in\mathsf{E}F
. Fix distinct
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathsf{E}G
which are below
f
. Since every end in
G
is above (at least) one end in
h
and
h^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{E}G
which are respectively below
g
and
g^{\prime}
, and therefore
f
. On the other hand, if
\sqsupset
is end-splitting, any end of
F
is above at least one end of
G
, which is in turn above two distinct ends in
H
. This shows
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}\circ\sqsupset
is end-splitting. (As
\mathbf{X}
is a subcategory of
\mathbf{L}
in which end-splitting morphisms are wide, end-splitting morphisms are a wide ideal in
\mathbf{X}
too.)

Fix now a sequence
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
in
\mathbf{L}
. In light of
Lemma 4.6
, to prove
(1)
one just has to prove that

\forall m\in\mathbb{N}\,\forall g\in G_{m}\,\exists n\geq m\,\exists h\in\mathsf{E}G_{n}\,(h\sqsubset_{n}^{m}g)

is equivalent to its uniform version, that is

\forall m\in\mathbb{N}\,\exists n\geq m\,\forall g\in G_{m}\,\exists h\in\mathsf{E}G_{n}\,(h\sqsubset_{n}^{m}g).

One implication is obvious. For the other one, fix
m
and for every
g\in G_{m}
find
n_{g}\geq m
and
h_{g}\in\mathsf{E}G_{n_{g}}
which is below
g
. Let
n=\max_{g\in G_{m}}n_{g}
. Since each
\sqsupset_{n}^{n_{g}}
is end-surjective (
Lemma 5.22
) we can find
k_{g}\in\mathsf{E}G_{n}
below each
h_{g}
. This concludes the proof. The proofs of
(2)
and
(3)
are analogous and left to the reader.
∎

Lemma 5.41
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
such that its spectrum
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is connected and Hausdorff.
Then
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-dense subsequence if and only if the set of endpoints is dense in
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition 5.26
, the endpoints of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
are exactly the selectors
(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}
where
\bar{S}
is a nondegenerate branch of spokes.

Let us first show the forward direction.
As
\{p^{\in}\}_{p\in\mathbb{P}}
is a base for the topology of
X
, we just need to check that every
p^{\in}
contains an endpoint.
Fix
p
and
n
such that
p\in G_{n}
.
Since
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-dense subsequence, by
Lemma 5.40
we can find
k\geq n
,
S_{k}\in*G_{k}
, and
e_{k}\in\mathsf{E}G_{k}\cap S_{k}
such that
e_{k}\sqsubset^{n}_{k}p
.
By
Lemma 5.25
,
S_{k}
can be extended to a nondegenerate branch of spokes
\bar{S}=(S_{n},\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})
with
(\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}^{m}_{n})_{n\geq m\geq k}
in
\mathbf{A}
.
Hence,
e_{\bar{S},k}=e_{k}\leq p
, and so
p^{\in}
contains the endpoint
(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}
.

Vice versa, assume that the endpoints are dense.
Fix
m\in\omega
and
g\in G_{m}
.
As
(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
is co-bijective,
g^{\in}
is non-empty, and so contains the endpoint
(e_{\bar{S},n})^{\leq}
for some nondegenerate branch of spokes
\bar{S}
where
S_{n}=[0_{n},s_{n}]
. Hence,
g\sqsupset^{m}_{n}e_{\bar{S},n}
for some
n\geq m
.

Since
(e_{\bar{S},n})
is a thread of ends of the co-bijective modification of the surjective core
([0_{n},t_{n}])
of
\bar{S}
, by condition
(2)
of
Proposition 5.19
there is
k\geq n
such that
t_{k}\sqsubset_{k}^{n}e_{\bar{S},n}
. Now, fix
k^{\prime}\geq k
such that
S_{k^{\prime}}{}^{\sqsubset_{k^{\prime}}^{k}}=[0_{k},t_{k}]
. If
t_{k^{\prime}}{}^{\sqsubset_{k^{\prime}}^{k}}=\{t_{k}\}
, then every
h\in[t_{k}^{\prime},s_{k^{\prime}}]
must satisfy the same, otherwise
t_{k^{\prime}}
would separate
[0_{k},t_{k})^{\sqsupset_{k^{\prime}}^{k}}
. In particular if this is the case
s_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubset_{k^{\prime}}^{k}t_{k}\sqsubset_{k}^{m}g
. Otherwise,
{\sqsupset_{k}^{k}}\restriction[0_{k},t_{k}]\times[0_{k^{\prime}},t_{k^{\prime}}]
is not co-bijective, hence
t_{k}\sim e_{\bar{S},k}
, and therefore
s_{k^{\prime}}\sqsubset_{k^{\prime}}^{k}e_{\bar{S},k}
. This is the thesis.
∎

Remark 5.42
.

Having seen the Cantor fan as the Fraïssé limit of
\mathbf{X}
, we have a second way to see the Lelek fan as a limit of a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
. Since this is not needed, thanks to
Corollary 5.33
, we just roughly sketch this argument below.

We pick a sequence in
\mathbf{X}
defined recursively as follows:
G_{0}
is the claw. If
G_{n}
has been defined, and it has spokes
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}
, we let
G_{n+1}
be a fan with
\sum_{i\leq k}|S_{i}|
many spokes, where the
(\sum_{i<m}|S_{i}|+j)
-th spoke, for
m\leq k
and
j\leq|S_{m}|
, is
\mathsf{X}S_{i}
(for
m=1
we set
\sum_{i<m}|S_{i}|=0
). To define the morphism
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
, we glue together the morphisms
\in_{S_{i}}
between the
(\sum_{i<m}|S_{i}|+j)
-th spoke, for
m\leq k
and
j\leq|S_{m}|
, and
S_{i}
. This sequence is lax-Fraïssé in
\mathbf{X}
, and therefore has the Cantor fan as its spectrum.

Our intention is to define subsets
H_{n}\subseteq G_{n}
giving a prime restriction with the Lelek fan as its spectrum. Let
H_{0}=G_{0}
. If
H_{n}
has been defined, re-enumerate the spokes of
H_{n}
so that
T_{i}\subseteq S_{i}
be the
i
-th spoke of
H_{n}
. For each
i
, list
S_{i}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{|S_{i}|}\}
in such a way that elements with consecutive indexes are adjacent and
x_{1}=0_{n}
. Let
m_{i}
be the natural such that
T_{i}=[x_{1},x_{m_{i}}]
. For a fixed
i
, list all the spokes which are
\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}
predecessors of
S_{i}
in
G_{n+1}
as
U_{1,i},\ldots,U_{|S_{i}|,i}
. For
j\leq m_{i}
, let
W_{j,i}=\{x\in U_{j}:x^{\sqsubset_{n+1}^{n}}\subseteq[x_{1},x_{j}]\}
, and if
j>m_{i}
, let
W_{j,i}=\{x\in U_{j}:x^{\sqsubset_{n+1}^{n}}\subseteq[x_{1},x_{m_{i}}]\}
. Setting
H_{n+1}=\bigcup_{i,j}W_{i,j}
and
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{n+1}^{n}}={\sqsupset_{n+1}^{n}}\restriction H_{n}\times H_{n+1}
gives the required relations, in light of
Lemma 5.41
.

Since star-refining/end-dense morphisms form wide ideals in
\mathbf{L}
, lax-Fraïssé sequences must have star-refining and end-dense subsequence. The condition on the sequence of ends being Fraïssé, needed in the case of
\mathbf{X}
, is in this case replaced by asking for an end-splitting subsequence.

Theorem 5.43
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{L}
. The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{L}
;

(2)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a star-refining end-dense end-splitting subsequence;

(3)

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is homeomorphic to the Lelek fan.

The sequence
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is Fraïssé precisely when the surjective core of every branch of spokes has a strictly anti-injective subsequence.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
: This follows from that star-refining end-dense end-splitting morphisms form a wide ideal in
\mathbf{L}
and
Proposition 4.31
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
: Fix
m
, a fan
F
, and assume that
{\sqsupset}\in\mathbf{L}_{F}^{G_{m}}
. Let
M=\max_{S\in*F}|S|
, and let
n
such that

•

\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is a composition of
M
star-refining morphisms, and

•

every vertex of
G_{m}
has below at least
|F|
elements of
\mathsf{E}G_{n}
.

For the first condition, we use that
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
has a star-refining subsequence.
For the second one, first find
m^{\prime}
such that
\sqsupset_{m^{\prime}}^{m}
is end-dense and then find
n
large enough so that
|g^{\sqsupset_{n}^{m^{\prime}}}\cap\mathsf{E}G_{n}|\geq|F|
for all
g\in\mathsf{E}G_{m^{\prime}}
.

Now, fix a vertex
g\in G_{m}
. By construction, we have that

|\{S\in\ast F:S^{\sqsubset}=[0_{n},g]\}|\leq|\{S\in\ast G_{n}:S^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}=[0_{n},g]|,

therefore there is a surjective function
f\colon{\ast G_{n}}\to\ast F
with the property that
S^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}=f(S)^{\sqsubset}
. For every
S\in\ast G_{n}
, consider

{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\restriction S^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}\times S\quad\text{ and }\quad{\sqsupset}\restriction f(S)^{\sqsubset}\times S.

Since
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is a composition of
M
star-refining morphisms and by the choice of
M
, we can find
{\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}}_{S}\in\mathbf{A}_{S}^{f(S)}
such that

{{({\sqsupset}\restriction f(S)^{\sqsubset}\times f(S))}\ \circ\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S}}\subseteq{\sqsupset_{n}^{m}}\restriction S^{\sqsubset_{n}^{m}}\times S.

Gluing all the
\mathrel{\vphantom{\sqsupset}\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\sqsupset$\cr$-$\cr}}}}_{S}
at the root of
G_{n}
gives a morphism (in
\mathbf{X}
) from
G_{n}
to
F
, which amalgamates, proving that
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is lax-Fraïssé.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(2)
. By
Lemma 5.41
, since endpoints are dense in the Lelek fan,
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has an end-dense subsequence. Further, since in every non-empty open set there are at least two endpoints of the Lelek fan, then every element of
\mathbb{P}
has at least two ends below. Lastly, since the Lelek is Hausdorff,
(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
has a star-refining subsequence.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)
: Since the Lelek fan is a smooth fan,
Corollary 5.33
gives a given sequence in
\mathbf{L}
having the Lelek fan as spectrum. Since
(3)
\Rightarrow
(2)
, this sequence is lax-Fraïssé. Since all lax-Fraïssé sequence give homeomorphic spectra, any given lax-Fraïssé sequence must have the Lelek fan as a spectrum.

The proof of the last statement (that Fraïssé sequence correspond to those which have strictly anti-injective subsequences) is the same as in the case of
\mathbf{A}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.44
.

We should note that our proof gives in fact as a byproduct the uniqueness of the Lelek fan. If we start with two distinct Lelek fans
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
(i.e. smooth fans whose endpoints are dense), by
Corollary 5.33
we obtain two sequences in
\mathbf{L}
having
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
as spectra, respectively. By
Theorem 5.43
, both these sequences are lax-Fraïssé, and therefore these have homeomorphic spectra. We conclude that Lelek fans are unique.

6.
Concluding remarks

6.1.
Other easy examples

There are certainly other categories with amalgamation we might have considered, which give rise to other interesting continua. In these cases, one can characterise lax-Fraïssé sequence in a similar fashion to what we did in the case of the arc and for fans. Since the proofs are similar, we only sketch the non obvious part of the arguments, leaving to the reader to sort out the details from our previous work.

For example, recall that a graph is a cycle if every vertex has degree exactly
2
. Let
\mathbf{C}
be the category whose objects are cycles and whose morphisms are monotone relations in
\mathbf{B}
(note that, once the cycles are long enough, co-injectivity follows directly from monotonicity, by the same reason that in the path of setting co-injectivity only fails at ends, see
Lemma 5.18
).
A cycle with three vertices is a weakly terminal object.
One can show that this category has amalgamation, and therefore Fraïssé sequences.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
(G_{n},\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
be a sequence in
\mathbf{C}
with induced poset
\mathbb{P}
.
The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\sqsupset_{n}^{m})
is a lax-Fraïssé sequence in
\mathbf{C}
;

(2)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has an edge-witnessing subsequence;

(3)

(\sqsupset^{m}_{n})
has a star-refining subsequence;

(4)

\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is homeomorphic to the circle.

A lax-Fraïssé sequence is Fraïssé precisely when it also has a strictly anti-injective subsequence.

Sketch of the proof.

The equivalences between
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
are exactly as in the proof of
Theorem 5.15
, as we are dealing with triangle-free graphs (as long as
|G_{n}|\geq 4
) and monotone maps.
To show that
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
is the circle, we choose a suitable sequence of minimal covers an apply Proposition
4.21
.
This can be done by arranging open balls of smaller and smaller radius around roots of the unit.
For example, let
C_{n,k}
be the intersection of the unit circle in
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
and of the open ball with center
c_{n,k}=e^{(2k\pi i)/2^{n}}
and radius such that the boundary contains the neighboring centers
c_{n,k+1},c_{n,k-1}
.

To show that a sequence of cycles without an edge-witnessing subsequence cannot give the circle as the spectrum of its associated poset, note that if
G_{m}
is a cycle and there is no
n\geq m
such that
\sqsupset_{n}^{m}
is edge-witnessing, then there is a pair of adjacent vertices
g,g^{\prime}\in G_{m}
such that
g^{\in}
and
g^{\prime\in}
are disjoint open sets. This gives either that the spectrum is disconnected (in the case
\wedge
divides
G_{m}
in at least two connected components), or a minimal cover of
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
which is a chain.
Since morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
are edge-surjective,
m
could be arbitrarily large, and therefore
\mathsf{S}\mathbb{P}
cannot be the circle.
∎

When we do not consider only monotone maps, and we allow cycles to wrap and unwrap multiple times, we expect again a category with amalgamation (this can easily be checked by hand). In this case, the spectrum of the poset associated to a lax-Fraïssé sequence should depend on how many times we allow our cycles to wrap. We speculate that
P
-adic solenoids, where
P
is a set of primes, can be obtained this way.

6.2.
The pseudoarc

We are left to comment the elephant in the room from
§​​ 5.2
, that is, the category
\mathbf{P}
whose objects are paths and morphisms are co-bijective edge-preserving relations, i.e., elements of
\mathbf{B}
.

Since the clique graph of a path is a path,
Corollary 4.43
gives that
\mathbf{P}
is clique-closed. By
Corollary 4.39
, to check amalgamation for
\mathbf{P}
it thus suffices to check amalgamation for surjective edge-preserving functions between paths. This was done in
[
IS06
]
, hence
\mathbf{P}
is a Fräissé category. What about its Fräissé limit?

Intuition (and
[
IS06
]
, and subsequent work) suggests that the answer here is probably one of the most interesting one-dimensional continua, Bing’s pseudoarc. This is a planar continuum which can be obtained as the intersection of a sequence of chains that get nested into each other in a crooked way. The pseudoarc was introduced by Bing in
[
Bin48
]
, and since then it has received lot of attention, being in some sense very close to an arc yet very intricate. As mentioned, the pseudoarc was treated as a projective Fraïssé limit by Irwin and Solecki in
[
IS06
]
(indeed, this was the first example of a topological space viewed from a Fraïssé-theoretic point of view), and by Bartoš and Kubiś in
[
BK
]
in the approximate setting.

This intuition was formalised by the second author and Malicki, who in
[
BM
, §4]
deeply analysed the notions of tangled morphisms and posets from
[
BBV25
, §2]
to show that Bing’s pseudoarc is indeed the Fraïssé limit of the category
\mathbf{P}
. This approach was subsequently used to reprove the existence and the uniqueness of the pseudoarc and its homogeneity, and moreover to show that the autohomeomorphisms of the pseudoarc have a dense conjugacy class.
In particular, Theorem 4.12 in
[
BM
]
gives equivalent conditions (related respectively to the poset and to a weaker Fraïssé condition on the sequence of interest called sub-Fraïssé) to ensure that the spectrum of a sequence in
\mathbf{P}
is hereditarily indecomposable. We do not know whether the exact analog of
Theorem 5.15
holds in this setting, and specifically whether all sequences having the pseudoarc as a spectrum are lax-Fraïssé, i.e. whether all sub-Fraïssé sequences in
\mathbf{P}
are lax-Fraïssé.
